#3105. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, October 30: Noticeably Lower

TL;DR: Mortgage rates slid enough today to get home buyers' attention.

Mortgage rates have mostly recovered today after yesterday's jump scare.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.18% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and two basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

Mortgage rates were elevated this month. The average 30-year mortgage rate for all of October was 7.3%, compared to September's monthly average rate of 6.98%.

While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday. Happy Halloween!

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Traders expected that the Fed would vote to hold the federal funds rate steady on Wednesday, so that wasn’t much of a factor for mortgage lenders as they set rates.

In his post-meeting press conference, Fed Chair Kevin Warsh said that September’s rate increase is “starting to work some” toward moving inflation down to the Fed's 2% goal, but that central bankers don't have any firm conclusions yet. He also acknowledged that the Fed’s monetary policy actions hadn’t achieved price stability yet, but that they’re “working on it.”

This hints that if inflation data continues to trend upward, central bankers might raise the federal funds rate again at their December 8-9 meeting.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, dropped yesterday— a day too late for central bankers to consider it in their decision-making process.

The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on November 25.

Next week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build an even stronger case for the Fed to raise the federal funds rate again in December, because it would suggest that the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.68% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

» Stay informed: Check out NerdWallet's mortgage news hub for all our latest coverage.

The rate you see advertised is a sample rate — usually for a borrower with perfect credit, making a big down payment, and paying for mortgage points. That won't match every buyer's circumstances.

In addition to market factors outside of your control, your customized quote depends on your:

Even two people with similar credit scores might get different rates, depending on their overall financial profiles.

Maybe — but even personalized rate quotes can change until you lock. That’s because lenders adjust pricing multiple times a day in response to market changes.

#3104. Stock Market Today, Nov. 2: Marvell Announces $115 Billion All-Stock Acquisition of Lumentum

On Nov. 2, 2026, the data infrastructure chipmaker unveiled a major deal that sent its shares down 13% and sparked investor concerns over dilution and financing risk.

Marvell Technology (MRVL -12.72%), a data infrastructure chipmaker for AI data centers, closed at $170.80, down 13%. The blockbuster announcement that the company was acquiring Lumentum sent shares lower, and investors are watching financing and integration risks next.

Trading volume reached 60.5 million shares, coming in about 171% above its three-month average of 22.3 million shares. Marvell Technology IPO'd in 2000 and has grown 1,111% since going public.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.51%) closed at 7,542, down 0.50%, while the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -1.07%) finished at 25,164, down 1.07%. In the semiconductors for data infrastructure sector, Broadcom closed at $322.23, down 1.18%, while Lumentum finished at $684.40, down 18.59% after the deal headlines.

On Monday, Marvell Technology announced a $115 billion all-stock agreement to buy Lumentum, a manufacturer of critical optical communications components, network modules, and commercial lasers for efficient data transmission. The acquisition is intended to boost Marvell's data center offerings to capitalize on the artificial intelligence boom.

Last week, reports that Marvell was in advanced talks to buy Lumentum caused shares to slide as investors evaluated execution risk and capital allocation. Now that the deal has been announced, the market is weighing dilution, valuation, and financing risk.

Marvell CEO Matt Murphy explained the rationale for the acquisition, stating, "AI infrastructure is undergoing a fundamental architectural shift toward optical connectivity, creating an opportunity that comes along once in a generation."

Marvell was already on a path toward massive growth. During its Investor Day on Oct. 6, the company raised its revenue outlook for its 2028 fiscal year (FY), ended Jan. 31, 2028, to $20 billion, and set a FY2031 target of $70 billion to $90 billion in sales. Adding Lumentum could be a game changer, as tech giants spend billions of dollars to build data centers capable of rapid computational processing in support of increasingly complex AI systems.

Robert Izquierdo has positions in Broadcom and Marvell Technology. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Broadcom, Lumentum, and Marvell Technology. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3103. How to Make a $50 Holiday Gift Feel Like $100

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Trade time and a little extra thought to enhance gifts this holiday season.

Each month, NerdWallet suggests one wallet win to help you feel more confident about your money. This month’s topic is making holiday gifts feel more valuable, and we’re going to show you ways to elevate a gift without overspending.

Making an inexpensive gift feel fancy is a winter challenge I relish.

As a relatively frugal person who often gets sticker shock when shopping for gifts, I’m always looking for creative ways to save money without diminishing the present. Over the years, I’ve developed a few strategies to make a $50 gift feel like it’s worth at least double that.

In the spirit of the season, I’m sharing these strategies with you. I hope you — and your recipients — find them as celebratory as I do.

What does your gift recipient love to do? It helps if they have a favorite hobby, sports team or other interest.

Let’s say your recipient likes to fish. Giving a gift card to a niche fishing store makes it feel far more valuable than the standard Amazon card because it shows that you considered who they are. And there are specialty stores for every hobby these days.

Maybe gift cards aren’t your go-to. You can easily enhance other gifts, too.

Paying $5 extra to engrave or embroider their name on the gift — whether it’s stationery, jewelry or a backpack — makes the item feel far more valuable than its price tag because it’s customized to the individual.

If you choose a gift that couldn’t have been given to anyone else, it increases its value.

Time required: A few extra minutes to think about your recipients’ preferences and apply them when shopping.

A vintage or thrifted scarf can be a wrapping paper alternative that also functions as a gift. Similarly, you could wrap a gift in tea towels, artwork or online prints that can be reused by the recipient.

Another idea: Wrap a gift in printed sheet music that contains a special meaning for the recipient.

Customizing the presentation of the gift elevates it without adding much to the expense.

Time required: Since you have to wrap the gift anyway, this method doesn’t need to take extra time. You’ll just need to find the cloth or paper you plan to use.

One of my favorite gifts in recent memory involved turning a family photograph into a Jane Austen-era image set on a bucolic English estate with the empire-waisted gowns to match. My mom is a big fan of Jane Austen, so I knew she would appreciate seeing the family in a Regency-style photo.

I hired an Etsy artist to transform my original image, but you may be able to do it yourself with a photo app. Similarly, you could create a custom song, illustration or other form of art made specifically for your recipient.

Adding your own creative spin on a gift shows you spent extra time thinking about the recipient to make something extra special for them.

Time required: Applying your own creativity can be time-intensive, but the process is enjoyable so it goes quickly.

I like to call this technique the hybrid gift approach: You buy something, then get crafty to transform the gift into something one-of-a-kind.

One of my favorite ways to do this is with a photo. I’ll buy a nice but inexpensive frame and then put a photo of the recipient, or the recipient alongside loved ones, inside it.

If the person you’re buying for posts on social media, you might not even have to ask them for a photo in advance. Instead, you can find a favorite one that they already shared and surprise them.

The options are endless, especially with online tutorials to help hone your crafting skills. Adding your own customization to a purchased gift makes it feel special, but doesn’t have to cost much.

Time required: Baking, crafting or creating can take several hours, but again, it’s an enjoyable process.

Experiential gifts tend to go over well, and you may even get to have some fun, too.

One year, I gave my mom a membership to a nearby art museum and tacked on an offer to attend an exhibit with her. We had something on the calendar to look forward to together, and she got the option to visit more times throughout the year.

My parents have given me memberships, too. More recently, they gave me one to a local nature center. It’s great because I can visit on my own or attend kid-friendly events at a discounted rate. It makes me feel part of the community, and I love supporting an organization that helps kids learn more about nature.

Another option is to donate to charity in a loved one’s name and then make plans to volunteer at the organization together.

Experiential gifts help create more family memories together, which is one of my favorite ways to celebrate the holidays.

Time required: Setting aside time to visit a museum or other destination can take an entire day, but it’s time well spent.

The bottom line is that giving a highly personalized gift adds value without eating too far into your own budget. Spend $50. Make someone feel like a hundred bucks.

If you’re following along with us on this monthly financial challenge, start by testing out the tips we shared today over the next few weeks. We’ll be back again next month to learn about setting money goals for the new year. And if you missed last month’s edition, take a look back at how to shop the holiday sales.

#3102. Tactile Medical's Spending Outpaces Sales: Rapid Earnings Reaction

A product changeover and heavier spending weighed on Tactile Medical's third quarter, even as its main lymphedema franchise kept growing.

Tactile Systems Technology (TCMD -0.74%) grew third-quarter revenue 7% to $91.9 million, but net income fell to $7.2 million from $8.2 million. Shares dropped about 8% in after-hours trading. Its core lymphedema business is still growing at a double-digit pace. The problem is that spending is growing just as fast, so those sales gains aren't reaching profit.

The airway clearance slump looks like a timing issue. Tactile launched a new version of its AffloVest vest in the second quarter, and the release blames the drop on medical equipment suppliers working through inventory during the switch. When dealers run down old stock before ordering a new model, sales dip even if patient demand holds. The release doesn't say when orders will normalize.

Spending is the bigger issue. Gross margin improved on lower manufacturing costs and better product mix, yet operating income fell to $9.6 million from $11.0 million. Sales and marketing, plus the reimbursement and administrative line, both grew faster than revenue. Tactile is hiring and spending ahead of growth, which only pays off if sales speed up.

Management held its 2026 revenue outlook and nudged up its adjusted EBITDA forecast. But the new range, after nine months of results, implies fourth-quarter adjusted EBITDA about 2% to 10% below last year's. The raise reflects a strong first half, not a better finish.

Watch whether airway clearance revenue rebounds in the fourth quarter as suppliers stock the new AffloVest. Then check whether expense growth slows to match sales.

David Kretzmann has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Tactile Systems Technology. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3101. UiPath vs. SentinelOne: Which Technology Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

One profitable automation leader trades at a fraction of its high-growth cybersecurity rival, but growth comes with a steep price tag.

As artificial intelligence reshapes the enterprise landscape, investors are weighing the merits of UiPath (PATH -2.84%) and SentinelOne (S -7.91%) to determine which company offers a more compelling path toward 2027.

UiPath focuses on business orchestration through automation, while SentinelOne secures digital environments with an AI-driven platform. Both companies occupy critical niches within the enterprise software market, yet they offer distinct financial profiles. This comparison explores their different approaches to growth, profitability, and market valuation.

UiPath provides a business orchestration and automation platform that coordinates agents, robots, and people. It helps organizations streamline complex workflows across various industries, maintaining a strong presence among tech stocks. The company maintains a robust distribution model through strategic alliances with partners like Snowflake (SNOW -5.03%) and BDO USA.

In its latest annual report for FY 2026, revenue reached nearly $1.6 billion, representing growth of approximately 12.7% over the prior year. The company reported net income of roughly $282.3 million and a net margin of close to 17.5%. This performance marks a significant improvement from the previous fiscal year when the company reported a net loss.

As of its January 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was 0.0x, and the current ratio of 2.5x indicates the company can cover short-term liabilities with short-term assets. Free cash flow reached nearly $352.2 million, which represents cash generated from operations minus capital expenditures. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 78.3% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

SentinelOne provides an AI-powered cybersecurity platform that detects and responds to attacks across endpoints, cloud workloads, and identities. Its platform is used globally by enterprises and government agencies, relying on channel partners like N-able (NABL -2.32%) and Lenovo to reach customers. The company continues to expand its reach with over 1,715 customers generating at least $100,000 in annual recurring revenue.

In its latest annual report for FY 2026, revenue reached nearly $1.0 billion, an increase of roughly 21.9% compared to the previous year. Despite this growth, the company reported a net loss of approximately $450.7 million and a net margin of roughly -45%. The widening net loss highlights the costs associated with scaling its infrastructure and investing in new AI initiatives.

On its January 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was 0.0x, while the current ratio of 1.4x measures the company's ability to meet short-term obligations with current assets. Free cash flow reached nearly $75.9 million, calculated as cash from operations minus capital expenditures. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 388.4% of operating cash flow, meaning reported cash generation is heavily inflated by this non-cash add-back.

UiPath faces significant risks related to its heavy reliance on a single automation platform and intense competition from larger enterprise software vendors. The company also navigates potential regulatory challenges regarding AI ethics and a concentration where its top 10% of customers provide a substantial portion of revenue. Geopolitical factors also play a role, as international operations accounted for close to 54% of its revenue in FY 2026.

SentinelOne operates in a highly competitive cybersecurity market and has a history of net losses that may make forecasting future results difficult. The company relies on the efficacy of its AI initiatives and faces risks related to potential security breaches, including those affecting its cloud provider, Amazon (AMZN +2.20%). Furthermore, evolving data protection laws and the EU AI Act impose significant regulatory compliance obligations on its global operations.

UiPath appears significantly cheaper than SentinelOne across both earnings and sales multiples, suggesting a more conservative valuation for the established automation leader.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I'd go with SentinelOne. What I like is that it is growing much faster than UiPath while becoming more profitable, and its products cover both AI-powered security and protection for AI systems themselves. In Q2 fiscal 2027, SentinelOne's revenue beat its own guidance and management raised its full-year outlook. Management expects revenue to keep climbing when it reports in early December.

To give UiPath its due, it has been GAAP profitable for four straight quarters, a milestone SentinelOne has not reached. The catch is that UiPath's annual recurring revenue is growing at roughly half the pace of SentinelOne's, so it is entering 2027 with less momentum.

For anyone building a portfolio with a long time horizon, owning a security platform that grows alongside AI adoption is a straightforward way to benefit as more companies put AI to work. Each new AI system a customer deploys is one more thing that needs protecting.

Sara Appino has positions in Amazon. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon, Snowflake, and UiPath. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3100. I Don’t Buy With a Little Help From My Friends

To save money on holiday gifts this year, Americans are using these 5 strategies that require coordination with friends and family.

Gift giving can bust a budget, so some Americans plan to have conversations with friends and family this year about setting limits around holiday gifting.

This isn’t as Scroogey as you might think: 55% of holiday spenders plan to use at least one money-saving strategy that likely involves coordinating with loved ones, according to a new NerdWallet survey conducted online by The Harris Poll.

Money can be a taboo subject, especially among family and friends. But a focused discussion on gift boundaries could be the difference between staying within your budget and piling up debt this holiday season.

What other Americans are doing: 28% of holiday spenders plan to set a per-gift dollar limit with family or friends.

Why this works: It’s simple and easy to remember. Unlike some other ideas on this list, you can probably coordinate this one over text.

For best results: If you’re the one bringing up the topic, you should come with a dollar amount that would be reasonable for everyone in your gift exchange group, so no one feels excluded or overextended.

Your circle of friends and family has a lot of kids.

What other Americans are doing: 23% of holiday spenders plan to buy gifts for children in a family, but not the adults.

Why this works: Cutting out adult gifts altogether can rescue a gift budget that has undergone major “kidflation” as new children join the family.

For best results: If you still want to include adults, give a gift that’s fun for the whole family, like a board game or an experience. And make sure your kid-free loved ones are on board.

You traditionally exchange gifts one-on-one with a friend, but the last few gifts you’ve given — and received — were duds.

What other Americans are doing: 18% of holiday spenders plan to skip exchanging gifts with at least one friend or family member.

Why this works: If you’re feeling gift fatigue, there’s a chance they are, too.

For best results: Frame this as an improvement to the gift-giving tradition rather than a scaling back. Propose an alternative way to celebrate your friendship, like swapping out the $40 gift that’ll probably get shelved for a $7 coffee together.

What other Americans are doing: 16% of holiday spenders plan to do a secret Santa gift exchange, or another game that requires purchasing only one gift.

Why this works: 15% of holiday spenders list buying gifts for more people than they originally planned for as among the reasons they’re most likely to overspend this holiday season. This strategy keeps your list in check, even if the number of people playing grows.

For best results: There are bound to be people in your group for whom the rules to the game are important. If you’re the one organizing, be ready to answer questions — can you trade or steal gifts, for example, and, if so, how many times? Also, this works best for family or friend groups who aren’t likely to go rogue and buy extra gifts anyway.

You have no idea what your college friend’s kids (or spouse) are into.

What other Americans are doing: 12% of holiday spenders plan to give one shared gift per family instead of individual gifts.

Why this works: In addition to saving money, this avoids the awkwardness that comes with uninformed gift giving.

For best results: Presentation is key. Don’t sheepishly give an UNO game you clearly bought on the drive over. You may be cutting back, but enthusiasm is free.

Talking about the holidays and talking about money can be stressful conversation topics. About four in five Americans avoid discussing some money topics with family and friends, according to a 2026 survey by the CFP Board. So discussing both at once may seem daunting. But without a doubt, you’re not the only one thinking about them.

Nearly 9 in 10 (86%) holiday spenders plan to use at least one money-saving strategy this holiday season. Start with an icebreaker: “Hey, have you noticed how expensive everything is lately? Would you be open to talking about how we can do gifts this year while keeping spending under control?” This conversation might be the thing that’s secretly on everyone’s wish list.

#3099. BlackRock's Rick Rieder sees 'historic' opportunity in high-quality bonds

Investors may be wary of the volatility hitting parts of the bond market right now, but they have an "historic" opportunity to grab attractive yields in high-quality credit, Rick Rieder, BlackRock's chief investment officer for global fixed income, told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Hypothetically, it's now possible for investors to build a diversified portfolio that yields more than 7% with less than three years of duration and an average credit quality of A-, he said. Duration is a measurement of a bond's price sensitivity to fluctuations in rates, and issues with shorter maturities tend to have lower duration.

"Not only is it historic in that these yields are where they are, but the only time these yields are ever here is when there's some problem, like an economic problem or default problem," Rieder said in an interview about his fourth-quarter outlook, released Wednesday.

That isn't the case now, where the economy remains strong, he said. "So you don't really have to worry about defaults of any significance."

While Rieder didn't specifically identify the credits in the aforementioned portfolio, the iShares Aaa - A Rated Corporate Bond ETF (QLTA) has a 30-day SEC yield of 5.68% and an effective duration of 6.48 years — higher than Rieder's low duration focus.

The iShares Flexible Income Active ETF (BINC), which is managed by Rieder, has a 5.66% 30-day SEC yield and three-year effective duration, but its portfolio extends beyond investment-grade credit.

Rieder sees the best opportunity right now in investment-grade corporate bonds — which he pointed out he hasn't said "in a really long time." Investment grade bonds are rated AAA through BBB- by S&P and Fitch, while Moody's rates them Aaa through Baa3.

That includes hyperscaler bonds, which have flooded the market with issuance this year as they ramp up their AI infrastructure. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms and Oracle have issued a total of about $228.4 billion in bonds year to date, as of Oct. 20, according to LSEG.

"They're very low leverage. They have tremendous amounts of cash flow coming in," Rieder said of the hyperscalers in general. "You're now getting to buy these entities which are higher quality, paying you a very attractive yield to get it."

Rieder's iShares Flexible Income Active ETF has upped its allocation to investment-grade corporates to 14.3% of its portfolio. In July, that stood at 8.5%.

That said, investors need to be patient and sit tight since rates could still move higher from here, he advised.

"Your starting point is by far your best barometer of what your return is going to be," he noted. "So if you get seven and a quarter, maybe you get eight, maybe you get six, but ... if your holding period is a year or longer, you're going to get that yield."

Caution should also be exercised outside of investment-grade credit, which requires more selectivity and deep research, he warned. That includes AI names.

"There's parts of the data center supply that's not hyperscaler guaranteed or hyperscaler backed," he said. "That we've been more cautious around, and we'll continue to be."

The bond market, particularly high-yield credit, has been rocked as investor concern grows over the massive amount of debt some companies are taking out to fund their artificial intelligence build-out.

Junk-rated CoreWeave has become the poster child, seeing its stock drop, bond yields jump and credit-default spreads widen. Oracle, which is sitting in the lower end of investment grade and is seen as a bellwether on the broader AI market, has also taken some hits.

Rieder is also keeping an eye on the Federal Reserve, which is expected to raise its federal funds rate again in December to combat inflation. The market is currently pricing in a nearly 90% probability that the central bank will hike at that time, according to the CME Group FedWatch tool.

The money manager expects that to happen, although he does not agree with it. Higher rates are hurting parts of the economy, like housing, while not having much of an impact on other areas — such as tech companies' capital expenditures, which have become a big driver of growth, he said.

"They're not borrowing off the overnight funds rate," Rieder noted. "There's a lot of floating rate finance in housing, credit card, auto loan finance, and you're seeing those areas already under pressure."

Instead, the market is and will continue to price the AI supply coming into the market, he added.

Rieder sees a number of possible risks ahead, including rising levels of government debt across the globe. In the United States, the budget deficit has grown to $2 trillion for fiscal 2026.

"The more the central banks raise the rates, the cost of debt to these to already indebted governments is something we've got to be careful about over time," he said.

Bond supply coming from AI companies should also be watched.

"With the amount of supply that's coming — data center, hyperscaler — there's some crowding-out effect that's taking place," he said. "With government rates moving higher, that is something that we just got to keep an eye on."

In the meantime, Rieder would just continue to reap the rewards of high investment-grade yields "and smile a lot," he said.

#3098. Tesla Has a Flying Car. What Does This Mean for Archer's Flying "Taxi" Business?

If the era of flying cars is approaching, what will happen to Archer's business model?

A few weeks ago, a Tesla (TSLA +1.00%) Roadster drove up a nearly vertical ramp, turned upside down, then fell -- I mean, launched itself -- into the air before its side thrusters kicked in, flipping the car upright and landing it on the road below. It was by far the most incredible maneuver I've ever seen a car pull off. The Tesla then drove out of a cloud of vapor as if nothing had happened.

If you haven't seen Tesla's new flying car, it's worth watching the video (in another stunt, the car launches itself upside down and literally barrel-rolls over five vehicles before landing upright). It's an extraordinary demonstration of engineering, and one of those rare glimpses of a future that, until recently, seemed firmly in the realm of science fiction.

But now, what does this mean for Archer Aviation (ACHR -0.71%)? After all, Archer has also spent years developing its own flying vehicle -- an electric air taxi. If Tesla can make a flying car, will there still be a market for Archer's flying taxi, assuming it can get it certified? Let's take a look.

I'll just come out and say it: I don't think Tesla's flying Roadster will have an impact on Archer.

For starters, Tesla isn't really competing with Archer. Look at what Tesla is making: A sports car -- a really sleek and aesthetically striking sports car -- that can burst briefly into flight. In contrast, Archer is building an eVTOL (electric vertical takeoff and landing) vehicle that can take off and land vertically like a helicopter and transition into forward flight like a traditional airplane. It's also an engineering feat, and unlike Tesla's Roadster, it doesn't need thrusters to launch into the air.

These are obviously different vehicles, built for different purposes. And between the two, Archer's is more practical. It's designed to carry four passengers and a pilot. The Roadster, on the other hand, can only carry one person.

Archer's aircraft is also likely closer to real-world use than Tesla's. The flying Roadster might be functional, but it's not approved for road use. There's some question as to whether Tesla could ever get regulatory approval to mass-produce a flying Roadster, but even if it did, it would likely not beat Archer to the punch. Archer has already completed three of the FAA's four type-certification phases and is now working through the final one.

Don't get me wrong, though: Tesla's recent demonstration was very impressive. But it was only impressive as a spectacle, not something that could solve transportation problems, like congested metropolitan roads. Archer's eVTOL, on the other hand, could alleviate traffic in some areas.

In sum, I don't believe Tesla's flying car changes much for Archer Aviation's future. If anything, it could even increase publicity around flying cars, helping Archer's air taxi services when it does get its eVTOL certified.

Steven Porrello has positions in Archer Aviation. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Tesla. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3096. Democratic Rep. Stevens endorses Republican Rogers over rival El-Sayed in Michigan Senate election

Michigan Democratic Rep. Haley Stevens, in a stunning move, endorsed Republican Senate nominee Mike Rogers over Democratic nominee Abdul El-Sayed on Sunday.

Stevens lost the Democratic primary to El-Sayed earlier this year, but since then had backed his candidacy.

Her endorsement of Rogers came after days of rising tension with El-Sayed and his campaign over an inflammatory ad by a political action committee, Rebellion PAC, which depicted a couple debating whether they could afford groceries due to U.S. support for Israel.

El-Sayed's campaign has said it was not involved or affiliated with the ad.

The stunning endorsement was announced two days before Election Day, when voters will decide which party will control Congress for President Donald Trump's final two years in the White House. The outcome of the Michigan Senate race could determine which party holds a majority in the Senate, where Republicans currently hold a slim majority.

"Hey Michigan, as you may have seen, our Democratic nominee for U.S. Senate, Abdul, said I'm no longer welcome in his campaign, and I should stay away, and he's right," Stevens said in a video statement on X.

"And that's why I am endorsing Mike Rogers for the United States Senate today," she said.

"I don't belong in Abdul's campaign. I do not stand with Hasan Piker, who gets trafficked on our college campuses," said Stevens.

Piker is a controversial political commentator who has campaigned with El-Sayed.

El-Sayed, standing with Rep. Jim Clyburn, D-S.C., and Sen. Angela Alsobrooks, D-Md., outside a church on Sunday, dismissed Stevens endorsement of his opponent.

"I actually don't think about Haley Stevens," El-Sayed said. "There are families that are preparing to send their kids to schools tomorrow that look like they did 50 years ago. They're worried about what they're going to feed their kids for lunch. They're worried that if one of their kids gets sick, they're not going to be able to pay the bills. Those are the things that matter."

Rogers and El-Sayed are vying for a seat set to become vacant after Democratic Sen. Gary Peters decided not to seek re-election.

In recent weeks, a deepening rift has worsened between El-Sayed and Stevens. At the center of it is U.S. support for Israel, which was a major issue in the primary contest.

Stevens is a staunch supporter of Israel; El-Sayed has called the country's conduct in Gaza after the Oct. 7, 2023, terror attacks by the radical group Hamas a genocide.

Stevens' fiancé, Jason Turkish, endorsed Rogers before Stevens' flip-flop, citing what he said was rising antisemitism.

"I'm sorry people are upset," Stevens told reporters at a press conference in Detroit, standing with Rogers, whom she had attended church with Sunday morning.

Staffers in Stevens congressional office quit following her endorsement.

"As of this morning, many members of our team have resigned, including myself," Justin German, Stevens' chief of staff for eight years, said in a statement obtained by MS NOW.

"We are shocked and heartbroken by Congresswoman Stevens' decision to support Mike Rogers," German said, adding that he was speaking on behalf of the team "in wholeheartedly supporting Abdul El-Sayed for Senate," German said.

Stevens had the endorsement of much of the Democratic establishment during the bruising primary, making her endorsement of Rogers even more stunning.

She was the preferred candidate of Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer of New York, who on Sunday reiterated his support for El-Sayed and accused Republicans of Islamophobia.

"Senate Democrats are united in our support for Abdul El-Sayed, because we know he will fight for the economic interests of working families in Michigan, unlike Mike Rogers, who is a tool of Donald Trump and billionaire donors," Schumer said. "We Democrats [are] equally united in our total revulsion at the racist, Islamophobic, and over-the-top false attacks on Abdul by Republicans."

In a later post to X, Schumer called her endorsement of Rogers "a despicable betrayal from Rep. Stevens."

Peters and Michigan Democratic Sen. Elissa Slotkin both disavowed Stevens for her endorsement.

"[A]nyone who makes an election this important about themselves, and not Michiganders and our democracy, should think hard about whether they are serving the greater good," Slotkin said in a post to X.

Curtis Hertel, the chair of the Michigan Democratic Party, appeared to suggest that Stevens had been kicked out of the party.

"Congresswoman Stevens is not a current member of the Michigan Democratic Party, nor will she be in the future," he said in an X post.

In a Truth Social post, Trump praised Stevens' endorsement.

"Congresswoman Haley Stevens, a Highly Respected Democrat, who narrowly lost in the Primary to Abdul Mohamed El-Sayed, and who has been breathlessly wined and dined by the Dumocrats to help in their Michigan Campaign for the United States Senate against Mike Rogers, has just ENDORSED Mike Rogers for Senate!" the president said.

"Her following is huge, and the level of respect from the People of Michigan, especially our African American and Jewish populations, is virtually unparalleled," he said.

#3095. Public Service Enterprise: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Grid upgrades won't require new stock issuance, while forward nuclear hedges lock in revenue despite regional pricing gaps.

On its third quarter of 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 2, 2026, Public Service Enterprise (PEG +0.12%) announced full-year profit guidance and reaffirmed long-term targets driven by power grid upgrades. The company offers predictable earnings by expanding its regulated electric and gas network while securing forward power sales for its nuclear fleet. Three things management said stand out.

Regulated capital investments in energy efficiency and power distribution drive rate base growth, which is the baseline asset value used to calculate allowed customer rates. Management confirmed it doesn't need to sell new stock or dilute existing shareholders to fund these plans.

Power generation pricing varies across regional power market delivery hubs. Selling nuclear output forward at local delivery points shields revenue from pricing gaps between adjacent transmission zones.

Public Service Enterprise faces regulatory lag, which is the delay between spending capital on grid improvements and recovering those costs through customer rate adjustments. Management plans to file a targeted proposal with state regulators to shorten this recovery timeline on recent upgrades.

Execution continues smoothly across regulated transmission lines and nuclear operations, providing steady cash flows for capital spending. System modernization and grid reliability remain key value drivers, while management watches pricing discipline and regional power auction delays.

Constructive regulatory filings and potential long-term nuclear supply contracts with large power users create incremental upside beyond baseline projections.

#3093. Aehr Revenue Triples as AI Orders Pile Up: Rapid Earnings Reaction

A record quarter, a bigger backlog, and a higher target. Here's what drove Aehr's swing to profit and what still has to go right.

Aehr Test Systems (AEHR -6.23%) posted record fiscal first-quarter revenue of $33.0 million, up 200% from a year ago, and shares rose about 17% in after-hours trading. The company makes burn-in systems that stress chips with heat and power to catch early failures. Orders still outran sales, and backlog now covers nearly all the revenue needed to hit the new full-year target.

Sales jumped while operating costs stayed nearly flat from last quarter. Most of the extra revenue fell to profit, turning last quarter's operating loss into $6.0 million of operating income.

Real orders back the outlook. Aehr needs about $117 million over the next three quarters to reach its target, and nearly all of that is already on order, though the release doesn't say how much ships this fiscal year. The new target is also just the top of the old range.

Profit hasn't turned into cash yet. Cash fell about $11 million since the end of May, and the cash flow statement shows cash used in operations, which is common when a ramp ties up money in inventory and receivables.

Watch the test system a major AI chip supplier plans to install in Taiwan in early 2027, since a production order there could be one of Aehr's largest deals. Also check whether operating cash flow turns positive.

#3092. Mortgage Rates Today, Monday, November 2: Noticeably Lower

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates kicked off the week with a drop, but they’re still above 7%.

Mortgage rates are cooling off as we roll into November. However, they’re still above the 7% threshold.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.07% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 11 basis points lower than yesterday and 13 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

This week’s employment data could potentially push mortgage rates one way or the other — more on that below the graph.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out on Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.57% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#3091. Marvell Technology Is Acquiring Lumentum. Why Does the Market Hate the Move?

Shares of Marvell were down big on Monday after it announced it would be acquiring Lumentum.

Marvell Technology (MRVL -12.72%) just announced a huge move. It's acquiring Lumentum Holdings (LITE -18.59%) in a deal that values the tech company at a whopping $115 billion, based on implied enterprise value. It's a move that will expand the company's growth opportunities in artificial intelligence (AI) and bolster its total addressable market to $550 billion.

But the tech stock is down sharply on the news, hitting levels it hasn't seen in several months. Why does the market not appear to like this move, and could buying Marvell's stock on the dip be a good idea right now?

Marvell announced on Monday that it would acquire Lumentum in an all-stock transaction valued at $115 billion. Management sees this as a way to create a "category leader in AI connectivity," with Lumentum making optical products essential to the AI infrastructure build-out. By combining chips with optical connections, Marvell CEO Matt Murphy plans to build solutions “that are highly sticky."

The market, however, may not be thrilled with the massive price tag for Lumentum, nor with the all-stock offering, which will be dilutive to existing shareholders. It comes down to whether the net effect will prove to be a positive one for Marvell and lead to more earnings growth as a result of this combination, but based on the stock's performance, the market doesn't appear convinced of the payoff.

It also comes at a time when tech stocks have come under pressure of late. Marvell's pursuit of a significant purchase such as this may simply have come at a bad time. However, it's not unusual for shares of an acquiring company to fall in value when news of an acquisition comes out, as investors often question the value they are getting back in the deal, and whether it will pay off in the end.

Marvell is making a big bet on the future demand for AI infrastructure, and the market may be unsure about that right now. With interest rates on the rise again, tech spending may slow in the near future.

Meanwhile, with Marvell's stock trading at around 60 times its trailing earnings, it was already a high-priced investment before this latest announcement. For investors who may have been thinking twice about the AI stock, Marvell may have given those on the fence a stronger reason to sell rather than buy, and this may just be the beginning of a bigger decline to come.

David Jagielski, CPA has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Lumentum and Marvell Technology. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3090. Why Wingstop Stock Keeps Going Up

This global growth story is still in its early chapters.

Shares of Wingstop (WING +8.89%) climbed nearly 9% on Monday, furthering their post-earnings announcement ascent.

The restaurant chain reported its third-quarter financial results on Oct. 28.

Revenue rose 5.3% year over year to $185 million, driven by new store openings. Wingstop launched 76 net new stores, bringing its total count to 3,331 locations as of Sept. 26. That includes 2,787 restaurants in the U.S. and 544 franchised locations in international markets.

Yet sales at Wingstop's existing restaurants fell. Domestic same-store sales, which measure revenue generated from locations open for at least a year, declined 5.4%. The company also lowered its full-year same-store sales forecast to a decline of 6.5% to 7%.

Wingstop, like many restaurant chains, is struggling to drive traffic to its stores. With gas prices and other costs rising, many consumers are pulling back on dining out.

Still, Wingstop's adjusted net income came in at $29.3 million, or $1.08 per share. That topped Wall Street's estimates, which had called for per-share profits of $1.02.

CEO Michael Skipworth said the chain's new Club Wingstop loyalty program is helping to entice guests to return to its restaurants more often. Skipworth believes this program, combined with operational investments designed to increase service speed and order accuracy, will help reinvigorate the company's sales growth.

Wingstop, in turn, plans to open as many as 450 net stores in 2026.

Looking further ahead, management sees a far larger opportunity. "Our development pipeline remains robust with over 2,300 committed restaurants globally," Skipworth said during a conference call with analysts.

Skipworth is confident that Wingstop can ultimately grow its global store count to more than 10,000 restaurants, including 1,000 stores in India alone.

Joe Tenebruso has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Wingstop. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3087. Micron Technology: Is This a Sign That Its Stock Has Already Peaked?

The memory stock was scorching hot early this year, but now, it's down about 34% from its 52-week high.

Earlier in the year, it looked as though Micron Technology (MU -5.78%) stock was unstoppable. It was soaring as demand for memory was through the roof, and the company was able to hike prices, benefiting from stronger margins and sky-high demand. Its numbers have been unreal, making it a must-buy for many growth investors.

But in recent months, that hasn't been the case. Even though the tech stock is trading at a low earnings multiple, investors haven't been taking the bait. And there could be another indication that the tech stock may have peaked: trading volumes are down significantly.

When a stock is a hot buy with retail investors, trading volumes are through the roof, and that can send it to astronomical heights. Micron hit a milestone earlier this year, crossing $1 trillion in market cap. But it's now below that threshold, trading closer to a market cap of about $940 billion. It's still massive, but it's down significantly from the highs it reached during the summer.

A big telltale sign that the stock may have already peaked is its 30-day average trading volume. It's down significantly. The chart below shows that as it hit its high around late June, volumes were also up considerably.

MU 30-Day Average Daily Volume data by YCharts

Theoretically, news that the memory shortage will last longer than expected could draw interest, potentially give the stock a bigger boost, and attract more investors. However, there have been multiple reports of the shortage lasting for years, and many investors aren't willing to shake concerns that more supply could come online in the near future, earlier than expected. Or perhaps they simply want to cash out before it's too late, especially with the stock sitting on massive gains -- more than 1,000% in five years.

The risk with volatile and historically cyclical stocks like Micron is that, while they can be hot buys when things are going well and there are shortages, they can quickly become the stocks no one wants to own when the bubble has burst and more supply comes online.

While Micron's earnings multiple of 11 may look enticingly low, it's skewed based on recent results which have been incredibly strong. Odds are, those numbers won't be sustainable over the longer term, and when prices inevitably come down, the stock price could crash even quicker.

With so many growth stocks trading lower these days, there are plenty of good options to pursue that don't come with nearly as much uncertainty and risk as Micron, which is why I wouldn't bother with it.

David Jagielski, CPA has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Micron Technology. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3084. Why Franklin Electric Stock Raced 5% Higher on Monday

An analyst tracking the company upgraded his recommendation on the shares.

Pump and motor manufacturer Franklin Electric (FELE +5.16%) was pumping up the value of stock portfolios on Monday. Investors eagerly bought the stock on news that an analyst tracking it had upgraded his recommendation. By the time the smoke had cleared, Franklin's shares were up in excess of 5% that trading day.

The upgrading party was DA Davidson's Matt Summerville, who now feels Franklin is a buy, whereas he previously flagged it as neutral. In making the change, Summerville also increased his price target to $135 per share from $105.

I think he's correct in identifying the somewhat under-the-radar, niche industrial company as a beneficiary in hot verticals these days; however, the core markets that make up the bulk of its business are vulnerable to rising interest rates and construction-industry downcycles. Still, the emergence of the "newer" businesses presents an enticing opportunity for Franklin.

#3082. When AI Does The Deciding, Who Gets The Loyalty?

Raj De Datta is CEO of Bloomreach, a platform that personalizes the e-commerce experience, and the author of “The Digital Seeker.”

​As AI increasingly becomes the intermediary between consumers and brands, more and more consumers are crediting AI for their experiences. Consumers are, therefore, becoming more loyal to AI systems, not brands.

Business leaders don’t need to be told that this is a pretty big deal.

But it shouldn’t come as a surprise, either. Consider what we’re seeing. ChatGPT crossed one billion monthly active users in May 2026, and Capital One reports 60% of consumers are using AI to shop, with AI-driven traffic to retail sites up 393% year over year.

It’s not just e-commerce: In travel, a recent Qlik survey found 58% of Americans are now using AI tools to plan summer vacations, compared to 55% using traditional travel sites, marking the first time AI has overtaken the old way.

But adoption is only part of the story. We’re now seeing the implications of that adoption, too. According to a survey of over 4,000 consumers across the U.S. and U.K. conducted by my company, 43% of consumers say they feel more loyal to the AI tool that found them the right product than to the actual brand that made it. That data should give every brand a reason to pause.

Loyalty is going to AI, not the brand behind the purchase. And yes, this might look like an existential threat to brands everywhere. But it doesn’t have to be.

This has actually played out before, whether its department stores a century ago or, more recently, with Amazon. You can even consider it from a technology lens, with the onset of e-commerce or the evolution of mobile. The lesson we’ve learned again and again is that brands need to evolve to survive. The game has changed, and there are two distinct problems brands now need to solve.

Brands need to ensure that AI systems represent them accurately and, crucially, do everything they can to stand out once customers show up.

Getting recommended by an AI system is no longer just a visibility problem. It’s an accuracy problem. A 2025 Columbia University Tow Center study tested AI search engines across 1,600 queries and found the majority of responses contained citation errors.

Brands need to ensure they’re being referenced and described accurately, which starts with ensuring their site and product data is in order. Highly-structured product catalogs, intent-driven metadata (“cocktail dress for a wedding” instead of just “mini dress”) and descriptions that reflect the brand all help the visibility and accuracy of what AI pulls.

Beyond that, they’ll need to be actively monitoring what models are saying about them, like how they’d monitor press coverage or social sentiment. If your site content, product pages and catalog data are thin, outdated or inconsistent, you’re invisible to AI and vulnerable to misrepresentation.

The bigger question is how you create memorable experiences once consumers choose your brand.

Consumers are more forthright with AI systems than they are with brand sites. For example, a customer may ask Claude to help plan a trip for her family and explain her kids’ ages, preferences, budget, dietary restrictions, pet peeves and whatever else they care most about. Most brands just don’t have that level of visibility.

AI systems are accumulating a richer, more current picture of each customer than any single brand will ever hold on its own. So, brands need to adjust their strategy, and that comes down to three things.

First, compete on depth, not breadth. An AI assistant may know a customer shops for swimsuits at five different brands. Only you know that this particular customer runs a size up, returns anything without UPF protection and bought a matching one for her daughter last summer. That’s relationship-specific memory, and it’s not something a general-purpose assistant is likely to have. It’s the same instinct that let independent, specialty retailers out-loyalize the department stores decades ago.

Second, give customers a reason to tell you directly. If someone is willing to tell an AI assistant their family’s dietary restrictions and travel budget, they’ll tell a brand, too. Brands can then provide better recommendations, faster service and a genuinely different experience next time. Every direct touchpoint or on-site conversation should function as an invitation to close that information gap. Brands need to integrate AI conversations and memory directly into their products and engagement programs to create an incredible experience.

Third, win on what data can’t buy. AI can synthesize information, but it can’t offer a discount or proactively communicate something new. That kind of relational trust is what built loyalty before AI, and it will matter even more moving forward. I want to know if my favorite pair of jeans is now available in a new color, or if the vacation package I was browsing has gone on sale. Brands must use the data they’ve collected to proactively help customers in their journey. This is where brands can win and build long-term trust.

Brands need to invest in personalization and start thinking about deeper ways to build loyalty. The consumer relationship isn’t disappearing into AI, but it is being rerouted through it. And the brands that remain laser-focused on delivering a better customer experience in this new world may just see new levels of loyalty as a result. ​​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3080. Biotech stocks are surging. How investors can play the new drug innovation boom

Moderna gave investors a scare as its stock plunged on Thursday. But it's still one of the hottest biotechs this year. And it's hardly alone – the sector is on a roll and looks poised for more gains.

The iShares Biotechnology ETF (IBB) is up 24% through October 23, building on a 28% gain in 2025. Two dozen of the ETF's 233 holdings have more than doubled year-to-date. Moderna leads the pack with a 587% surge — even after an 18% decline following a leak of clinical data for its cancer vaccine (which was actually positive).

Analysts say a new biotech cycle is underway, fueled by clinical trial results, M&A activity, and promising technologies such as genomics, mRNA — and AI.

"We're in the midst of a powerful innovation cycle," said John Hall, portfolio manager of the T. Rowe Price Biotechnology ETF. "What's super exciting is we're seeing newer modalities start to make it to market." Companies "understand the disease biology better," he adds, and that could ultimately lead to more novel drugs making it to market.

Investors are excited about technologies like mRNA and genomics, along with a steady stream of new drug approvals. The FDA approved about a dozen novel biologic drugs in 2025, out of 46 approvals in total. Roughly 66 novel drugs are in the pipeline for FDA approval, including potential blockbusters like Moderna and Merck's cancer drug for melanoma, and a next-generation obesity drug from Eli Lilly.

Analysts say AI could also lead to more approvals as companies deploy the software to speed up drug development.

"Biotech is a phenomenal application for artificial intelligence and machine learning," says Piper Sandler analyst Ted Tenthoff. "You can set up exciting predictive models that, frankly, can do things that we've never been able to do in the space in terms of novel drug discovery."

Granted, the industry is recovering from a fallow period. The sector's last strong performance was during the Covid era when ultra-low interest rates encouraged investment in early-stage drug developers, and mRNA vaccines fueled a revenue surge for companies like Moderna, Pfizer and Biontech.

The sector then went cold as many early-stage companies fizzled. Demand for Covid vaccines evaporated, and surging interest rates put a chill in IPOs and M&A.

The risks haven't evaporated – this is still a sector whose products take years to develop, resulting in far more failures than successes. But analysts say the outlook is healthier now. While that's reflected in strong performance and richer valuations, there's still good reason to invest.

Recent clinical trial results are stoking excitement that novel drugs in the works could tackle some of the toughest problems in medicine.

Moderna and Merck's melanoma drug, for instance, uses mRNA to trigger an immune response in patients, targeting tumors that have been surgically removed and genetically analyzed. It's not a preventive vaccine, but it uses a similar immunology mechanism and showed a 42% reduction in cancer recurrence or death (used in conjunction with Merck's cancer drug Keytruda).

While the Phase 3 results disappointed Wall Street, oncologists say the outcomes were still highly positive. And they see the technology as a potential game changer, ushering in an era of personalized treatments for cancers. Moderna and Merck have said they aim to submit the drug for FDA approval and get it launched in 2027.

Inflammation treatment is another promising area. AstraZeneca and Amgen recently released positive Phase 3 results for another use for their drug Tezspire, an injectable approved for nasal polyps and asthma. In clinical trials, it showed that it successfully relieved swallowing symptoms and healed microscopic and visual tissue damage in patients with severe throat inflammation.

Roche, meanwhile, notched a win after the FDA approved its drug Enspryng, a therapy to treat rare inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. It became the first and only at-home injection for adults with thyroid eye disease.

Obesity drugs are also advancing beyond the first generation of GLP-1s. Eli Lilly's retatrutide, a "triple agonist" targeting three different hormone receptors, cleared two more clinical trials over the summer. The drug produced 28%-30% weight loss at the highest doses in the study, Lilly said. The company plans to submit the drug for FDA approval in the first quarter of 2027.

Much of the innovation isn't just happening in drug-making – it's also in areas like gene sequencing, mRNA, and high-tech instruments.

Illumina has more than doubled this year. The DNA and gene sequencing company is seeing accelerating demand for its NovaSeq X high-end sequencing platform. Pharmaceutical companies including Bristol-Myers Squibb, Merck and Kura are working with Illumina to develop precision oncology solutions.

Another big winner is 10x Genomics, up more than 300%. The biotech firm offers its lab instruments to help scientists research cancer, immunology and neuroscience. The company's Atera platform is helping produce data sets to train AI models, a process that helps accelerate personalized therapies.

On the AI front, companies are using the technology for more efficient drug discovery, says Shreefāl Mehta, president of Guidant BioTherapeutics. Using AI, "you can now bring a drug to clinical trials with less capital. More investment is also going into companies that are demonstrating that," he said.

Global AI in drug discovery will grow from a $20 billion market in 2025 to more than $160 billion by 2035, according to forecasts by Towards Health, a research and consulting firm.

One other catalyst for the sector could be M&A. Dealmaking for biopharma hit $134 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $112 billion for all of 2025, notes Jeet Mukherjee, biotech analyst at BTIG. That's a positive signal, indicating more drugs in development are showing clinical promise.

It also helps that Big Pharma has a "patent cliff" ahead. Products representing $180 billion of revenue will lose exclusivity by 2030, according to research firm RTW Institute. Companies with looming expiries "are looking for novel products and innovative products to put into their pipeline," Mehta says.

GLP-1 blockbusters have also left some companies flush with cash for acquisitions. Eli Lilly alone has completed 10 acquisition deals in 2026 worth $25 billion.

ETFs can capture the investing theme, though they aren't all alike. The iShares Biotech ETF is one of the lower cost funds. It tracks a market-cap weighted index of biotech stocks, led by companies like Gilead Sciences, Amgen, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, and Moderna.

The State Street SPDR S&P Biotech ETF holds stocks in equal weight, offering more exposure to small- and mid-cap names. Its top holdings are Moderna, Natera, Twist Bioscience and Halozyme Therapeutics.

Piper Sandler calls Illumina and 10x Genomics the "primary infrastructure plays" in the life science arena, saying their technologies are "driving the next generation of precision medicine."

Piper analyst David Westenberg has a buy rating and $305 target on Illumina, implying 14% gains from recent prices around $267. He has a hold rating on 10X with an $80 target, above recent prices around $70.

Twist Bioscience is another name to consider. The company makes customized synthetic DNA and sells DNA sequencing tools. Twist has partnered with Eli Lilly's AI and machine-learning platform and linked up with Anthropic in August to start testing its AI model, Claude, for protein development.

Twist isn't expected to turn an EPS profit until 2029, according to consensus forecasts. But revenue is expected to go from $572 million in 2027 to $833 million in 2029. Analysts have an average target of $168 on the stock, implying 21% returns from recent prices around $138.

In the small-cap space, Piper Sandler's Tenthoff likes Monte Rosa Therapeutics, a pioneer in protein degradation that selectively destroys disease-causing proteins.

The firm has partnered with Novartis to develop a drug for inflammatory conditions and autoimmune diseases. The companies announced a Phase 2 clinical study in September for an autoimmune disease treatment, which has been "triggering milestones to Monte Rosa," Tenthoff said.

Tenthoff has a buy rating and $37 target on the stock, which recently traded around $10.70.

Also in the small-cap arena, BTIG's Mukherjee recommends Faeth Therapeutics.

The biotech is developing a novel two-drug regimen to treat breast cancer and other tumors. The regimen "has the potential to address safety and dosing limitations of another recently approved drug for breast cancer," he says. And it could become "a mainstay of the future breast cancer treatment paradigm and other cancers."

Faeth is a clinical-stage company that has yet to bring a drug to market. Yet the stock is up 93% year-to-date on positive clinical trial results, trading around $18. Mukherjee has a $55 target on it.

Kymera Therapeutics is worth considering too. The company is developing an oral version of the blockbuster drug Dupixent (made and co-developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals and Sanofi). Dupixent is an injectable, however, and oral agents "are frequently preferred by patients," Mukherjee says. "The drug has the potential to not only take share from patients taking Dupixent for numerous immunological diseases but also expanding the market to those not taking systemic biologic drugs," he tells CNBC.

Kymera has said it doesn't expect a patent fight over Dupixent because its drug is distinct, not a biosimilar or biologic copy.

T. Rowe's Hall also likes Kymera for its potential "to unlock disease targets that have been classically considered un-druggable." The stock is the second largest holding in T. Rowe's Biotech ETF, accounting for 4.2% of the portfolio.

#3078. Stock Market Today, Nov. 2: Rivian Rallies on UBS Upgrade and Solid R2 Demand Outlook

Today, Nov. 2, 2026, the EV maker surged after UBS called the selloff overdone, citing solid early R2 demand and a path to positive automotive gross margin.

Rivian Automotive (RIVN +8.47%), an electric trucks, SUVs, and delivery vans maker, closed at $13.19, up 8.47%. UBS upgraded the stock to Buy and called the recent sell-off a buying opportunity ahead of the R2 ramp. Investors are watching early R2 demand and margin progress next.

Trading volume reached 64.3 million shares, coming in about 165% above its three-month average of 24.3 million shares. Rivian Automotive IPO'd in 2021 and has fallen 87% since going public.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.51%) fell 0.50% to 7,542, while the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -1.07%) dropped 1.07% to 25,164. Among electric vehicle (EV) manufacturing, passenger EVs, and commercial delivery vans peers, Tesla (TSLA +1.00%) closed at $403.82, up 1.00%, and Ford Motor (F +2.86%) finished at $12.60, up 2.86%, as EV-capable automaker stocks moved higher on strong recent quarterly results.

UBS also said early R2 demand looks solid and expects Rivian to exit 2026 with a positive automotive gross margin percentage. That margin path is central to the stock's rerating.

The company's budget R2 vehicle became its best selling auto by volume in Q3, demonstrating consumer demand for the all-electric midsize SUV is strong. The report gave the stock a fresh fundamental backdrop before the UBS call.

Rivian also said its Volkswagen joint venture received a $1 billion term loan in October. That financing adds liquidity context as the company works through its R2 launch.

So far, Rivian looks like it's on a growth trajectory despite a volatile macroeconomic environment of rising interest rates, thanks to its R2 release. This is quite an achievement, especially after federal EV subsidies were ended in 2025. The UBS upgrade further validates Rivian's strategic bet on R2 is a win.

#3072. AMD Reports Tuesday. Here's the Number That Will Show Whether It's Gaining on Nvidia

Third-quarter revenue will probably come in close to expectations. The fourth-quarter guidance is what matters more.

Advanced Micro Devices (AMD -7.52%) is scheduled to report third-quarter results after the market closes on Tuesday, and the stock is heading in on a shaky note. Shares fell nearly 11% on Friday and sold off further on Monday amid broader chip stock weakness, with no AMD-specific news. In all, AMD is down nearly 30% from its all-time high, reached less than a month ago.

Of course, we have no idea what AMD will report, but the headline results probably won't be the surprise. AMD guided for revenue of about $13 billion, plus or minus $300 million, and analysts expect $13.09 billion, which would be 41.5% higher than a year ago.

The more important figure is likely to be AMD's fourth-quarter guidance, and specifically how much of it comes from data center GPUs. AMD began shipping its Helios rack-scale systems, built on its MI450 chips, in September, so the third quarter won't include a full period of its volume. However, management said on its last call that it expects the ramp to accelerate meaningfully in the fourth quarter.

The average estimate among Wall Street analysts is about $16.3 billion in fourth-quarter revenue, and Jefferies analyst Blayne Curtis expects closer to $17 billion. A forecast at or above that higher level would suggest Helios demand is turning into revenue at an impressive pace. AMD has plenty of big customers lined up, including OpenAI, Meta Platforms (META -1.34%), Microsoft (MSFT -2.83%), and Anthropic, so expectations are high.

To put AMD's AI business in perspective, Nvidia (NVDA -1.26%) generated $89 billion of data center revenue in its most recent quarter. AMD's entire data center segment, which includes its EPYC server processors, generated $6.7 billion in revenue. AMD isn't going to close that gap anytime soon.

However, it doesn't need to. AI companies want a second source of computing power so they aren't completely dependent on one supplier, and AMD has emerged as the most credible alternative. CEO Lisa Su has said each gigawatt of AI capacity represents double-digit billions in revenue, so even a modest share of the market would be an enormous win.

Of course, there are risks to watch. For example, AI chips carry slightly lower margins than server processors, so a fast GPU ramp could weigh on gross margin. It's also worth noting that Intel's (INTC -8.80%) warning about a shrinking PC market could also be a headwind for AMD's client business, which grew 23% last quarter.

In short, I'm watching the guidance more than the results. If the fourth-quarter outlook shows Helios ramping up on schedule, the recent pullback could be a buying opportunity for long-term investors in an excellent AI stock.

Matt Frankel, CFP® has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Advanced Micro Devices, Intel, Meta Platforms, Microsoft, and Nvidia. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

A plant study reveals that some mRNAs regulate other genes without making proteins, challenging the divide between messenger RNA and regulatory RNA.
