#3070. Oracle files to sell $20 billion in new stock, bonds to finance AI buildout

Oracle filed plans on Monday to issue new stock and more debt to finance its artificial intelligence buildout.

The hyperscaler will sell $10 billion in common stock and $10 billion in senior notes, according to filings with the Securities and Exchange Commission. While the fundraising was already outlined at an investor day last week, shares tumbled 8.8% Monday.

"This seems to be exactly what they previewed," said Rob Oliver, senior research analyst at Baird. "So I'm not sure why the market is freaking about it."

Oracle said it plans to use the proceeds for general corporate purposes, which may include capital expenditures, repayments of debt and future investments.

The hyperscaler's stock initially bounced after last Wednesday's investor day, when Oracle also issued a revenue forecast of at least $225 billion for fiscal 2030. At the time Oracle also said it was limiting its additional capital market funding for the fiscal 2027 to 2030 investment cycle to that $20 billion.

"This is a major relief given concerns about the state of debt markets and the cost of capital (although UBS has argued that these debt market concerns have been over-stated)," UBS analyst Karl Keirstead said in a note on Thursday.

Oracle plans to turn to vendor financing, customer-provided hardware and customer prepayments to help it transition to positive free cash flow, which it expects to reach in the second half of 2028.

Stock and bonds in Oracle, co-founded by former CEO Larry Ellison, have been under pressure amid growing investor concerns about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Oracle shares are down 33% in 2026 and 50% over the past 12 months. The yield on its investment-grade 4.95% bond due in 2031 is currently 7.276%.

The sentiment shift is also seen in Oracle's five-year credit default swaps, which are insurance against default. The spread sits at 292, up 265% in one year. That's more in line with a high-end junk bond rating, according to S&P Global Market Intelligence's Gavan Nolan.

While still an investment-grade credit, Oracle was downgraded by S&P to a BBB- in July, one rating level above high yield. It has $125.34 billion in debt, as of Aug. 31, with the bulk in senior notes.

What ultimately matters for Oracle is execution, Pat Nerney, head of investment solutions at Dynasty Financial Partners, said in an email to CNBC Monday.

"Can they do what they say they are going to do? We view this as the narrative across the AI space," he said. "If this gets executed according to plans laid out, they will be rewarded. If they have to come back to market in the back half of '27 after telling everyone this was the last of the capital needed ... look out."

— CNBC's Seema Mody and Ari Levy contributed reporting.

#3069. Stock Market Today, Nov. 2: Apple Stock Slides Ahead of Earnings, but Its Latest Results Tell a Different Story

Investors braced for fiscal Q4 results with focus on holiday guidance and margin scrutiny after Microsoft and Alphabet's recent reports, today, Nov. 2, 2026.

Apple (AAPL -1.83%), the technology giant behind the iPhone and its expanding digital services business, closed Monday at $338.32, down 1.83% amid a broader technology sell-off. Following the closing bell, Apple reported better-than-expected quarterly earnings, supported by strong iPhone demand, providing investors with a more encouraging outlook after a difficult trading session.

Trading volume reached 55.4 million shares, approximately 32% above its three-month average of 42.1 million, as investors anticipated the company's earnings announcement.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.51%) fell 0.50% to 7,542, and the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -1.07%) lost 1.07% to 25,164. Among consumer electronics, personal computers, smartphones and digital services peers, Microsoft (MSFT -2.83%) closed at $507.12, -2.83%, while Alphabet (GOOGL -0.30%) (GOOG -0.07%) closed at $278.99, -0.30%.

Apple's latest earnings offered investors some reassurance following a difficult session for technology stocks. The company reported fiscal fourth-quarter revenue of $114.5 billion, exceeding Wall Street expectations, while earnings of $2.10 per share also topped forecasts. Strong demand for its latest iPhone 18 helped drive the results, with iPhone revenue increasing approximately 17% year over year to $57.6 billion.

However, the report wasn't without concerns. Apple's gross margin declined to 47.9% from 50.1% in the previous quarter, reflecting rising component costs and a smaller benefit from tariff refunds. Sales in China and the iPad business also fell short of expectations, highlighting challenges beyond its core iPhone franchise.

For investors, the results reinforce Apple's ability to generate revenue growth even amid broader economic uncertainty. With technology valuations under scrutiny, the focus now shifts to whether Apple can sustain strong iPhone demand through the holiday season while protecting profitability.

#3063. Nike’s Fall, Netflix Growth, and What We Learned in Q3

A review of the lessons about the economy and markets through the first three quarters of 2026.

In this episode of Motley Fool Hidden Gems Investing, Motley Fool contributors Travis Hoium, Lou Whiteman, and Jason Hall discuss:

To catch full episodes of all The Motley Fool's free podcasts, check out our podcast center. When you're ready to invest, check out this top 10 list of stocks to buy.

Travis Hoium: Nike just didn't. Motley Fool Hidden Gems Investing starts now. Welcome to Motley Fool Hidden Gems. I’m Travis Hoium. I’m joined today by Lou Whiteman and Jason Hall. I do want to get to Nike’s numbers, guys, because they were not very impressive after the market closed yesterday, but we’ve also ended the calendar third quarter. #3059. Accenture vs. Microsoft: Which Technology Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Microsoft sells AI through high-margin cloud and software platforms but is spending heavily to expand capacity. Accenture earns its revenue helping companies adopt AI, with growth expected to be modest.

As businesses race to integrate artificial intelligence, Accenture (ACN +1.05%) and Microsoft (MSFT -2.83%) represent two different ways to play this trend. Choosing between them requires weighing service-based consulting against high-margin software platforms.

Accenture acts as a bridge, helping organizations implement complex new systems across various industries. Microsoft provides the underlying infrastructure, from cloud services to productivity software, that many of those same organizations rely on daily. Both companies occupy critical positions in the enterprise landscape, but their financial profiles and growth drivers differ significantly.

Accenture operates as a global professional services leader, assisting over 9,000 clients with their digital and operational transformations. The firm reported approximately 814,000 employees in its latest annual report and recently expanded its reach by acquiring Avicado Construction Technology Services LLC. This consulting focus is a major driver within the broader market for tech stocks, as companies require expert guidance to navigate modern software shifts.

In FY 2026, revenue reached nearly $74.2 billion, representing a growth rate of roughly 6.5% compared to the previous year. This resulted in a net income of approximately $8.4 billion. The company maintained a net margin of about 11.3% during this period, showing consistent performance across its global operations.

As of its August 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.4x, which represents total debt divided by shareholder equity. The current ratio of nearly 1.4x indicates the ability to cover short-term liabilities with short-term assets. Free cash flow reached roughly $11.6 billion, a figure calculated by subtracting capital expenditures from operating cash flow.

Microsoft provides essential technology solutions to individuals and businesses, operating through a massive network of direct sales and partners. In its latest annual report, filed for the fiscal year ended June 30, 2025, the company highlighted its long-term strategic partnership with OpenAI to enhance its AI capabilities. This strategy centers on the Microsoft Cloud, which includes Azure and Microsoft 365, to maintain a dominant position in enterprise software.

In FY 2026, revenue reached approximately $331.8 billion, a significant increase over the nearly $281.7 billion reported in FY 2025. This growth led to a net income of about $133.7 billion. With a net margin of roughly 40.3%, the business generates substantial profit relative to its total sales.

As of its June 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.3x. The current ratio of roughly 1.2x reflects its ability to meet short-term obligations using its current assets. Free cash flow reached nearly $67.0 billion, which represents the cash remaining after the company pays for its operating activities and capital investments.

Accenture faces significant risks from macroeconomic and geopolitical volatility which can lead to client spending reductions. The business model depends on keeping a competitive edge in rapidly changing AI technologies while managing cybersecurity threats involving sophisticated attacks. Additionally, the firm must navigate complex global regulatory requirements and successfully integrate strategic acquisitions to realize their expected benefits.

Accenture is cheaper by P/S ratio (market cap divided by sales over the past twelve months), while Microsoft has a higher Forward P/E based on future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I'd go with Microsoft. Every company that wants to use AI needs somewhere to run it, and Microsoft's cloud is often the No. 1 place they turn to. Many have already signed long-term contracts, which gives it a clear view of future revenue.

In fiscal Q1 2027, Microsoft's cloud revenue growth sped up to 30% and overall revenue topped the company's own guidance for the quarter. At the same time, building AI data centers is expensive, and it is weighing on free cash flow. Microsoft recently offered voluntary buyouts to about a quarter of its workforce to help manage those costs. The good news is that customers are already lining up to use the new capacity.

To give Accenture its due, companies around the world hire it to plan and carry out their AI projects, and its most recent quarter beat expectations. But it expects its sales to grow only modestly in fiscal 2027, a fraction of the pace of Microsoft's cloud business.

For someone focused on where a business is headed over the next decade, the winning business model is simple. Microsoft earns more every time a company runs AI on its cloud or uses Copilot, while Accenture earns fees for the work its people deliver. Microsoft's growth is tied directly to how much AI gets used, which is the position I'd want to own over a long time horizon.

Sara Appino has positions in Amazon. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Accenture Plc, Amazon, and Microsoft. The Motley Fool recommends the following options: long January 2028 $260 calls on Accenture Plc and short January 2028 $280 calls on Accenture Plc. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3055. Stock Market Today, Nov. 2: CoreWeave Slides on Debt Concerns After CrowdStrike Partnership Expands

On Nov. 2, 2026, premarket security partnership news faded as investors refocused on leverage and cash burn risks ahead of earnings.

CoreWeave (CRWV -8.60%), a specialized cloud computing provider for generative AI workloads, closed at $50.15, down 8.60%. Premarket partnership news with CrowdStrike faded as leverage concerns weighed on the market, and investors are watching Nov. 10 earnings.

Trading volume reached 47.1M shares, coming in nearly 21% above its three-month average of 38.9M shares. CoreWeave IPO'd in 2025 and has grown 25% since going public.

The S&P 500 (SNPINDEX: ^GSPC) dropped 0.50% to 7,542, and the Nasdaq Composite (NASDAQINDEX: ^IXIC) fell 1.07% to 25,164. Among peers in AI cloud infrastructure and specialized data center services, Oracle closed at $119.10, down 8.85%, and Nebius Group closed at $141.39, down 8.27%, underscoring weakness across chip-adjacent infrastructure names.

The latest price action in CoreWeave shows continued shareholder discomfort with the cloud stock's reliance on debt.

Although the expanded partnership with CrowdStrike is objectively good news for the company, the excitement over that deal was short-lived as the stock's downtrend continued.

Customer interest is not the issue. Demand for neocloud infrastructure is insatiable, as CoreWeave's backlog totaled $104 billion, not including Q3 customer commitments.

Still, that demand does the company little good if its ability to finance infrastructure builds remains in question. Considering that industry insiders like Elon Musk think many neoclouds will fail, CoreWeave is likely to remain under the gun for the foreseeable future.

In fairness, CoreWeave is not the only neocloud stock struggling, as both Nebius and Oracle also plunged. However, Oracle still has other legacy businesses, and Nebius is a profitable company with a debt level that's a fraction of CoreWeave's approximate $35 billion in obligations.

The real test for CoreWeave stock may come when it announces its earnings for the third quarter of 2026 on Nov. 10. Factors such as growth, capital expenditures, and debt levels will be where investors will probably focus.

If CoreWeave can continue building infrastructure, address its backlog, and service its debt, it may eventually win back investors' confidence. Nonetheless, any interruptions to this growth could be costly for CoreWeave, making investing in the stock a risky proposition at this time.

#3053. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#3050. Why Capri Holdings Stock Crushed it Today

The company was the target of a clutch of analyst price target raises.

Still riding the positive momentum from a satisfying quarterly earnings report, Capri Holdings (CPRI +7.33%) stock cruised to a more than 7% gain on Monday. Also providing plenty of fuel for this was a set of analyst price target increases on the fashion conglomerate's stock.

Capri's second quarter of fiscal 2027 results were published Friday morning. Although revenue declined by almost 6% year over year to $809 million, the company flipped to a non-GAAP (adjusted) net income of $45 million. On top of that, it obliterated analyst profitability estimates and comfortably beat on the top line.

On Monday, analysts tracking the stock reacted overwhelmingly positively to the consumer discretionary company's performance. Several expressed this by raising their Capri price targets.

Among these was Brooke Roach from the ever-influential investment bank Goldman Sachs (GS +0.47%). She lifted hers to $24 from $18, albeit while maintaining her neutral recommendation. A more bullish note was authored by Barclays' (BCS +1.39%) Adrienne Yih, who now feels the stock is worth $24 apiece, up from her preceding $19. She kept her overweight (i.e., buy) recommendation intact.

I'm always rather hesitant about fashion stocks, as they're subject to trends, whims, and the health of the economy. That said, Capri's Jimmy Choo line did especially well and looks set to continue doing so, even if the larger Michael Kors unit is currently in turnaround mode. Meanwhile, management is effectively trimming costs behind the scenes. If I were to consider buying any fashion title, I'd certainly add this one to the list.

#3046. BBB Foods Same-Store Sales Jump 20.5%: Rapid Earnings Reaction

Mexico's fast-growing grocery discounter is getting more out of the stores it already has. Here's what that means for its margins, its thin profit, and its cash.

BBB Foods Inc. (TBBB +0.65%) grew third-quarter revenue 40.3% to about 28.4 billion Mexican pesos, with most of the gain coming from stores open at least a year. Shares rose about 5% in after-hours trading. The Mexican grocery discounter is adding stores faster than a year ago, but its older stores still do most of the work, and their growth is what's widening margins.

A hard discounter lives on thin margins, and rent, staff, and trucks cost about the same whether a store sells a little or a lot. When existing stores' sales climb this fast, those fixed costs get spread over far more revenue, which pushed store operating costs down to 9.8% of revenue. The release doesn't say whether more shoppers or bigger baskets drove the gain.

The swing to a profit owes more to shrinking stock pay than to operations, because a large 2025 share grant is expensed most heavily in its early quarters. Profit was also thin: Without a 97 million-peso currency gain, pre-tax profit would have fallen short of the quarter's tax bill.

Cash is the clearer signal. BBB Foods sells its groceries before it pays suppliers, so growth produces cash rather than eating it. Third-quarter operating cash flow was about 2.5 times spending on stores and equipment, a cushion that depends on suppliers keeping today's terms.

Watch whether same-store sales hold near 20% as openings continue, since that pays for the margin gains. The release gives no outlook, so the fourth-quarter report is the next real check.

#3042. Eli Lilly Stock: Is It Still a Buy at $1,400?

The company just released some incredible quarterly results, but its valuation is also fairly high.

Eli Lilly (LLY -0.46%) stock recently hit a new all-time high of more than $1,400. The company has given investors more reasons to be bullish about it after releasing its latest quarterly numbers, which, once again, demonstrated impressive year-over-year growth.

The problem, however, is that a big chunk of that growth, or perhaps all of it, may have been priced into its valuation. That's because with a high earnings multiple, expectations will be high, and the market may already be pricing in significant growth.

At $1,400, Eli Lilly stock certainly isn't cheap. But given its growth potential, is the healthcare stock still worth buying at around these levels?

Eli Lilly's revenue for the third quarter, which ended on Sept. 30, totaled $24.4 billion, rising by 39% year over year. That's actually a bit of a slowdown for the business, as its growth rate over the past nine months has been 46%.

That's more of a sign of how big the business has become, however, as inevitably, growth rates will come down the bigger the numbers are. And they're big, with revenue from the company's leading GLP-1 products, Mounjaro and Zepbound, totaling a combined $16.1 billion, accounting for roughly two-thirds of the company's overall sales.

Management has raised its full-year guidance, now projecting sales between $91 and $92 billion (versus an earlier forecast of $85 to $87 billion). However, it also slightly lowered its earnings-per-share guidance by 50 cents on both the upper and lower ends, now projecting between $35 and $36 per share.

In just the past six months, Eli Lilly's stock has skyrocketed around 46%. The excitement around its GLP-1 drugs has been a huge catalyst for the stock. But it also begs the question of whether the bloated valuation already prices in significant future growth.

With the stock trading at around 45 times its trailing earnings, I believe that a good chunk is indeed priced in. That can make the stock a bit riskier to buy than usual. And with so much depending on it remaining the dominant player in the GLP-1 market and so much of those products making up the lion's share of its top line, there isn't much, if any, room for error. There's no margin of safety in the stock, which makes buying at this stage a bit concerning.

While Eli Lilly can still be a solid long-term investment, investors may want to temper their expectations for the stock’s near-term performance, as gains may be much more limited than they have been of late.

David Jagielski, CPA has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Eli Lilly. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3040. Fanatics takes bigger NHL design role with all-32 team Hometown Remix jerseys

Sign up today for the CNBC Sport Newsletter

In most professional sports arenas, rinks and stadiums across the globe, the gear that fans are now wearing to games is made and designed by sports merchandise giant Fanatics. But that isn't always the case for the jersey that the players are wearing on the field, ice or court.

On Monday, Fanatics and the NHL debuted the Hometown Remix program, a collection of uniforms and apparel for all 32 NHL teams. The new designs and look of each jersey is focused on each team's city and fan base. It marks the first time that Fanatics has designed and developed a league-wide jersey program for one of its leagues partners, which include the NFL, NBA, and MLB.

For some leagues, such as the NFL, while Fanatics may manufacture jerseys that fans buy in stores, other consumer-focused products and even what coaches wear on the sidelines, what players wear on the field may be manufactured and designed by a different company. In the case of the NFL, that is Nike. In MLB, while Fanatics manufactures player jerseys, Nike designs them.

Fanatics has been a partner of the NHL since 2005, mainly focused on retail and e-commerce. But in 2023, as the league's jersey deal with Adidas lapsed, Fanatics expanded its partnership to include on-ice uniforms and authentic jerseys.

When that deal began alongside the 2024-25 season, it marked the first time that Fanatics branding appeared directly on an official player jersey in a professional game, a massive evolution for the company.

The new collection takes that a "giant step forward in our partnership with the NHL," according to Andrew Low Ah Kee, CEO of Fanatics Commerce, the company's merchandise business arm.

"What's exciting to me about it is that not only is it all 32 teams, it's actually that Fanatics is singularly responsible and accountable for the design and execution of the program in partnership with the [NHL] and the clubs," Low Ah Kee said. "Whereas we work with Nike and others on other leagues and different programs."

Low Ah Kee said the process for this collection began when its expanded deal with the NHL took effect in 2024, and Fanatics' design team has been working across the league, its teams and players since.

Some of the process itself hasn't been totally new: Fanatics has designed jerseys in conjunction with the NHL and some of its teams for tentpole events like the Winter Classic, Stadium Series and the 4 Nations Face-Off. However, not every NHL team has participated in one of those events.

But Low Ah Kee said doing it in the NHL, which he said has the "most complex construction of any sports jersey out there," while also adding new design elements, highlighting unique aspects of each team's fans and local culture, has been a big moment for Fanatics.

"I don't think this will be a capstone, but this is certainly a big milestone in the relationship that we have," Low Ah Kee said.

Brian Jennings, NHL senior vice president and chief partnerships and club strategy officer, said the league appreciates the legacy and reverence that hockey jerseys — often called sweaters — hold for fans of the sport. At the same time, Jennings said the league has been excited to create new designs around a league-wide platform that celebrates local team culture.

"Each market and city is unique … we love to celebrate that uniqueness," Jennings said.

Jennings said that each team will wear its jersey for a set of games throughout the remainder of this season, providing opportunities to highlight the designs and storytelling.

While this is just set to be a program for the 2026-2027 season, Jennings said that if a team wanted to integrate some of the logos, colors or designs into a future alternate jersey or other products, the NHL and Fanatics would be open to it.

This Hometown Remix collection is also setting the groundwork for future league-wide efforts with Fanatics, Jennings said. "In an ideal world, we'll start to ideate immediately around what is another version," he said. "Once we have the ability to collect some insights, what was successful, what wasn't, what do we want to turn the volume up on … we want to keep the design integrity really, really high and delight and excite fans."

Jennings said these moments do boost the business side as well. While he declined to provide specific targets, he pointed to the bump that the league and teams see when new jerseys and merchandise are released for special event games like the Winter Classic. He also noted the follow-on effects on things like ticket sales and sponsorship activation when teams host special theme nights or special jersey matchups. "It creates a lot of excitement for the clubs that they can market around," he said.

Low Ah Kee said Fanatics is "very aware" that the launch of this league-wide program is happening just ahead of holiday shopping season. At the same time, this collection of jerseys is "limited supply," he said. The prices of these jerseys do vary slightly from some of the traditional NHL jerseys due to some of the unique production features, Fanatics said.

Pricing on Hometown Remix jerseys with custom lettering and numbering are $275 for a premium jersey and $500 for authentics (same as worn on ice by players).

Both Jennings and Low Ah Kee acknowledged the challenges that come with launching new designs of beloved team jerseys: the somewhat unpredictable fan response.

Jennings said that there are so many unique design elements, not just on the front of the jersey but down to the hockey socks that the players wear and how certain parts of the jersey are embossed and raised, that he believes that "fans will really respect that fact."

"The debate about the design will always be ongoing, but I think they'll see the quality come shining through," Jennings said.

When these new jerseys hit the ice on players' backs, it'll come with the Fanatics flag brand on it as well, and Low Ah Kee said while that is a point of pride for the company, it's also a reflection that Fanatics is "accountable for the quality of the garments, the manufacturing of the garments, the design of the garments," he said.

"The protagonists in the story are the fan and their team; we're the supporting actor, and we know that," Low Ah Kee said. "Our job is to think about how do you make that better. Hometown Remix we hope is going to be a really great way for fans to express their love of their cities, their clubs and their markets overall, and we hope people have fun."

#3037. Alphabet Lost $500 Billion in Market Value. Should You Really Buy AI Stocks Now?

Last week, the stock had its worst day ever.

Alphabet (GOOG -0.07%) (GOOGL -0.30%) investors had reason to frown last week as the stock posted its worst trading day ever. The shares fell 13% in one session after certain key elements in the tech giant's earnings report disappointed investors.

What happened? It all starts with artificial intelligence (AI). Alphabet is at the forefront of the AI movement, something that has led to soaring revenue quarter after quarter. But to make this happen, Alphabet had to greatly invest in an infrastructure build-out, and this spending isn't over. Alphabet isn't the only company in this situation; others operating in the cloud space also are investing hundreds of billions of dollars annually to get the job done. In fact, Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, and Meta Platforms aim to spend more than $700 billion this year alone on infrastructure expansion. Though these companies are generating growth from AI, investors worry it may not be enough given their spending levels.

Considering the full picture, following Alphabet's loss of $500 billion in market value in one trading session, you might approach AI stocks with a bit of hesitance. Should you really buy AI stocks now? Let's find out.

We'll start by talking about the negative impact of this spending on Alphabet. You probably know this company best for Google Search, as it's something many of us use daily. The Google platform is indeed the company's biggest revenue driver -- advertisers pay to promote across it, and that generates billions of dollars for Alphabet. In the recent quarter, Google ads accounted for 64% of the company's total revenue.

But Alphabet also has a cloud computing business that's growing in leaps and bounds, supercharged by demand for AI infrastructure and enterprise solutions. That business delivered a record 103% gain in revenue in the quarter to more than $30 billion. This is positive and offers investors reason to be optimistic, but as mentioned, to reach this level of growth, Alphabet poured investment into an infrastructure build-out.

As a result, capital spending has exploded higher, and this has hurt free cash flow -- these two elements were the points of concern in the company's earnings report. Alphabet kept its capex forecast for the year unchanged at $195 billion to $205 billion. As for free cash flow, it came in at negative $9 billion, missing analysts' estimates of $2.7 billion.

All of this reawakened investors' concerns that, though AI investment is driving growth, this growth still may not justify the spending levels of Alphabet and other tech players in this AI revolution.

Now, let's return to our question: Should you really buy AI stocks right now? AI companies today may indeed be in a period of transition. Over the past couple of years, investors have been excited about the initial AI opportunity as hardware and services companies have offered chips and related products to customers. This resulted in enormous revenue growth and double- and triple-digit gains in stock prices. And this, prior to the major infrastructure ramp-up, didn't involve today's capex levels.

Today, we're at a point where, to move forward along the AI path to fulfill demand and potentially generate more growth, tech giants must heavily invest. While this investment is unfolding, they will experience pressure. Amazon chief Andy Jassy told analysts during the company's second-quarter call in July that this spending would continue to be a headwind for free cash flow as the build-out unfolds.

With some investors frowning on this spending, we could see more turbulence in terms of stock performance in the months to come. If you're a very cautious investor, you might wait on the sidelines for this moment to at least partially pass. But for most investors, right now, as these stocks decline, it's the perfect time to buy at a bargain price. The long-term AI story remains bright, with the technology's ability to transform the way businesses operate in a positive way. And this means investors who include AI stocks in a well-diversified portfolio could score a major win over the long run.

Adria Cimino has positions in Amazon. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms, and Microsoft. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3033. Mechanics Bancorp (MCHB) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Core income of $60.6 million reflects balance sheet optimization and merger synergies.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Mechanics Bancorp (MCHB +2.80%) reported core net income of $60.6 million for the third quarter, focused on balance sheet optimization through the exit of noncore business lines and a restructuring of the investment portfolio. Management completed the sale of the runoff indirect auto loan portfolio and reinvested $308 million of available-for-sale securities into higher-yielding mortgage-backed securities to enhance future earnings. The company reported continued progress on cost synergies following the 2025 HomeStreet merger, contributing to an improved core efficiency ratio. Despite net interest margin compression driven by rising deposit costs, the bank maintained elevated capital ratios and confirmed plans for a $100 million dividend in the fourth quarter. Management updated forward guidance for 2027 to reflect a higher interest rate environment while noting significant embedded repricing opportunities within the legacy loan and securities portfolios.

Operator: Good morning, ladies and gentlemen, and welcome to the Mechanics Bancorp Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] As a reminder, this conference call is being recorded. I would now like to turn the call over to Nathan Duda, Chief Financial Officer of Mechanics. Please go ahead.

Nathan Duda: Thank you, operator, and good morning, everyone. We appreciate you joining our earnings conference call. With me here today are CJ Johnson, our President and CEO; and Carl Webb, our Executive Chairman. The related earnings press release and earnings presentation are available on the News and Events section of our Investor Relations website. Before we begin, I'd like to remind everyone that any forward-looking statements are subject to risks, uncertainties and other factors that could cause actual results to differ materially from those anticipated future results. Please see our safe harbor statements in our earnings press release and in our earnings presentation. All comments expressed or implied made during today's call are subject to those safe harbor statements.

Any forward-looking statements made during this call are made only as of today's date, and we do not undertake any duty to update such forward-looking statements, except as required by law. Additionally, during today's call, we may discuss certain non-GAAP financial measures, which we believe are useful in evaluating our performance. A reconciliation of these non-GAAP financial measures to the most comparable GAAP financial measures can also be found in our earnings release and in the earnings presentation. CJ, let me hand it over to you.

C. Johnson: Thank you, Nathan, and good morning. We appreciate everyone joining our call and for your interest in Mechanics Bancorp. I'll start things off by hitting the highlights of our third quarter performance. I'll also provide an update on the bank's strategy as we close out 2026 before handing things off to Nathan to review our financials in more detail. Carl, Nathan and I will then open up the call for your questions. With that, let's turn to Slide 4. We had a noisy but productive third quarter, reporting $46 million in net income.

On a fully diluted basis, our earnings per share was $0.20, and our tangible book value per share ended the quarter at $7.05 with $0.25 per share of dividends paid to investors. The third quarter was a very busy one and included an AFS securities restructure and the sale of nearly all our remaining runoff auto loans. We also had a negative provision for a combination of reasons, along with several nonrecurring gains. I'll get into more detail on this on the next page. After adjusting for these various onetime items, we earned $60.6 million of core net income in Q3 for a core ROAA of 1.14% and a core ROTCE of 15.1%.

Our total assets are now $20.9 billion with total net loans of $12.9 billion, total deposits of $18 billion and tangible common equity of $1.63 billion. As I mentioned last quarter, we wrapped up our HomeStreet integration in Q2 and a lot of that benefit showed up in Q3 as our noninterest expense declined over $10 million on a linked-quarter basis. This helped lower our core efficiency ratio to 54.4% from 56% in Q2. Overall, we significantly exceeded our cost savings budget for the merger, which is good to see hit the bottom line.

The big news during the quarter was, of course, the shock up in long-term interest rates with the yield on the 10-year treasury rising over 80 basis points. As a result, our AOCI decreased $112 million despite the AFS restructure. Relatedly, our cost of deposits increased 8 basis points in the third quarter, driven partly by the continued mix shift into money market funds from noninterest-bearing accounts as well as higher CD pricing. Beginning in the second quarter, we increased CD pricing to more competitive market rates to stem the runoff we had deliberately planned post the HomeStreet merger.

That repricing had a more significant impact on our third quarter cost of deposits given that most of our CDs have a maturity of less than seven months. Overall, our deposits decreased $70 million this quarter with $66 million of the decline in time deposits. Our pace of CD runoff slowed again in Q3, and we expect CD balances to be relatively flat moving forward given our more competitive pricing. Our NIM decreased 7 basis points this quarter, with most of the compression due to the increase in our cost of deposits, although there was some noise given the AFS restructure and auto loan sale that Nathan will discuss later.

We also had elevated dividend income in the second quarter, which contributed 3 basis points to the Q3 drop in NIM. An important point to note is that while deposit cost pressures clearly increased earlier in the year as interest rates rose sharply, we got very little benefit from our floating rate earning assets repricing that will show up in the fourth quarter as most of the floating rates reset with a bit of a lag. As expected, we had another clean credit quarter with only 4 basis points of non-auto net charge-offs. Our ACL is now 1.03% of loans and totaled $134 million. Our NPAs were flat again for the quarter and remained at 0.28% of total assets.

Our CET1 ratio continued to move higher to a very robust 14.7% with the increase aided by the reduction in risk-weighted assets from our auto loan sale. Let's turn to Slide 5, where I'll update you on the bank's strategy and forward outlook. We had a lot going on in the third quarter with several major initiatives accomplished. First, at the end of July, we sold $308 million of AFS securities yielding 1.78% at a $32.3 million pre-tax loss and reinvested the net proceeds in mortgage-backed securities yielding 5.5%.

This was a strategic use of capital to enhance future earnings, and we are fortunate to have excellent capital ratios that allow us the continued flexibility to consider actions such as this. Next, in mid-September, we sold nearly all runoff auto loans at a price close to par, resulting in the successful acceleration of the exit of this noncore business line. This is something we have been considering for some time, and we were very happy to see such strong execution. The transaction improved our CET1 ratio by reducing risk-weighted assets, and we expect it to modestly enhance earnings moving forward as lower loan servicing expense should more than offset lost NIM.

We expect noninterest expense to decline again next quarter, mostly as a result of the auto exit and now forecast approximately $420 million in annualized core NIE, excluding CDI amortization in Q4. I'll quickly mention that we had $15.2 million of provision release this quarter with $3.2 million related to the sale of our one-off auto loans. The other $12 million of negative provision was primarily due to lower expected loss rates on multifamily and CRE loans. We recorded a $5.1 million gain on other equity investments, which is from our modest ownership stake in Inclined, which Nathan will discuss in more depth later.

We also closed out our purchase accounting from the HomeStreet merger with an additional $2.9 million in bargain purchase gain, which was tax related. We only had $600,000 of merger-related expenses in Q3, which was good to see as we continue to move past the noise of the HomeStreet deal. Core net income grew to $60.6 million from $59 million quarter-over-quarter as expense efficiencies more than offset modest NIM compression. Deposit pressures, however, will persist in this challenging interest rate environment, and we expect a relatively flat near-term NIM as we plan to absorb three additional Fed funds rate increases over the next 12 months.

We remain liability sensitive on the short end of the curve, although that gap has narrowed with our recent growth in floating rate assets, most notably incline loans. Longer term, we have significant repricing opportunity embedded in our balance sheet with $4.7 billion of legacy low-yielding earning assets that will cash flow over time and be reinvested at much higher market rates, which should provide a meaningful tailwind to our NIM. We continue to have substantial excess capital and expect to pay a $100 million dividend or $0.43 per Class A share in Q4, subject to Board and regulatory approval.

This should bring our total dividends paid to shareholders in 2026 to $400 million or $1.78 per Class A share, representing a 2026 dividend yield in excess of 11%. However, the interest rate environment, especially the expected rise in short-term rates has caused us to modestly reduce our forward guidance to a 17% ROTCE and 1.2% ROAA in 2027, although we do remain confident in an 18% plus ROTCE over the long run. Importantly, we expect a 2027 dividend yield to investors of approximately 8%. I'll touch quickly on Slide 6, which shows an overview of mechanics.

We are just under $21 billion in assets with 166 branches and the fourth best deposit market share across California and the West Coast with a branch map that stretches from Canada to Mexico and all the way out to Hawaii. Mechanics is a high-performing bank despite taking very little risk. On the left-hand side of this page, we compare mechanics to all publicly traded banks, $10 billion to $100 billion in assets, which, including us, has 81 banks in the peer group. As you can see, we rank very favorably across all these KPIs. Going to Slide 7, which I think best illustrates our investment thesis.

The strength of our deposits and the efficiency with which we expect to run our bank from both an expense and a capital management standpoint, allows us to deliver high-quality earnings at a market-leading 8% dividend yield. We expect to pay these significant dividends despite a very conservative balance sheet, which is carefully underwritten, highly liquid and core funded relative to our banking peers. We believe high-quality, low-risk earnings that are efficiently paid as dividends to investors will command a premium multiple over time, especially during periods of market stress. To wrap up my section, let's turn to Slide 8.

This slide hits our investment highlights and starts with our fantastic market share across the West Coast with a branch footprint that's nearly impossible to replicate. We have very strong profitability, thanks to our great deposits and efficient business model despite taking very little credit risk. We are core funded with no wholesale borrowings or broker deposits and are highly capitalized with a very liquid balance sheet. We are prudent with our capital and expect to continue to pay a market-leading dividend to investors. There's also a firm alignment between our public and private investors as Ford Financial Fund owns 74% of the company.

Finally, we have an experienced management team who I'm lucky to work with every day with a strong operating and M&A track record. With that, let me turn the call over to Nathan to provide more details on our third quarter results. We've also added a new page on our consumer lending efforts, which he will go through. Nathan?

Nathan Duda: Thank you, CJ. Starting on Slide 10. For the third quarter, net interest income declined $2.3 million or 1.3% to $174.9 million compared to the linked quarter. Average interest-earning assets declined approximately $133 million during the quarter, driven primarily by lower average loan balances. Our net interest margin decreased 7 basis points to 3.55%, driven primarily by higher deposit costs. Our total cost of deposits increased to 1.33% from 1.25% in the second quarter, reflecting higher average deposit rates and a shift in deposit mix. This pressure was partially offset by higher investment securities income from the partial quarter benefit from our securities portfolio restructuring completed in July.

There was also some noise in the reported margin this quarter from the timing of the sale of our auto loan portfolio in September. With both the securities restructuring and the auto loan sale occurring during the quarter, the third quarter results did not reflect a full quarter impact from either action. Third quarter interest income included $11.4 million of discount accretion on loans acquired in the HomeStreet transaction. As of September 30, 2026, we had approximately $125 million of remaining discount on those acquired loans. Lastly, earning asset mix remained relatively stable during the quarter as investment securities increased modestly as a percentage of average earning assets following the sales of auto loans. Turning to Slide 11.

This slide provides some additional detail on what we continue to view as one of the most important drivers of our future earnings growth. We have approximately $4.7 billion of lower-yielding legacy assets with a weighted average roll-off yield of 3.25%, comprised of multifamily loans, single-family residential loans and held-to-maturity securities. As these assets mature, pay down or reset, we have an opportunity to reinvest or reprice them at significantly higher current market rates. As shown on the slide, approximately $2.6 billion or 56% of the portfolio is expected to cash flow through 2030.

Based on the replacement yields illustrated on the slide and assuming a static rate environment, this repricing opportunity could ultimately generate approximately $154 million of incremental annual pre-tax income or approximately $113 million after tax. Importantly, this earnings opportunity is already embedded within our existing balance sheet and does not require balance sheet growth or a change in our conservative risk profile. As these assets continue to reprice over time, we expect this embedded opportunity to provide a meaningful tailwind to net interest income and margin. Turning to Slide 12. This illustrative analysis shows the potential impact of changes in short-term interest rates by comparing our floating rate assets with our rate-sensitive deposits.

At September 30, we had approximately $3.9 billion of floating rate assets compared with $8.5 billion of rate-sensitive deposits, resulting in a gap of approximately $4.6 billion. As shown on the slide, we currently estimate that a 25 basis point increase in short-term rates would reduce annualized net interest income by approximately $5.2 million, while a 25 basis point reduction would provide a similar benefit. Our deposit beta was approximately 30% during the last Fed hiking cycle, and we expect it to be modestly higher this time around. Importantly, we expect this rate sensitivity to diminish over time as more of our fixed rate assets mature, pay down or reset and are reinvested at current market rates.

Turning to Slide 13. Noninterest income decreased $37.2 million to a net loss of $13.4 million compared to $23.8 million in the second quarter. The third quarter included $34.6 million of net nonrecurring losses, primarily reflecting the $32.3 million loss associated with the AFS securities restructuring and the $10.3 million loss on sale of auto loans. These losses were partially offset by a $5.1 million gain on other equity investments and a $2.9 million bargain purchase gain adjustment related to the HomeStreet acquisition. Excluding these noncore items, noninterest income was relatively stable at $21.2 million compared to $21.6 million in the prior quarter.

The modest decline primarily reflected lower ATM network fee and bank card royalty income, partially offset by higher bank-owned life insurance income. Turning to Slide 14. Noninterest expense decreased $10.3 million or 8.3% to $114.2 million compared to $124.5 million in the second quarter. Merger-related expenses declined to $0.6 million during the quarter from $5.9 million in the prior quarter and were primarily comprised of consulting and professional service fees. Excluding merger-related expenses, noninterest expense declined $4.9 million from the linked quarter, driven primarily by staffing and core conversion synergies realized during the quarter. As a result, our core efficiency ratio improved to 54.4% compared to 56% in the second quarter.

Excluding CDI amortization, annualized core noninterest expense was approximately $427 million during the quarter. We expect continued expense improvement in the fourth quarter and now anticipate a $420 million core annualized run rate in Q4. Turning to Slide 15. Loan interest income declined $2.8 million or 1.6% to $175.4 million compared to the second quarter. Loan yields declined 4 basis points to 5.18%, driven primarily by slightly lower purchase discount accretion. Multifamily yields declined 12 basis points, reflecting lower discount accretion, lower contractual yields and lower prepayment and late fees. This was partially offset by a 10 basis point increase in single-family residential yields driven by higher discount accretion and contractual yields.

C&I yields also declined due to lower discount accretion. Total loans declined approximately $561 million during the quarter, driven primarily by the sale of the auto portfolio and normal loan repayments, partially offset by new originations and advances. Our CRE concentration ratio improved modestly to 340% at quarter end from 342% at June 30. During the quarter, we originated approximately $568 million of loan commitments, predominantly in single-family residential and life insurance cash surrender value-backed loans. We also sold $417 million of auto loans and approximately $13 million of single-family residential loans during the quarter. Turning to Slide 16. Our commercial lending portfolio remains highly granular and well diversified across both collateral types and geography.

We continue to maintain a significant focus on lower-risk multifamily lending, which represents approximately 72% of our $7.2 billion commercial real estate portfolio. Our multifamily loans have an average loan size of approximately $4 million and an average LTV of 55% and an average debt coverage ratio of 1.54x. Our retail and office exposures also remain modest and conservatively underwritten. Retail loans have an average LTV of 47% and debt coverage ratio of 1.95x, while office loans have an average LTV of 49% and debt coverage ratio of 1.7x.

Office balances declined $27 million or 5% during the quarter, and we have just 7 office loans totaling $35 million in the central business districts of Los Angeles, Oakland, San Francisco and Seattle. We also continue to reduce the legacy HomeStreet syndicated loan portfolio, which has declined from approximately $142 million at September 30, 2025, to approximately $64 million at September 30, 2026. Lastly, we have no loans to non-depository financial institutions, while technology-related exposure represents less than 1% of our C&I portfolio. Turning to Slide 17. Our single-family residential and HELOC portfolios continue to reflect strong credit characteristics.

Our $3.6 billion single-family residential portfolio has an average FICO score of 793 and an average LTV of 49%, while our $550 million HELOC portfolio has an average FICO score of 786. In addition, we service approximately $4.1 billion of single-family residential loans for others, which provides a stable source of revenue, particularly in an elevated interest rate environment. As we originate new single-family residential loans, we have increasingly focused on intermediate-term addressable rate mortgages to help reduce interest rate risk. Approximately 90% of our 2026 year-to-date SFR portfolio originations by dollar volume have been intermediate ARMs.

Lastly, we continue to see attractive growth in our partnership with Inclined, which originates floating rate loans secured by the cash surrender value of whole life insurance policies. We ended the quarter with $837 million of these loans outstanding, up from $599 million at the end of 2025. Since the program's inception, we have experienced 0 net charge-offs and no loans over 30 days past due. The portfolio also carries nominal servicing expense and given its floating rate structure, helps reduce interest rate risk and balance the duration of our overall loan portfolio. Turning to Slide 18. Our asset quality remained strong during the quarter.

Non-auto net charge-offs were approximately 4 basis points annualized and nonperforming assets remained stable at 0.28% of total assets. Total delinquent loans declined to $67.5 million or 0.52% of total loans from $95.4 million or 0.70% at June 30. The decrease in delinquencies was primarily attributable to the auto portfolio sale and 2 matured commercial real estate loans that were extended and brought current during the quarter. During the third quarter, we recorded $15.2 million reversal of provision for credit losses compared to a $2.8 million reversal in the second quarter. The third quarter reversal was primarily driven by lower expected losses on multifamily and CRE investor loans, corresponding reductions in qualitative factors and the sale of the auto portfolio.

Our allowance for credit losses totaled $133.6 million or 1.03% of total loans held for investment at quarter end compared to $152.6 million or 1.12% at June 30. The allowance remains approximately 2.24x total nonperforming assets. Turning to Slide 19. Securities interest income increased $5.4 million or 10.2% to $58.5 million compared to the second quarter as the average portfolio yield increased 24 basis points to 4.21%. During the quarter, we completed a $308 million restructuring of our available-for-sale securities portfolio, reinvesting the proceeds from lower-yielding securities into higher-yielding mortgage-backed securities. The transaction resulted in a $32.3 million pre-tax loss with an estimated earn-back period of 4 to 5 years.

The restructuring contributed to the improvement in portfolio yields during the quarter despite only a partial quarter benefit. Turning to Slide 20. Total deposits declined $70 million during the quarter to approximately $18 billion. The total cost of deposits increased 8 basis points to 1.33% compared to 1.25% in the second quarter. The increase in deposit costs reflected higher average rates and a shift in deposit mix. Non-maturity deposits declined modestly during the quarter, while the mix continued to shift toward higher-yielding money market accounts. Importantly, our deposit franchise remains a significant strength with noninterest-bearing deposits representing approximately 35% of total deposits at quarter end. Turning to Slide 21.

This slide provides some additional detail on the composition and granularity of our deposit franchise. Our deposits remain well diversified across consumer, business and public customers with an average account balance of approximately 44,000 and a median balance of approximately 3,400. The relationship weighted average age of these accounts is approximately 19 years, reflecting the long-standing nature of our customer relationships. We believe this granularity and relationship tenure are important strengths of our core deposit franchise. Turning to capital and liquidity on Slide 22. We remain very well capitalized with a 14.7% Tier 1 risk-based capital ratio, a 17% total risk-based capital ratio and an 8.7% Tier 1 leverage ratio at September 30.

Each of our capital ratios remains above the applicable regulatory requirements and our internal policy limits. Regulatory capital ratios for the third quarter remain preliminary. Available liquidity totaled approximately $15.8 billion at quarter end compared with $15.9 billion at June 30. This included approximately $15 billion of available borrowing capacity, $400 million of excess reserves at the Federal Reserve and $400 million of other unencumbered securities. During the third quarter, we also paid dividends of $0.25 per share of Class A common stock and $2.50 per share of Class B common stock, reflecting our continued commitment to return capital to shareholders. #3029. Prediction: This Will Be SpaceX’s Stock Price by the End of 2027

Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +1.23%) has had an eventful first four months on the stock market. But the company hasn't made much money for investors who have held on since its debut. As of writing, SpaceX's shares are trading at $134 each, below the company's IPO price of $135. Still, SpaceX boasts attractive opportunities. How will the company perform over the next year? Let's try to determine what its share price might be by the end of 2027.

SpaceX's financial results have been strong this year. In the third quarter, the company's revenue grew by 161% year over year to $14.6 billion. It also posted a net income of $4.4 billion -- or $0.35 per diluted share -- substantially better than the prior-year quarter's net loss of $1.1 billion (or $0.36 per share).

SpaceX's annual revenue run rate is now at $58.4 billion. Analysts expect the company's total revenue for the current fiscal year to be $48.93 billion (according to Yahoo! Finance), and its earnings per share (EPS) to be $0.22, as of this writing. What about next year? Analysts are projecting $180.37 billion in revenue and $4.03 in EPS, on average.

Note that at current levels, SpaceX is trading at about 33.3x projected 2027 earnings, which seems reasonable for a company growing as fast as it is. But there is a complication. These projections may already be baked into the share price. After all, despite excellent financial results for as long as it has been public, SpaceX stock has gone nowhere.

The question, then, is whether SpaceX will perform roughly in line with, above, or below expectations over the next 12 months. Let's consider the bullish case first. SpaceX's artificial intelligence segment, dubbed Super Intelligence (SI), is booming. The company has more demand for AI compute capacity than it is willing to rent out. Third-quarter SI revenue soared 830% year over year to $7.6 billion. Perhaps the market is underestimating the company's potential in this industry.

The SI segment could maintain outstanding growth provided SpaceX adds more compute deals to its portfolio. Connectivity is also performing. SpaceX's push into additional regions within this market could deliver significant subscription growth. Then there is SpaceX's space segment.

This unit isn't profitable, but progress on the company's Starship rocket could be key over the next year. If Starship launch cadence improves meaningfully within the next year, and there are no significant issues with the rocket -- regulatory or otherwise -- that could be a massive catalyst for the stock.

The bull scenario might convince the market that SpaceX's prospects beyond 2027 are very attractive, allowing the company to continue to command high earnings multiples. Assuming the fiscal year 2026 and 2027 predictions are correct, along with a constant forward P/E (based on the following year's earnings) and projected 2028 EPS of $7.01, the stock could soar by about 74% to $233 by the end of 2027.

In my view, that's too optimistic. Several factors might hinder SpaceX's prospects. Revenue and earnings growth could normalize after their blistering recent pace. Launch cadence for SpaceX's Starship might not reach the levels the market expects, and competition across the company's business will intensify. That last point is important.

Nobody should expect one of SpaceX's competitors to catch up to the company in the space launch or connectivity markets within the next year. Still, if they make significant progress, the rocket company might no longer command the same kind of valuation premium. If we make just one change to our model and assume, instead, that the company's forward P/E drops to 25, SpaceX could gain 31% by the end of 2027. Under this scenario, the company's share price will be $175.25.

That's much closer to what may happen, in my view, but here's the problem: SpaceX's prospects look attractive, but they are also incredibly uncertain. Beyond the variables already discussed, there is the very real possibility that SpaceX will seek to acquire Tesla (TSLA +1.00%) within the next year. We can't be sure, and there is no official announcement yet.

But CEO Elon Musk himself has said it would be a good idea and has indicated the formation of independent special committees to weigh the pros and cons of a potential merger between the two companies.

Will a merger be announced? If it is, how much regulatory scrutiny and public backlash will it attract? Nobody knows the answer to these questions yet. With all this uncertainty, SpaceX may well deliver outstanding returns through the end of 2027 or beyond, but it might also be a wealth destroyer. I'd like a much bigger margin of error before buying the stock.

Prosper Junior Bakiny has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Tesla. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3026. Palantir's U.S. Revenue Soars 119% as Commercial Revenue Surpasses Government Revenue for the First Time. Here's Why Its Growth Is Still Surging, Even as Other AI Stocks Falter

The AI and data mining specialist delivered another quarter of triple-digit U.S. revenue growth and boosted its full-year guidance.

In many ways, Palantir Technologies (PLTR -3.13%) is a battleground stock like no other. Bulls cite the company's track record of accelerating growth, while bears argue that its stratospheric valuation is laughable.

Palantir weighed in with the results of its quarterly financial report, and to say that the adoption of artificial intelligence (AI) continued unfettered just might be an understatement.

Given the recent rout in AI-related stocks, Palantir had a lot to prove heading into its third-quarter earnings report, and the AI and data-mining specialist delivered. Revenue of $2.3 billion rose 96% year-over-year -- marking the company's highest-ever growth rate -- while also climbing 20% quarter-over-quarter. This fueled earnings per share (EPS) of $0.44, which surged 144%.

For context, analysts' consensus estimates called for revenue of $2.2 billion and EPS of $0.41, so Palantir simply crushed Wall Street's expectations.

Palantir's U.S. commercial segment, which includes its flagship Artificial Intelligence Platform (AIP), led the charge. Revenue for the segment rose 148% year over year and 29% quarter over quarter to $984 million, outpacing U.S. government revenue for the first time. Growth for the U.S. government segment held its own, soaring 96% year over year and 18% sequentially to $953 million.

The company's total contract value (TCV) closed during the quarter was $2.92 billion, up 6% year over year. Of that, the U.S. commercial segment closed TCV of $1.46 billion, up 11%. Furthermore, Palantir's net dollar retention rate of 165% shows that existing customers are spending 65% more than they did this time last year. That's evidence of deepening relationships with its customers.

The building blocks are in place for future growth. Palantir's U.S. commercial segment's remaining deal value (RDV) -- the value of contracts in process or awaiting fulfillment -- surged 79% year over year and 4% quarter over quarter to $6.52 billion.

Other metrics help illustrate the broad base of Palantir's stellar execution. The company's Rule of 40 score -- which measures the quality of earnings -- rose to 158% -- with any number above 40% indicative of financial strength and resilience. Palantir also delivered operating cash flow and adjusted free cash flow of $1.2 billion each, with roughly a 53% margin.

Palantir's financial position continued to improve, with cash and equivalents totaling $10.56 billion.

Moreover, management increased its full-year revenue guidance to roughly 91% growth, up from its forecast for 82% growth issued just last quarter. This shows that Palantir's strategy continues to gain converts.

Make no mistake, despite the company's parabolic growth, its valuation continues to spark a fierce debate. Palantir is now selling for 125 times earnings, but in all fairness, the most commonly used valuation metrics fall short when measuring high-growth stocks.

Using the more appropriate price/earnings-to-growth (PEG) ratio -- which factors in the company's phenomenal growth rate -- returns a multiple of 0.53, when any number less than 1 is the standard for an undervalued stock. For context, Palantir just delivered its 13th consecutive quarter of accelerating revenue growth, and its forecast suggests there’s more in store.

While some AI companies are eager to get their hands on data, Palantir has taken a different approach. CEO Alex Karp argues that businesses should not cavalierly hand over their data to others -- particularly those peddling AI solutions. In doing so, he says, they are unintentionally handing over their proprietary knowledge, workflows, and strategic advantage -- what he calls a business's "alpha" -- or that which makes them special or gives them an edge.

Palantir uses its boot camps to demonstrate how businesses can get real-world AI-powered results and a solid return on investment -- something its rivals lack. Once customers have seen the evidence firsthand, they simply keep coming back. That's why Palantir’s growth continues to surge, even as its AI rivals stumble.

There's no question that the stock carries a hefty premium. As such, any failure -- real or imagined -- will spark a rerating that's both swift and severe. For that reason, Palantir won't be a fit for every investor. Those looking to start a position or add to an existing one should consider dollar-cost averaging.

For my money, the evidence is clear. Palantir stock is a buy.

Danny Vena, CPA has positions in Palantir Technologies. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Palantir Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3025. Nvidia Reports Nov. 17. History Says What Its Stock Usually Does Next.

The chipmaker has topped its own revenue forecast every quarter for over two years. So why have its shares dropped the next day more often than not?

Nvidia (NVDA -1.26%) will report results for the three months ended Oct. 25 (its fiscal third quarter of 2027) after the close on Tuesday, Nov. 17. As of this writing, shares sit near $205, putting the stock about 14% under its Oct. 6 closing high of $239.24.

Investors hoping a strong report can push the stock back toward its record might want to see how the artificial intelligence (AI) chip giant's shares have reacted lately. In all of Nvidia's past 10 reports, going back to May 2024, revenue beat the top of the range management had forecast. But the stock fell the next trading day after six of those.

Still, I don't think this record is much of a reason to avoid the stock before the report.

Because Nvidia's reports come out after trading ends for the day, the easiest way to gauge the move is from the report-day close to the next day's close. On that basis, the worst day of the stretch was in February 2025.

The stock dropped around 8.5% after a fiscal fourth-quarter report with revenue up 78% year over year to $39.3 billion. That was almost $2 billion more than the $37.5 billion management had guided for.

The pattern carried into 2026. Nvidia's revenue for its fiscal 2026 fourth quarter was $68.1 billion in February, over the company's $65.0 billion forecast, and shares dropped around 5% the next day. In May, fiscal first-quarter 2027 revenue of $81.6 billion beat a $78.0 billion forecast, and the stock dipped around 2%. With the drops after the August and November 2025 reports, that was four straight.

Yet August's report snapped the streak. Revenue for the fiscal second quarter more than doubled, up 106% year over year to $96.2 billion, well above the $91.0 billion outlook. And the stock climbed around 9% the next day.

Over all 10 reports, shares moved about 5% up or down on average.

There's no simple pattern in which way they went, either. The February 2025 drop came with a forecast for Nvidia's gross margin to dip to 70.6% from 73%. But August's report also called for a lower gross margin (74%, down from 75%), and the stock climbed.

Of course, the stock's other big gain in the stretch, around 9% in May 2024, followed the same report in which Nvidia announced a 10-for-1 stock split.

For a long-term shareholder, what came after the first day counts more. Here, Nvidia's record looks far better.

After five of the six drops, shares were back above their report-day close by the time Nvidia next reported, around three months later. After the February 2026 fall, for example, the stock rose about 21% from its post-report close by the May 20 report.

The only exception was May 2026. Shares dipped around 2% the day after the report and were about 6% under their report-day close by the time the August report arrived.

The record can go the other way, too. All of the 9% rise after August's report has since faded. As of this writing, shares are around 10% below where they closed the day after that report.

Nvidia is guiding for fiscal third-quarter revenue of $108.0 billion, plus or minus 2%. If it hits the midpoint, revenue would climb around 89% from the $57.0 billion Nvidia pulled in for the same quarter last year.

True, growth at that rate would be a slowdown from the 106% Nvidia posted in its fiscal second quarter. And shares could easily fall again on Nov. 18, the first trading day after the report. Going by the latest 10 reports, a drop is arguably a bit more likely than a rise.

But the stock's valuation already looks reasonable.

Analysts' consensus is for around $15.80 in earnings per share for fiscal 2028, which ends in January 2028. Using that figure, the stock trades at around 14 times earnings. That's a modest price for a company whose revenue more than doubled year over year in its latest quarter.

So, does history say to wait for the report before buying? I don't think so. The day after has tilted negative. But most of those drops didn't last, and Nvidia has kept topping its own revenue forecasts. I'd consider buying shares before Nov. 17 rather than guessing the next day's move.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Nvidia. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3023. Anthropic Just Released Updated Numbers Ahead of Its Nov. IPO. Revenue Growth Slowed Sharply.

Anthropic’s amended S-1 shows revenue still growing at a breakneck pace, but sequential growth fell from 144% in Q2 to about 47% in Q3. Is that an early sign its pricing power is slipping ahead of a possible $1.5 trillion IPO?

Anthropic, the company behind the AI model Claude, is about to go public in what could be one of the biggest public listings of all time, following the record-setting debut of Space Exploration Technologies Corp (SPCX +1.23%) in June.

The company could be valued at around $1.5 trillion, down from earlier talk of $2 trillion. That's a lot to ask, and justifying it relies, in part, on continuing to deliver the blistering growth revealed in its S-1, filed with the SEC last week. But the frontier lab just amended its prospectus, adding Q3 numbers into the mix, and they reveal something that could be a concern.

Here's what investors need to know ahead of the much-anticipated IPO.

Anthropic is growing at an incredible pace for any company, but for a company that's already dealing in quarterly revenues measured in double-digit billions, it's extraordinary. Third-quarter revenue reached just shy of $17 billion, up 1,127% year over year.

It's also up about 47% from the previous quarter.

That seems impressive, and again, at this scale it certainly is, but context matters a whole lot here. The company delivered sequential growth of 144% in Q2, up from 132% in Q1, which was up from 48% in Q4 of last year.

One of the cornerstones of Anthropic's pitch to investors heading into its IPO is that improving AI models will drive broader adoption and continued rapid revenue growth. Investors considering a valuation in the trillions are betting there's a lot more growth ahead.

A sequential slowdown in growth -- a very substantial one at that -- challenges expectations that the earlier pace can continue.

Now, I want to be clear: there are potential explanations that are relatively benign. Anthropic's filing describes capacity constraints, seasonal slowdowns in business activity during the summer, and restrictions on customers who violated its terms, though it doesn't quantify how much each contributed to Q3's slowdown.

The company also disclosed that price reductions on new models and contractual discounts reduced Q3 revenue by about $600 million. Simply adding that back would put sequential growth at roughly 52%, still well below Q2's 144%, although that calculation assumes customers would have used just as much at higher prices.

It's more than possible that these factors are sufficient, that this is basically a temporary dip, and growth will reaccelerate. But I think it's also possible that there's more going on here. I think it's possible that we are seeing the beginnings of Anthropic losing its pricing power as businesses see diminishing returns on using the latest model and AI moves toward becoming a commodity -- something I've been saying for some time now.

I'm not sure Anthropic has a real moat: Claude doesn't need to lose its technological lead for the business to face serious pressure. Competing models only need to be good enough for the work customers actually need done. The truth is, Anthropic could see AI demand keep growing while still seeing revenue growth decelerate or reverse as pricing power erodes.

Johnny Rice has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
