#3022. LeBron Log No. 3: The King dishes, Caitlin Clark visits and closing time

LeBron Log No. 3: The King dishes, Caitlin Clark visits and closing time originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James' latest week with the Sixers included his first 10-assist game with Philadelphia, his best performance with the team, a visit with Caitlin Clark, a crowd-wide finger wag and one jumper he would probably like back.

Some of it belonged in the box score. The rest quickly made its way around social media.

Welcome to the third edition of the LeBron Log.

Throughout the preseason, the Sixers discussed the responsibility that accompanied their desire to play faster.

They wanted to advance the ball before defenses could organize, but Nick Nurse was not interested in accepting extra turnovers as part of the tradeoff. When asked whether he could live with more mistakes if they came with better pace, his answer was simple.

Seven games into the regular season, the Sixers have not yet become one of the NBA's fastest teams. They entered Monday ranked 20th in pace.

Philadelphia has committed an NBA-low 12.7 turnovers per game. James has helped organize the offense as its leading facilitator, recording 35 assists against 11 turnovers.

He leads the Sixers in total assists despite playing only five games. Tyrese Maxey, who has appeared in all seven, is second with 31.

James provided the cleanest example Wednesday night against the Pacers. He finished with 13 points, 10 assists, five rebounds, two blocks and no turnovers during the Sixers' 114-109 comeback victory.

It was his first double-digit-assist performance with Philadelphia. According to StatMuse, James also became the oldest player in its database to record at least 10 points and 10 assists while committing no more than one turnover.

James' night was not flashy by his standards. He attempted 12 shots and finished fifth on the team in scoring. Still, he helped five Sixers reach double figures without surrendering a possession.

The Sixers signed the NBA's all-time leading scorer. Through the opening weeks of his Philadelphia career, he has been their leading passer.

James entered his first NBA Cup game with the Sixers possessing more tournament history than anyone else on the roster.

He led the Lakers to the inaugural Cup championship in 2023 and was named the event's first MVP after averaging 26.4 points, eight rebounds and seven assists during Los Angeles' unbeaten run.

Three years later, his first Cup game in red, white and blue produced his best Sixers performance yet.

James recorded 20 points, nine rebounds, eight assists, one steal and one block in Friday's 98-93 win over the Knicks. He shot 8 for 15 and saved two of his most important baskets for the final 70 seconds.

Jalen Brunson's three-pointer gave New York a 90-89 lead with 1:32 remaining. On the ensuing possession, James backed down OG Anunoby, turned toward the middle and sank a fadeaway to move the Sixers back in front.

VJ Edgecombe extended the lead with a three-pointer as the shot clock expired. After Brunson made two free throws, Maxey drove for a layup and missed. James followed the play, collected the offensive rebound and scored the putback with 11.9 seconds remaining.

The basket sealed Philadelphia's second win over the defending champions and its first of NBA Cup group play.

"Best game all around," Nurse said. "He's been good, but that was his best game for sure."

James has played more than 1,600 regular-season games, but his Philadelphia closing reel has officially begun.

The fourth-quarter baskets were not James' only memorable moments Friday.

After converting an and-one earlier in the game, James broke out his familiar shoulder-shrug celebration. Edgecombe joined him, smiling as he mirrored the move alongside his 41-year-old teammate.

The pair had already developed a personalized handshake featuring two taps to the head and a forceful shrug. This one came during the game.

James supplied another recognizable celebration in the third quarter.

Anunoby drove toward the basket with James trailing the play. James caught him near the rim and was officially credited with his fifth block of the season.

He turned toward the crowd and wagged his finger. The fans joined in.

Edgecombe later delivered the biggest shot of the game, and James met him with an embrace near the sideline. The two are separated by more than 20 years, but they have quickly developed a collection of celebrations.

"He doesn't shy away from the moment," James said of the 21-year-old. "Young kid, but he thrives in the moment."

James was not the only basketball attraction inside Gainbridge Fieldhouse on Wednesday.

Indiana Fever star Caitlin Clark watched the Sixers' win from courtside and brought a small camera with her. It was the second time this year she photographed James during a game.

In March, Clark served as a credentialed photographer when James and the Lakers visited Indiana.

"The KING 👑 … Shot by ME," Clark wrote when she shared those photos.

After Wednesday's game, Clark made her way toward the visiting locker room. She briefly met Maxey before speaking with James in the hallway.

James gave Clark one of his No. 23 Sixers jerseys. The team shared photos of the exchange with a fitting caption.

Clark publicly expressed hope that James would join the Pacers before he signed with Philadelphia. Their mutual respect goes back further.

During Clark's first WNBA season in 2024, James compared the attention surrounding her professional debut to what he experienced when he entered the NBA directly from high school.

"I'm rooting for Caitlin because I've been in that seat before," James said at the time. "I've walked that road before."

James did not speak with reporters after Wednesday's game, so he was not asked about their conversation.

Maxey was asked about meeting Clark for the first time.

"That's just another person that I like to watch," he said. "She cold. She nice. Seeing her today was really cool. Sometimes you try not to fan out, but it was really cool seeing her. It's her building; she plays here too."

James and Edgecombe also produced the week's most widely shared Sixers warmup clip.

Before Wednesday's game, the two danced together near the three-point line. Edgecombe moved away while James stepped back and launched a jumper.

The clip spread quickly across social media. James still walked out of downtown Indianapolis with 10 assists, no turnovers and a victory.

James has made more field goals than anyone in NBA history. Even the career scoring leader is entitled to one warmup mulligan.

#3021. C3.ai vs. SentinelOne: Falling Revenue vs. Steady Sales Growth

C3.ai's revenue has collapsed by more than half since early 2025, while SentinelOne has grown every quarter -- a stark divergence that raises questions about market positioning.

C3.ai (AI -2.53%) primarily earns its revenue by selling enterprise software applications designed for various commercial and government clients across industries like utilities, manufacturing, defense, and financial services.

It recently introduced a new software application development environment to accelerate workflows and appointed a new general counsel, while also navigating an ongoing class action securities fraud lawsuit concerning past disclosures.

SentinelOne (S -7.91%) generates its revenue by providing centralized cybersecurity software that integrates endpoint protection, cloud workload defense, and connected device security to protect digital environments from threats.

It introduced new autonomous security operations software for early access, expanded its collaborative integration agreement with Lenovo, and executed a major convertible debt private placement offering for institutional buyers.

Revenue shows investors the total money flowing into a business from its core commercial activities. Closely tracking this top-line financial figure helps investors understand the total scale and growth trajectory of a business.

C3.ai vs. SentinelOne Revenue over the last 8 quarters.

Data source: Financial Modeling Prep. Data as of Nov. 2, 2026.

A look at the revenue trends between C3.ai and SentinelOne immediately reveals which company is struggling and which is soaring. SentinelOne's sales have consistently experienced quarter-over-quarter expansion stretching back the past two years. This performance illustrates the growing demand among organizations for cybersecurity. The need is particularly pressing amid the rise of artificial intelligence, as the tech can execute lightning-fast hacks with efficiency and at unprecedented scale.

Due to AI’s arrival, I expect SentinelOne's revenue trajectory to continue climbing. In fact, the company forecasted its fiscal third quarter sales to come in between $309 million and $311 million.

C3.ai was doing well two years ago, with revenue growing every quarter until Q2 of 2025. CEO Thomas Siebel had to resign from the position due to health issues last year, and that was when sales nosedived. The company announced his return in June, but it will take time for Siebel to undo the damage from his absence.

C3.ai's $52.4 million in Q2 of this year suggests the possibility of a return to quarterly sales growth. Not only was Q2's top-line figure an improvement over Q1, the company reported a 73% quarter-over-quarter increase in customer bookings. This suggests C3.ai is signing up clients at a solid clip. Subsequent quarters will show if that performance translates into revenue growth.

#3020. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3019. Fed will vote on rates less often. What that could mean for borrowers

After the Federal Reserve held its benchmark interest rate steady last week, Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh announced plans to reduce the number of rate-setting votes over the next two years. That move could potentially extend the timeline for everyday Americans to see borrowing costs adjust, some experts say.

"Fewer Fed meetings in 2027 could mean consumers wait longer to see relief when borrowing costs are elevated," said James Vrachas, executive director at advisory firm Post Oak Group.

Americans may feel somewhat removed from the Fed, but the central bank's moves have a ripple effect on many types of consumer products, including the borrowing rates for credit cards, home loans and auto financing.

For example, most credit cards have variable rates tied to the prime rate, which moves with the Fed's benchmark. However, even with the central bank holding rates unchanged, credit card rates are edging higher as issuers protect themselves from risk, according to LendingTree.

Card issuers are mitigating their exposure from borrowers who may fall behind on payments or default in the face of stubborn inflation in everyday expenses, said Matt Schulz, LendingTree's chief credit analyst.

Generally, card issuers are slow to bring rates down, unless prompted by the Fed's actions, said Schulz: "Issuers will tinker with rates a bit at the margins at other times, but the big moves tend to only come when issuers are pushed."

On Oct. 28, the central bank voted to keep the benchmark borrowing rate at the current target range of 3.75% to 4% as policymakers deliberated next steps with inflation remaining well above the Fed's 2% goal.

The Fed will keep meeting eight times a year, Warsh said at the news conference following the October meeting, but next year, one of the eight meetings will not entail a vote on interest rates or include a post-meeting statement. The "strategy" meetings will increase to two the following year, leaving just six opportunities to adjust rates — with the exception of emergency meetings, if necessary.

"For consumers, that could mean longer periods of uncertainty around borrowing costs," said Jordan Ricciardi, a senior wealth strategy associate at UBS. "Our clients looking to buy a home, refinance a mortgage or finance a business could be impacted as fewer scheduled opportunities means the Fed isn't as quickly responding to changing economic conditions."

With fewer scheduled opportunities to adjust rates, that means borrowing rates could remain stubbornly high even if economic conditions begin to improve, Post Oak Group's Vrachas also said.

As it stands, financial institutions can adjust rates whenever market conditions warrant, regardless of the Fed's meeting schedule, he said. In the meantime, though, many consumers may continue to face elevated borrowing costs for mortgages, car loans and credit card debt, among other financial products.

"For consumers, the bigger concern isn't necessarily fewer meetings, but whether lenders pass along lower borrowing costs as quickly as they pass along increases," Vrachas said.

Consumers may be even more impacted by the lack of transparency, economists say, as Warsh implements measures to curtail so-called forward guidance, or how the Fed signals its future rate moves.

Although economic conditions have become more complicated and information is more widely available, "Warsh keeps his proverbial cards closer to his chest than his recent predecessors have," said Mark Hamrick, an economic analyst and founder of The Hamrick Brief, a weekly newsletter.

"I wouldn't expect consumers to see the universe of market rates change simply because the Fed votes less often," he said. "But the public may have less opportunity to hear from the source about how and why the Fed is making the decisions that shape what they pay to borrow and what they earn on their savings."

In the interim, "the best protection for consumers remains shopping around, particularly for the highest yields among savings accounts and [certificates of deposit]," Hamrick said.

As long as the Fed keeps rates unchanged, some top-yielding online savings accounts are offering interest rates as high as 4% or more, Bankrate data shows. "That's one of the upsides of an elevated interest rate environment: savers can earn meaningfully higher yields," Hamrick said.

Got a confidential news tip? We want to hear from you.

#3018. Southern Copper: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Expanding milling capacity by a third while pursuing a 1 million-ton production target hinges on execution of regional permitting and cost control.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 22, 2026, Southern Copper (SCCO +1.03%) reported record net sales and net income while outlining plans to push annual production past 1 million tons. Structural cost advantages and substantial organic growth projects position the miner to capitalize on global demand for electrification. Three things management said stand out.

Selling secondary minerals like molybdenum, silver, and zinc provides revenue offsets known as by-product credits, which dramatically reduce net mining expenses. When metal prices rise, these credits insulate operations against underlying inflation in fuel, power, and labor.

Organic mine development expands refined output without requiring expensive corporate acquisitions. Bringing projects like Tia Maria in Peru and El Pilar in Mexico online expands long-term volume capacity and lifts overall asset returns.

Increasing daily processing throughput at existing sites allows miners to extract more valuable metal from existing reserves. Upgrading infrastructure at the Cuajone facility in Peru strengthens long-term operational scale at relatively low incremental capital expense.

Copper demand remains structurally supported by global industrial consumption, and executive messaging radiates quiet confidence in the face of temporary operational friction. Large-scale low-cost reserves, high-grade by-product credits, and an extensive pipeline of fully owned development projects give the business distinct competitive moats. Leadership is keeping a close watch on regional project execution, regulatory permitting steps, and cost control before committing final capital to larger expansions.

Capturing downstream margin through internal refining capacity keeps processing value inside the corporate balance sheet. Rather than relying solely on raw metal sales, expanding domestic processing facilities shields operations from volatile international treatment fees while securing reliable customer pricing power.

The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3017. 2026-27 NBA Power Rankings Week 3: Houston takes over No. 1; Boston, Memphis leap into top 5

As we often see early in the season, there are big moves up and down the rankings this week as teams settle in (and schedules start to balance out). Unfortunately, injuries are knocking too many teams down the ladder.

Record: 6-1Ranking last week: No. 3Games this week: Boston, Minnesota, at Phoenix

Last season, Houston's offensive spacing was often a mess. This season, with the return of Fred VanVleet and moving Amen Thompson to more of an off-ball role, everything has looked much better — Houston has a 121.1 offensive rating, best in the league. VanVleet said this week that he has asked to play more minutes next to Reed Sheppard, something we have seen for just 13 minutes so far this season. The Rockets, unsurprisingly, picked up their fourth-year option on Sheppard this week, keeping him with the team through 2028, and he is extension-eligible this summer if the sides can agree on a number.

Record: 5-1Ranking last week: No. 2Games this week: at Charlotte, Philadelphia, Los Angeles Lakers

Detroit's front office wanted to add shooting this offseason, and we're seeing the impact on the court: The Pistons are taking six more 3-pointers a game and hitting them at a higher percentage, 36.3% this season. Amazingly, they are shooting 39.2% on above-the-break 3s. Jalen Duren remains out with a hamstring strain, but so far Paul Reed (13.8 points and 7 rebounds a game, shooting 63%) and John Collins (9.2 points, 7.2 rebounds) have stepped up and thrived next to Cade Cunningham.

Record: 6-1Ranking last week: No. 5Games this week: Chicago, at Detroit, at Cleveland, at Chicago

The Philadelphia 76ers keep getting off to slow starts — they have a -15.9 net rating in the first quarter — then somehow climbing out of the hole to get the win. Through seven games, the 76ers have a +20.9 net rating in the fourth quarter (second best in the league). That manifests in things such as the 13-0 run late against Charlotte to secure the win this past week. This feels like a team winning on its impressive talent so far but without the intentionality and focus of a great team, especially on offense, something that will either come with time or catch up to them. Right now, take the wins, bank them, and don't ask questions.

Record: 4-1Ranking last week: No. 9Games this week: at Houston, Milwaukee, at Charlotte, at Milwaukee

Jayson Tatum is making a lot of headlines for his not-beef-but-lamb-with-gravy back-and-forth with Jalen Brown, easily the most annoying storyline of the early season. Look past that, and he's played fantastic ball to start the season, averaging 28.3 points, 6 rebounds, and 7.7 assists a game. His 3-point shot is still finding its range (24.4%), but he's looking a lot like the All-NBA, MVP-ballot consideration kind of player he was before his Achilles injury. Boston takes to the road now, with five of its next six away from TD Garden, starting with a tough one Monday night against the Rockets.

Record: 6-0Ranking last week: No. 17Games this week: New Orleans, Indiana, Oklahoma City

The last undefeated team in the NBA, and while maybe that came against a soft schedule (which continues until OKC on Friday), you have to beat the teams in front of you, and that's exactly what the Grizzlies have done. Convincingly. Ty Jerome and Cameron Boozer are grabbing the headlines for their scoring, but two guys going under the radar but making critical contributions are showing up in other stats: Cedric Coward and Zach Edey are two of the top three in +/- on the team so far this season. Boozer became the second-youngest player to record a triple-double when he did it in his fifth NBA game last Thursday.

Record: 5-2Ranking last week: No. 6Games this week: Miami, at Sacramento, Portland, at Detroit

Austin Reaves is living up to his massive offseason contract: 23.8 points and 5.3 assists a game, shooting 42.4% from 3-point range. The Lakers offense looks special, as expected. On the other end of the court, the defensive drop-off from Walker Kessler to anyone else at the five is steep, but the Lakers' defensive issues are bigger than the backup big — they can't contain anyone on the perimeter. Everything starts there. That said, Kevon Looney has seemingly fallen out of the rotation, with Jared Vanderbilt and Sandro Mamukelashvili getting some run as the backup five.

Record: 4-2Ranking last week: No. 8Games this week: Utah, Dallas, at Sacramento, at Portland

San Antonio has found its footing somewhat after a slow start, and now boasts a top-10 offense and defense. And it has done that without the Spurs' big off-season acquisition, Tobias Harris, who is nearing a return but there is no official timetable. The Spurs have stretches where they look like the best team in the league: Defending with ferocity, moving the ball, and getting easy buckets around the rim. Then the next quarter they fall in love with isolation basketball and things go cold, which happened too much against Minnesota on Halloween, but they got bailed out by 43 points from Victor "The Witcher" Wembanyama.

Record: 4-2Ranking last week: No. 11Games this week: Oklahoma City, at Oklahoma City, Philadelphia, New York

Evan Mobley is taking and making his 3-pointers this season, averaging a career-high 4.3 attempts a game and hitting 57.7% through six games — when he is out spacing the floor like that, and with the addition of Peyton Watson on the wing, the Cavaliers' offense looks fantastic. We know who Donovan Mitchell is, we know exactly who James Harden is, but if this team is taking a step forward, it's on Mobley and Watson, and this has been a promising start. Check it out for yourselves Monday night, exclusively on Peacock, when the Cavaliers take on the Thunder. That's the start of a brutal week for the Cavs with two games against the (banged up but still dangerous) Thunder, plus the 76ers and Knicks.

Record: 4-1Ranking last week: No. 16Games this week: at Denver, at Phoenix, at Los Angeles Lakers, San Antonio

Ja Morant returned to Memphis last Thursday for the first time since being traded and it was a bit of a letdown because he was forced to exit early due to a left hamstring injury, which is going to keep him out at least a month. That injury feels like it throws cold water on one of the best starts and best stories of the young NBA season. What's been impressive about Morant and the Trail Blazers as a whole is how everyone has bought into the system — they are all passing, driving and kicking out, getting into good positions. "The buy-in has been super, and I really didn't have to do a whole lot," new head coach Micah Nori said. "I wish I could tell you that there was some secret sauce where I was coaching my ass off as far as getting them to buy in. But just as human beings, they're just really good people. They want to win. They know they're going to play. They know their roles, and so far... the buy-in has been wonderful for all."

Record: 4-2Ranking last week: No. 21Games this week: Sacramento, at San Antonio, Utah, Brooklyn

The Mavericks are the giant killers, knocking off the undefeated Thunder and Rockets in the same week, which is one reason they are jumping up the rankings 11 spaces to No. 10. The other reason is that Cooper Flagg has taken a big step forward off his Rookie of the Year season and looks like an All-NBA player averaging 26.8 points, 6.6 assists and six rebounds a game, plus 2.6 steals a game on the defensive end (fourth best in the league) — his second-season numbers are comparable to LeBron James second-season (both age 20) numbers. Flagg has a long way to go to really earn LeBron comps, but early on this season he has been that good.

Record: 4-2Ranking last week: No. 1Games this week: at Cleveland, Cleveland, at Memphis, Washington

That was as rough a week as any team may ever have had. Despite the offseason trades, Oklahoma City had great depth, but now it's being tested with Jalen Williams and Cason Wallace out for most (if not all) of the season, and Chet Holmgren missing at least two months with a patellar tendon tear. For Wallace's role, Ajay Mitchell steps right in and he dropped 30 in the Thunder's win over the Suns, he's a better offensive player but not the level of defender that Wallace is. A combination of Isaiah Hartenstein, Kenrich Williams, Aday Mara and Jaylin Williams will step in. Williams is the toughest to replace; there aren't other guys like him. Trading away Lu Dort now comes back to haunt them more, because he was the guy defensively who could step in for Williams. Now they don't have that guy and who covers an Anthony Edwards or Luka Dončić in the playoffs? A great stat from Ben Golliver of ESPN sums up how different things are in OKC this season: Five of the top six players in total minutes from the 2024 championship run are no longer with the team this season (due to injuries or being traded away).

Record: 4-3Ranking last week: No. 28Games this week: Phoenix, Miami, Orlando

Winners of four straight after dropping the Warriors dropped their first three. Wild stat: Curry and Draymond Green are in their 15th season as a duo, tying them for the third-longest-tenured duo in NBA history (John Stockton and Karl Malone spent 18 years together in Utah; Tony Parker and Manu Ginobili were teammates for 16 years in San Antonio). #3016. Why RH Stock Jumped 20% in October

Investor sentiment is shifting toward housing stocks.

Shares of RH (RH -0.10%), the high-end home-furnishing company formerly known as Restoration Hardware, were moving higher last month on a broad upswing in housing stocks.

Interest rates started to edge lower. The Federal Reserve declined to raise the benchmark Federal funds rate, and investors rotated out of AI stocks, landing on housing stocks as one of several potential opportunities. RH also seemed to benefit from a short squeeze, as the stock had become heavily shorted after several quarters of sluggish growth. As of Oct. 15, 58% of the float was sold short.

There was little company-specific news out on RH last month, but the rally in the housing sector was clear, and it was enough to lift the stock 20%, according to data from S&P Global Market Intelligence.

As you can see from the chart below, RH stock started moving late in the month as interest rates fell. Notably, the stock also moved up as the S&P 500 went down, showing what appeared to be a rotation out of AI stocks and into the housing sector.

Starting on Oct. 20, a combination of a short squeeze and a broader rotation into housing stocks seemed to lift RH shares. The stock jumped 5% on Oct. 20, another 7% on Oct. 21 on high volume, and then popped 9.4% on its highest daily volume since its earnings report on Oct. 22, indicating a possible short squeeze. After a brief pullback, the stock rallied again into the end of the month.

On Oct. 22, the yield on the 10-year Treasury note also fell sharply, closing at 4.95%, down 3.8% and its first close below 5% in a month. That move was a response to fears about AI spending, seen in Meta's first quarter of negative free cash flow in its earnings report the night before. It also signaled a rotation out of AI stocks and into bonds.

That pattern continued through the end of the month as tech stocks and Treasury yields fell and RH stock moved higher.

RH delivered better-than-expected results in its most recent earnings report as the company now sees full-year revenue growth of 5.5%-7%, even as it continues to open new galleries and pushes forward in Europe.

It will take a significant recovery in the housing market to accelerate the company's growth and boost its margins, but that will come at some point. While the Fed is not expected to lower rates, market dynamics change quickly, and investors seem to think the housing sector has rebound potential.

Investors will have to be patient with RH stock, but if momentum returns to the business, it has a lot of upside potential.

Jeremy Bowman has positions in RH. The Motley Fool recommends RH. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3015. Why GE Vernova Stock Crept Higher on Monday

It was mentioned positively in a new research note on AI infrastructure stocks.

GE Vernova stock bucked the market's general downward trend as the trading week kicked off. Its shares ended Monday slightly higher, comparing favorably to the marginal decline of the benchmark S&P 500 index. A key reason for this was its inclusion in a researcher's list of top artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure stock picks.

That researcher is Bernstein SocGen, which published a report that morning that addressed the state of the U.S. AI data center build-out. While demand remains strong for increased AI compute, Bernstein feels that 55% of the present data center pipeline could come under political pressure after the midterm elections, according to reports. In its view, certain gubernatorial candidates that favor limits on such developments, in line with some segments of their electorates, could curb the progress of numerous projects.

That said, Bernstein believes that the significant opportunities for economic growth from data center projects could outweigh such considerations -- and politicians will, if need be, find ways to secure funding without incurring too much constituent ire. The financial services company flagged several companies that stand to benefit from this dynamic, and GE Vernova was one of them.

In fact, the company is its No. 1 pick in alternative energy and technology for this stage of the AI revolution. That's hardly surprising, as GE Vernova has established itself as a steady and reliable supplier of crucial 24/7 power systems and related services.

One crucial piece of evidence supporting this is GE Vernova's rapidly growing backlog, which, one analyst recently estimated, could easily balloon to $200 billion next year. That's a staggering amount, even for a company of this scale, and I think it's more than sufficient to drive growth, no matter how high the political pressure gauge rises.

Eric Volkman has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends GE Vernova. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3014. Milwaukee Bucks vs. Minnesota Timberwolves Preview & Game Thread: Let’s get serious

The Milwaukee Bucks play their seventh game of the year tonight against the Minnesota Timberwolves, a squad widely seen as one of the Western Conference's best before the season began, but one that has had anything but a smooth start to the year, sitting just above .500. Of course, that bumpy ride is set to continue with the news that they will be without Anthony Edwards for about two months while he undergoes knee surgery. Although that news is obviously very bad for the Wolves—and you never wish injury on anyone—this opens the door for the Bucks in a game they otherwise would have had almost no shot at winning. These two teams played in the preseason, and the Wolves waxed the Bucks with little effort—hopefully that won't be repeated tonight.

The Timberwolves, after their offseason reshuffle, are still adjusting. After three scintillating opening wins from Minnesota—a 25-point win over Miami, a 22-point blowout over Orlando, and a 23-point win over Toronto—they fell flat in an overtime thriller against Cleveland, marked by turnover issues and late-game execution struggles. After the loss, news came out that Anthony Edwards would undergo knee surgery that would keep him off the court for up to two months.

Since Edwards has been out, the Timberwolves have gone 1-2, including a 121-111 loss to the San Antonio Spurs where new addition LaMelo Ball shot 2/10 and managed just 10 points in 20 minutes. Ball has had many exciting moments in Minny, but also some frustrating ones; his foul trouble and playmaking inconsistency have been issues at times. Crucially, Rudy Gobert has also started slow, and a lack of size outside Gobert—along with Donte DiVincenzo's absence—has contributed to an inconsistent seven games so far. Minnesota currently sits in 10th place in the West at 4-3, and they'll want to regain momentum heading into a stacked November.

Rudy Gobert has drawn online mockery after a rough start to the season. The 7'1" center has been in a shooting slump, going at just 41.2% from the field and sub-50% from the charity stripe; he even went 0/13 across a two-game stretch! Sure, Gobert has never been the most complete offensive player, but he's been flat-out inefficient on shots he usually makes, shooting just 48% on rim attempts. On the other hand, Gobert remains an elite rebounder (averaging north of 12 boards per contest) and one of the league's most intimidating defenders.

The matchup between Gobert and Myles Turner—another struggling center—will be very intriguing. Of course, the two have history, having got into an altercation in 2021 when Gobert played for the Jazz and Turner played for the Pacers. Keeping Gobert from finding an offensive rhythm and drawing him out of the key on defence will be crucial in the Bucks' attempt to secure their second win of the season.

Who: Milwaukee Bucks (1-5) vs. Minnesota Timberwolves (4-3).

Ousmane Dieng scores more than 10 points for the first time this season. He's gotten off to a slow start, averaging 2.4 PPG, 1.2 RPG, 1.2 APG on .422/.311/.716 shooting splits in just 11.8 minutes per night. However, this slump may be temporary, and despite Milwaukee's lockdown defense and standout wings, I sense a Dieng resurgence.

#3011. Game Preview #8: Wolves vs. Bucks Game Thread

On October 5th, the Minnesota Timberwolves played a preseason game against the Milwaukee Bucks in which the Wolves won by 19, behind a perfect 5/5 shooting night from Antonio Reeves, who is unfortunately no longer a member of the team.

Now, just under a month later, the Wolves will look to bounce back from a Halloween night loss to the San Antonio Spurs by looking to take out what is expected to be one of the worst teams in the East. Even if Minnesota is without Anthony Edwards and Rudy Gobert, they have more than enough talent to keep themselves above the 500 mark.

Let's get straight into some things to look out for, and some predictions, for Monday night's game.

Who: Minnesota Timberwolves (4-3) vs Milwaukee Bucks (1-5)

If you missed the Timberwolves Halloween hosing by the refs on October 31st, I don't blame you! You would've been better off enjoying some candy or horror movies – not my thing, but certainly someone's – than spending the evening watching as the officiating simply moved further and further in San Antonio's favor in a night in which LaMelo Ball fouled out.

However, to act like fouling hasn't been a concern for this Wolves team so far would be simply disingenuous and wrong.

Of all the Wolves' issues, from the injuries to the offensive inconsistency and the fit issues between Ball and Gobert, the biggest one fully in this team's control is the positions they put themselves in with the referees. Part of the blame can be on the zebras (in Ant's words), most of it must be on the players.

Especially on LaMelo Ball and Jaden McDaniels.

The Wolves have six different players (Ball, Gobert, Edwards, McDaniels, Ayo Dosunmu, Jonathan Kuminga) averaging over two fouls a game through only seven contests. While that pace will surely balance out as the season goes along, it's still quite a lot of fouls to be committing.

Beyond the 24 points-per-game in free throws those penalties could allow (not an exact science), foul trouble has kept many of the Wolves' most important players out of the game when the team needs help.

Even if the Bucks look like a bad team on paper, the Wolves aren't much better than them without two of their top three players and even more valuable pieces warming the bench because they antagonized the refs. Avoiding fouling will be paramount to getting back on track this season.

The Wolves have now played five games in seven days, including an overtime game against the Cleveland Cavaliers in the second game of that stretch.

Safe to say they are exhausted and in need of rest they are not going to get. Despite that, Minnesota is scheduled for this match against the Bucks just two days after their second back-to-back in five days. This is just a brutal set of games for any team to go through, and the Wolves being forced to do so without their two most floor-raising players means bad things for fans hoping for 50 wins.

Exhaustion is often underemphasized in the NBA. Stamina in games is properly accounted for, but stamina in seasons is not.

With how many star players have gotten hurt lately, maybe that's bound to change.

The NBA season is a marathon, not a sprint, but the Wolves have started their season with an obstacle course and ankle weights. To survive this stretch, maybe playing some deeper bench options in the hopes that they can simply eat innings, let alone help win games, could be a smart choice for head coach Chris Finch.

Either way, with only three games in the next weeklong span, the Wolves should slowly be able to catch their breath.

So far this season, Wolves fans have experienced the highs and lows of what LaMelo Ball can be.

To start the year, his fit with Anthony Edwards created gaudy scoring numbers and exciting highlights as the roster purred in sync, along with truly glorious crowd pops that only Target Center and maybe a handful of other arenas can produce.

However, over the past handful of games, LaMelo has been far less consistent and far more deserving of criticism. Last game, his fouling, although it was certainly exaggerated and embellished by the Spurs, left the Wolves without their offensive engine as San Antonio ran away with the win.

The highs have always been there for Ball. It's why he won Rookie of the Year over Ant in a year in which LaMelo was more exciting off the jump, while Edwards proved to be more consistent over a long season.

Stability, though, is what the Wolves need from him now. Without Ant present, Ball needs to be the offensive fulcrum he was for the Charlotte Hornets last year, in place of the organiser and maestro he's been to date in Minnesota.

Against the Bucks, who lack any particularly amazing defensive players, LaMelo should be dominating the ball and the opponents en route to a Wolves win.

If the Wolves do not win this game, I will be hiding in a bunker as Wolves Twitter proceeds to light the world on fire with takes about the recently benched Naz Reid and the worries of that 2033 first-round pick, which might not even matter if an asteroid hits the Earth or sports betting continues to burn at the fabric of league optics and fan interest.

Either way, Minnesota should take this one comfortably.

UPDATE: LaMelo Ball and Terrence Shannon Jr. have popped up on the injury report and are both questionable tonight.

#3006. Hawks face their own injury scares as NBA absences pile up

The Atlanta Hawks entered Saturday's game against the New Orleans Pelicans hoping to avoid the significant injuries already disrupting teams across the NBA. They finished it facing uncertainty surrounding two important contributors.

Jalen Johnson exited with a left ankle injury, while Jock Landale left with a left hip injury. Neither returned to the game, leaving Atlanta awaiting more clarity on both players.

Their departures brought an uncomfortable league-wide storyline directly into focus for the Hawks. Anthony Edwards, Kawhi Leonard, Jalen Williams, Chet Holmgren, Paolo Banchero, Jaren Jackson Jr. and Ja Morant are among the notable players sidelined early this season. Before teams have reached even 10 games, availability has already become a major concern.

Now Atlanta has its own injuries to monitor. Neither player's absence from the remainder of Saturday's game establishes how much additional time, if any, he will miss. Still, seeing Johnson and Landale leave the same game creates understandable concern for a team trying to find its footing.

The Hawks know how quickly an injury can change their outlook. During the 2024–25 season, Johnson suffered a torn labrum in his left shoulder that required season-ending surgery. That setback interrupted his development and forced Atlanta to adjust without one of its most important players.

Saturday's ankle injury should not be equated with that previous diagnosis. However, the memory reinforces why Johnson's availability carries so much weight. His scoring, rebounding and playmaking influence nearly every part of Atlanta's offense, making his presence difficult to replace through a simple lineup change.

Landale's situation also matters. He provides an experienced option behind Onyeka Okongwu, giving Quin Snyder another interior presence and a different offensive look. Any absence would test a frontcourt already operating without Henri Veesaar and Mouhamed Gueye.

Atlanta added depth this offseason, but losing multiple contributors at the same position would stretch those resources. Depending on the updates, other frontcourt players could be asked to take on greater responsibilities sooner than anticipated.

That uncertainty arrives during a season in which the Hawks hope to build on last year and respond to their disappointing playoff exit against the Knicks. Developing chemistry and establishing consistent rotations become harder when injuries force changes before those combinations have settled.

Injuries are an unavoidable part of basketball, and no amount of roster planning can eliminate them. Depth can help a team navigate absences, but keeping its core available remains essential to understanding what the group can accomplish.

For now, the Hawks' immediate concern is learning more about Johnson and Landale. What began as a hope that Atlanta could avoid the NBA's growing injury wave has become a much more immediate question about the health of its own roster.

#3004. How Yaxel Lendeborg joined Warriors icons in franchise history vs. Clippers

How Yaxel Lendeborg joined Warriors icons in franchise history vs. Clippers originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Yaxel Lendeborg has given the Warriors a youthful jolt of energy, and he has entered some rare company through seven games.

In the absence of Steph Curry and Draymond Green, Lendeborg took complete charge of the offense during Sunday's 124-112 win over the Los Angeles Clippers.

The No. 11 overall selection in the 2026 NBA Draft scored a career-high 37 points with six made 3-pointers to lead Golden State to its fourth consecutive win.

The outburst pushed Lendeborg's numbers on the season to 22.3 points per game on 52-percent shooting from the field and 40.9 percent from distance as the Warriors' second-leading scorer.

Lendeborg became the third player in franchise history to average over 20 points per game through his first seven games, joining Warriors legends Wilt Chamberlain and Rick Barry.

His 37 points also were the most scored by a rookie since Curry's 42 in a 122-116 win over the Portland Trail Blazers in April 2010.

While Curry finished second in the Rookie of the Year race in the 2009-10 season to the Sacramento Kings' Tyreke Evans, both Chamberlain and Barry won the award.

Chamberlain was the first player in NBA history to win MVP as a rookie when he did so in the 1959-60 season, and Barry made First-Team All-NBA as a rookie in 1965-66, although the number of teams at the time was considerably smaller.

The numbers do not just compare against franchise legends, but also against the current crop of NBA stars.

Lendeborg is one of two rookies to rank top 25 in the NBA in scoring this season, alongside Sacramento Kings guard Darius Acuff Jr.

Beyond the scoring, Lendeborg has had an impressive all-around impact. His 7.3 rebounds per game leads the Warriors, and coach Steve Kerr frequently has used him as a playmaker in the Warriors' offense.

Lendeborg's consistency has been on full display, with 25-plus points, five or more assists and nine or more rebounds in four games.

The rookie's play is making headlines, but his personality only has amplified his popularity. Lendeborg said he "lost all respect" for Los Angeles Lakers superstar Luka Dončić after the Warriors' 127-105 opening-day loss, and promptly refused to get media trained.

After a lackluster start to the season with three straight losses, Lendeborg has been a huge reason for the Warriors' turnaround, and he will continue to be featured if the Warriors are to maintain this momentum until Jimmy Butler and Moses Moody return.

#3002. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#3000. 10 Takeaways from Celtics gritty win over the Magic

Following a 4-1 month of October, the Boston Celtics opened up November with their first road win of the season, taking down the Orlando Magic 108-97. This extends their winning streak to 5 games, tying their longest winning streak from last season.

In Halloween fashion, the Magic gave Boston a scare after coming back from a 15-point lead the Celtics held midway through the third quarter. However, some clutch shot making by Jayson Tatum and Paul George down the stretch of the fourth quarter allowed Boston to escape the house of Disney World with a victory.

Jayson Tatum had a great first quarter, finishing with 8 points on 3-6 shooting but at the end of the quarter he was hit in the head, resulting in a cut that saw him bleeding and having to go back to the locker room. Much like Rambo saying, "they drew first blood, not me," Tatum finished the game with another spectacular performance, finishing with 32 points, 7 assists, 4 rebounds, and 2 blocks on 8-19 shooting from the field, 1-5 from three and 15-17 from the free throw line.

A bad third quarter made Tatum's final numbers look worse than it was but the thing that impressed me the most was his ability to get to the free throw line. We have seen Tatum already set a new career high in free throws attempted against the Nets on Tuesday and with 17 more in this game, he became the first Celtic since Paul Pierce in 2008 to attempt 54 free throws in a 4 game span according to Dick Lipe. It feels like he has become a lot less reliant on the three-point shot and is just bullying his way to the basket on most of his possessions.

Tatum's defense was a stand out as well. There were multiple possessions where the Magic decided to go one-on-one against Tatum and he stood his ground. He was fighting around screens and did a great job on constantly putting pressure on Orlando's ball handlers. It really is just a pleasure to watch him on the court, further cementing the fact that he is one of, if not the best two-way player in the game.

When clutch time hits, there is also no one I would rather have than Jayson Tatum on the floor controlling the offense. After a long stretch off to start the fourth quarter, he came in and immediately hit two pull-up jumpers to build Boston's lead back up to 8 points. Tatum is now averaging 31.4 PPG, 6.4 RPG, and 7.0 APG on 61% TS% in his last 5 games and looks prime for a run at potentially an MVP.

We saw a change to the starting lineup for the first time this season as Joe Mazzulla decided to start Hugo Gonzalez in the place of Jordan Walsh. Gonzalez held his own in 14 minutes of play, finishing with 7 points, and 2 steals on 3-5 shooting from the field and 1-3 from three.

All of Gonzalez's points came in the first quarter but he looked to be holding his own on the offensive end. He hit a three and had a couple nice finishes in the paint. When it came to his work on the glass, I feel like the best way to describe it is the epitome of a bulldozer. He was able to clear out space a ton and it opened up the Celtics to get more offensive rebounds.

I hope we get to see more of Hugo Gonzalez in the starting lineup moving forward. Even in the limited sample size he was on the court, he looked comfortable amongst the rest of the Celtics starters and specifically thrived next to Jayson Tatum. The idea of Walsh as a starter isn't bad, but I feel like Gonzalez can do pretty similar things to him and might have a little bit more upside.

Although Grand Theft Auto VI is going to be based in Florida, the Celtics played their own version of the game when it came to their defense against the Magic. Boston hounded Orlando into 16 turnovers, 9 steals, and 7 blocks on the game, holding them to only 97 points.

The Celtics set the tone early in this game, forcing the Magic into 8 turnovers in the first quarter alone. Although it could be chalked up to sloppy play by Orlando, Boston's defense was a big factor and it helped them get out from an early deficit after the Magic started the game on a 13-4 run. The defense came up huge again in the fourth quarter, as they held Orlando to 7-24 (29.2%) shooting from the field and 2-9 (22.2%) shooting from three. A gritty effort by the Boston defense.

We all know Boston is known for their three point shooting and we also know the Celtics have shot themselves out of games in the past when they are missing from beyond the arc. However, we saw the Celtics shoot 12-44 (27.3%) from three in this game against the Magic and still came out with the victory. This was due in part to the fact they shot 25-40 (62.5%) from inside the arc and more specifically shot 12-16 (75%) at the basket.

It's good to see Boston have some more variety when the threes aren't falling. It is a huge help that Jayson Tatum and Paul George are proficient when it comes to shooting in the midrange and it will make them all the more dynamic of an offense this season.

Hugo Gonzalez wasn't the only the only change to the starting lineup ahead of today's game, as Mitchell Robinson was ruled OUT do to rest since the Celtics are playing a back-to-back. This gave us a chance to see Neemias Queta back in the starting lineup and he was able to show out a little more. He finished with 8 points, 8 rebounds, and 2 blocks on 4-9 shooting.

The first half saw Queta showcase his talents on the offensive end, rolling to the basket and finishing with monster slams or nifty reverse layups. The second half is where we saw his defense come in to play, specifically in the fourth quarter. Queta had 2 huge blocks in the final two minutes of play that the Celtics up to hit the dagger and put the game out of reach.

I hope we get to see Queta in the starting lineup more. He has been fine coming off the bench this year but Mitchell Robinson hasn't really impressed in the starting role so far. Queta was a great starter last year and has great chemistry with Tatum. I feel like Boston could go either way with the starting center but as of now it feels like Robinson would be better suited for a bench role.

If you were watching this game with the Orlando Magic's broadcast team, you would have heard them call Luka Garza the "Magic Killer" because every time he sees their shade of blue he has a big game. That is a true statement after last year where in the final game of the season he finished with 27 points and 12 rebounds on 10-18 shooting including the game winning three. Garza was once again a force against Orlando in this matchup as the Celtics second string center, finishing with 12 points, 6 rebounds, and 4 assists on 4-7 shooting from the field and 2-4 from three.

Garza is known for turning into the "Garzilla" when it comes dominating the offensive glass but it was his three point shooting that was the biggest factor in this game. He hit his first three in the second quarter to end a 1-15 drought to begin the game for Boston and his second came in the fourth quarter during a big Celtics run. Garza played some huge minutes as the only backup center Boston played in this game and just continues to be a consistent presence off the bench.

One thing that has jumped out to me about the Celtics offense this year is the amount of assists that they are able to rack up. In this game against the Magic they finished with 29 and the ball movement was beautiful to watch. Even with the shots not falling in, Boston was able set up their teammates with a lot of wide open opportunities and they kept Orlando's defense on their heels all game.

However, a stat that the NBC Sports Boston broadcast brought up was the fact that the Celtics have tallied 25+ assists in all six of their games this season. It is the longest streak in franchise history and is a testament to the way Boston has been playing. The unselfish brand of basketball the Celtics have been known for is flowing to start this season, with Boston ranking tied for 4th in assists per game as a team at 28.0. They are also ranked 9th in the league with a 1.83 Assist/Turnover rate.

Going into the fourth quarter, the Celtics led the Magic 77-74 with Jayson Tatum scoring 11 points in the third. Tatum was going to sit to begin the fourth quarter and Boston was going to have to fill some big minutes without their best player on the floor. In that time, the lineup of Payton Pritchard, Derrick White, Baylor Scheierman, Paul George, and Luka Garza weathered the storm.

They outscored Orlando 20-17 with Tatum on the bench including an 18-6 run that gave them a 12 point lead with 5:02 remaining. Tatum was set to come in after Paul George shot free throws but Joe Mazzulla decided to hold him back a little longer, trusting the group that had the momentum. It almost back fired entirely with Desmond Bane and Tristan da Silva hitting back to back threes to cut it to a 6 point game with 4:22 remaining, but I liked the sentiment.

Mazzulla was trying to reward the guys on the floor who put out a double digit lead and maybe it lasted a little too long, it was still a fine decision. It not only gave Tatum time to rest a little more given the Celtics were on the first half of a back-to-back, but it also allowed him to come in fresh and takeover the game. Loved what I saw out of that group and it gives me more confidence in them moving forward.

Paul George has been better than anyone could have hoped when it comes to the beginning of his Boston Celtics career. There are shades of Clippers PG on offense still apparent in his game now it came out in full force against the Magic. George finished with 17 points, 8 assists, 6 rebounds, 2 steals, and 2 blocks on 6-14 shooting from the field and 3-10 from three. However, in the fourth quarter he turned it on the fourth quarter, scoring 10 points on 3-4 shooting from the field and 2-3 shooting from three, including the dagger with 1:32 remaining to give the Celtics the win.

George was the main facilitator of the Non-Tatum minutes and has thrived in that role throughout this season. Via databallr, the Celtics have a +25 net rating when Paul George is on the court without Tatum including an offensive rating of 138.8 per 100 possessions. Those are just the basic numbers without anything filtered so there could be an outlier there but those are still some pretty great numbers. George continues to impress me every game he plays with Boston and he fit into this system about as perfect as anyone could have imagined.

There always seems to be something off anytime the Celtics play against the Magic in Orlando. Every time they play in the Kia Center Boston and Orlando always seem to have a heavy weight match that ends in a pretty close contest. The Celtics were 1-4 playing in that arena over the last seasons with the one win coming last season so there was bound to be something going on today.

Even with the gods of the Kia Center working against them, Boston gutted out a win over a Magic team who is a lot better than people give them credit for. Desmond Bane and Tristan da Silva are legit flame throwers every time they play the Celtics and even though Franz Wagner had a bad game, he can also light you up. This game was the first of twelve road games that Boston is going to play in the month of November, so I expect to see more gritty wins like this one as the Celtics World Tour across the country continues.

#2998. Defense, healthcare, crypto and AI: How the midterm election could affect stocks

Lockheed Martin gets about 70% of its sales from the U.S. government. Medicaid accounts for three-quarters of Molina Healthcare's premium revenue. Coinbase is waiting on Congress to finish writing the rules for cryptocurrency.

For some of the biggest U.S. companies, Washington is part of the business model. Now, the midterm election could change who holds the leverage.

A Democratic victory in either or both chambers of Congress would grant the party new leverage to investigate companies, challenge federal spending and demand legislative concessions. But their reach would have limits: President Donald Trump would retain his veto and broad authority over tariffs, immigration and federal regulation.

Historically, stocks have performed well under divided government. Under Republican presidents, the S&P 500 has gained an average of 23% in the year after midterms when Congress was split, compared with 16% when Democrats controlled both chambers, according to a Morgan Stanley analysis published Thursday. Analysts often attribute the pattern in part to congressional gridlock limiting sweeping policy changes.

Still, some analysts caution against assuming that pattern will repeat.

"We don't think that blueprint holds up this time around, and we still see plenty of reasons to be constructive on the U.S. equity market," said Ariana Salvatore, head of U.S. public policy research at Morgan Stanley.

For investors, the midterm election could reshape which businesses preserve their advantages in Washington, particularly in industries such as defense, healthcare, banking and artificial intelligence.

"There are some policy changes that might be market moving, at least for certain segments," said Jeff Buchbinder, chief equity strategist at LPL Financial.

Control of either the House or Senate would give Democrats committee gavels and subpoena power, allowing them to demand documents, compel testimony and haul executives before public hearings.

On Oct. 21, Sen. Richard Blumenthal, D-Conn., the top Democrat on the Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations, sought records tied to Trump's White House ballroom from donors including Cisco, Salesforce, ServiceNow and Kraken. Apple and Nvidia each committed $25 million to the project, according to records obtained by The Washington Post.

Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., has separately sought records involving tariff exclusions, including communications involving Apple's former CEO, Tim Cook, as Democrats investigate whether Trump used trade policy to reward political allies and donors.

While donations do not prove wrongdoing or favorable treatment, congressional probes still carry risks: subpoenas consume executive bandwidth, reveal internal communications and generate unpredictable news coverage. Few CEOs relish the idea of sitting at a witness table before lawmakers and television cameras.

"It creates volatility because you get a negative headline," said Monica Guerra, Morgan Stanley's head of policy and geopolitical strategy, who expects Democrats to cast a wide net for their probes.

Democrats have signaled interest in Trump family businesses, government contractors, firms tied to Donald Trump Jr.'s 1789 Capital, and companies associated with Elon Musk and the so-called Department of Government Efficiency.

Then there is the most basic power Congress has: deciding where the money goes.

Trump's 2025 tax cuts and spending law, known as One Big Beautiful Bill, is projected to reduce federal Medicaid spending by $911 billion through 2034, according to a KFF analysis of Congressional Budget Office estimates. KFF is a nonpartisan nonprofit that provides health policy information. Meanwhile, new work requirements for Medicaid enrollees are scheduled to take effect Jan. 1, or earlier in some states, while tighter state budgets could mean fewer people are covered and greater pressure on provider payments.

Molina Healthcare generated $32.2 billion, or 75% of its 2025 premium revenue, from Medicaid, according to the company's annual Securities and Exchange Commission filing. In the same filing, Molina warned that losing government contracts or failing to renew them on favorable terms could materially reduce premium revenue.

A Democratic-controlled chamber could not repeal the 2025 law on its own but could use must-pass budget negotiations to seek funding changes or delays to some cuts.

"They're probably going to push back on those Medicaid cuts," Buchbinder told CNBC, noting that preserving coverage could help stabilize enrollment for Medicaid insurers and patient volumes for hospitals.

Hospitals face their own squeeze: fewer insured patients can mean more uncompensated care, while state budget pressure can reduce reimbursement rates, according to KFF. Congress created a $50 billion rural health fund over five years to cushion some of the blow, but KFF and medical providers have said the money would not fully offset the broader Medicaid reductions.

Democratic gains could still cut both ways for healthcare. The same lawmakers seeking to protect Medicaid funding could also intensify scrutiny of insurers, hospitals and drugmakers over prices, coverage and profits.

Defense contractors face a fight over which weapons and programs Congress keeps funding. The White House has requested $1.5 trillion for national defense in fiscal 2027, with a focus on replenishing weapons, bolstering readiness and covering other needs, some tied to the Iran war.

The White House is separately seeking an $87.6 billion supplemental appropriation from Congress for fiscal 2026, driven primarily by military operations related to Iran.

A Democratic Congress could challenge some Iran- and Israel-related spending while pushing for more aid to Ukraine. However, bipartisan consensus on China deterrence, nuclear modernization and missile defense could protect other major programs, according to multiple analysts.

For contractors, the difference could mean billions of dollars in orders.

Lockheed Martin, for example, recently reached a framework agreement with the Pentagon covering 850 F-35 fighter jets over a decade. But the agreement is not yet a finalized contract: Congress must authorize the multiyear purchase and provide the funding.

"You want to be a stock picker and not just ride the whole sector," Guerra told CNBC, pointing to areas such as shipbuilding, autonomous systems and missile defense as likely winners.

For crypto companies, the midterms could determine whether Trump's regulatory shift becomes durable law or remains vulnerable to the next administration. The industry's push for permanence stalled in September, when the Senate failed to advance the Trump-backed Clarity Act, a sweeping market structure bill.

Shares of Coinbase and stablecoin issuer Circle fell as much as 11% when the measure failed.

Getting a bill through the next Congress could come at a price. Democratic gains would give the party more leverage to demand stricter consumer safeguards, limits on yield-bearing stablecoins and tighter scrutiny of Trump's crypto businesses.

"In some ways, the industry doesn't really care who got elected," said Sam Stovall, chief investment strategist at CFRA Research. "They just want to know what the rules are going to be for the coming year."

Federal regulators estimate their proposed capital overhaul would cut requirements for the largest banks by roughly 4.8%, potentially freeing billions for lending and shareholder payouts. The Federal Reserve writes the rules, but Senate control shapes who fills future vacancies, and congressional committees can pressure regulators through hearings and investigations.

"It's at the regulatory level where stuff can happen that matters," Buchbinder said.

AI may be the messiest political trade of all.

Trump has made rapid data center construction an economic and national security priority. Morgan Stanley expects hyperscalers including Microsoft, Amazon, Alphabet and Meta to spend more than $1 trillion on capital projects in 2027, even as the buildout faces growing backlash over electricity bills, water use and infrastructure costs.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey from October found that 63% of registered voters oppose having a data center within 10 miles of their home, compared with just 13% who support one. The level of opposition is higher from Democrats than Republicans.

That friction has already reached Congress. In September, the House voted 417-3 to require state regulators to consider making large data centers cover the additional electricity infrastructure costs needed to serve them. The measure stalled in the Senate, 57-43 on a procedural vote, after Democrats argued it was too weak and pushed for mandatory protections instead of trusting the industry to police itself.

The midterms therefore could decide who gets the power. Democratic gains could put more grid-upgrade costs on tech developers, raising project costs just as access to power is constraining new construction.

But Congress is only part of the equation. State regulators, utilities and local governments will still decide where projects connect to the grid and which facilities get built.

"We think the election can have meaningful consequences, but on a highly localized basis," Salvatore said.
