#2993. 2026-27 NBA News, Injury Updates Nov. 2: Injuries hit Jalen Williams, Cason Wallace, Chet Holmgren in OKC

Welcome to our weekly Monday Morning NBA notebook, a quick hit of NBA news and injuries from around the league to catch you up on the past seven days. My opinion on the first two weeks of this season? I stand with Jeff Stotts (of InStreetClothes.com).

This was simply as bad a week as a team can have.

Jalen Williams suffered a dislocated left ankle and fractured fibula from a fall last Sunday against the Clippers. "This is an injury that generally we see more in football players," Thunder general manager Sam Presti said. In that same game, starting guard Cason Wallace left with a shoulder injury that turned out to be a torn labrum in his left shoulder, which will also require surgery. While the Thunder will not put a timeline on either player, it's unlikely either of them plays again this season.

Later in the week, the Thunder announced that Chet Holmgren underwent a "biological treatment today to address a small patellar tendon tear" and will be out 8-10 weeks. That likely puts his return around New Year's or in the first couple of weeks of 2027. (If you're wondering what a "biological treatment" is, a league source suggested it's platelet-rich plasma, PRP, therapy.)

While Holmgren will return, the injuries to Williams and Wallace diminish their title chances. Oklahoma City will still be very good behind two-time MVP Shai Gilgeous-Alexander and a deep roster. However, they go from a playoff team that can beat anyone regardless of style to one that will be more matchup-dependent (how do they stop elite wings such as Anthony Edwards or Luka Doncic in the West without Williams or Lu Dort, whom they traded away this summer for financial reasons).

What you shouldn't expect is Presti and OKC to panic into a trade to "fix" things. That's not how Presti operates, and he's been in this job 19 years he's not at risk of getting fired. The Thunder will go forward, see what their team looks like, who is reasonably available, then make a call.

Minnesota's Anthony Edwards underwent arthroscopic surgery on his ailing right knee and that could sideline him up to two months. Which sucks, Edwards had played at an MVP level to start the season, averaging 33.3 points, 4.8 rebounds and 3.5 assists per game while shooting 40% from 3-point range.

The positive spin for Timberwolves fans is that this is a proactive move by Edwards and the team: Get the work done now, have him back and healthy around Christmas, or heading into 2027. Looking at the landscape of the West this is a team with sky-high expectations but it needs that MVP-level Edwards to get there.

In the short term, Minnesota needs more of the Charlotte version of LaMelo Ball: One who takes on a larger scoring load and lifts up teammates. So far, Minnesota is 1-2 since Edwards went out.

Portland — and how well the Ja Morant and Damian Lillard backcourt was clicking — was one of the early-season stories. This news just threw cold water on it.

Morant left his return to Memphis early and the next day came the news that he would be out at least a month with a strained hamstring. Expect Jrue Holiday to move into the starting five, but the real guy to watch is Scoot Henderson, who is going to get a real chance after starting the season coming off the bench and not looking sharp (7.5 points per game on 34.4% shooting)

Morant looked the best he has in years to start the season, averaging 16.3 points and 6.3 assists per game while shooting 58.8% overall and 44.4% from beyond the arc. He had a more mature game.

The timing of this sucks: Kawhi Leonard will miss his one game this season at the Intuit Dome in Los Angeles against the Clippers.

Leonard is out due to swelling in his left knee and will be re-evaluated later this week, the team announced last week. Toronto said in its release that Leonard "will undergo a treatment and rehabilitation program to address swelling in his left knee. Medical imaging revealed no acute injury." Leonard said this on social media.

The reality is the Raptors knew what they were signing up for when they traded for Leonard, that he would miss time and there would be load management. Expect Leonard to miss a couple of weeks as the Raptors will keep him in bubble wrap this season.

After missing so much of last season, it's been great to see Tyrese Haliburton back on the court — except now we won't see him for at least a couple of weeks due to a left hamstring strain.

This is trouble for the Pacers, who are getting outscored by 10.4 points per 100 possessions when Haliburton is off the court this season, and they are now 0-3 since he went out after falling to previously winless Brooklyn on Sunday.

It's been a rough start to the season in Utah and this just piles on top of it: Jaren Jackson Jr. is out with a torn MCL in his right knee. The good news is that it doesn't require surgery. Utah says Jackson Jr. will be reevaluated in six weeks but expect him to miss closer to eight weeks,

Utah was going to roll out a big front line this season with Jusuf Nurkic, Jackson Jr. and Lauri Markkanen. Except we haven't seen it. Nurkic has yet to play due to a partial plantar plate tear in the second toe of his right foot, and now JJJ is out until Christmas or later.

Already without Paolo Banchero, more bad injury news for Orlando with the news guard Jalen Suggs is out with a right knee bone bruise and will be re-evaluated in two weeks. The good news is an MRI revealed no other structural damage.

Sean Sweeney, welcome to coaching Orlando.

• It's the end of an era. After 37 years, beloved host Ernie Johnson is stepping away from "Inside the NBA" after this season, he announced on X.

• We aren't done with the injury news: The Washington Wizards announced that forward Justin Champagnie underwent a surgical procedure to repair a fracture in his right foot, something he suffered during last week's game against Charlotte.

• Former NBA player Malik Beasley, who was indicted earlier this year for federal gambling crimes, will be changing his plea to guilty. Beasley was charged with manipulating his play in four games while he was with the Milwaukee Bucks during the 2023-24 season to help him pay off a loan after falling into debt

• Philadelphia applied for a disabled player exception due to Ariel Hukporti's season-ending Achilles tear. It's not much, it's just a $1.7 million exception (half of Hukporti's salary this season), but it's something they might use.

• Hall of Famer Paul Pierce is claiming he is "tapped out" financially in court documents in a case about support payments to an ex-girlfriend and the mother of one of his children.

#2986. Is Cooper Flagg already a top-10 player?

Is Cooper Flagg already a top-10 player at just 19 years old?

This season we've watched Flagg lead Dallas to wins while outplaying Shai Gilgeous-Alexander and Giannis Antetokounmpo, and he handed Houston its first loss behind a 33-point, 9-rebound, 6-assist effort.

In the final 100 seconds on Friday, he made two back-breaking shots that led the Mavericks to an NBA Cup victory:

First, Flagg drained an and-one floater that put Dallas up five points, and later he drove right and spun left into a heavily contested fallaway.

The floater is becoming a bit of a signature shot for Coop. Consider these three moments against Oklahoma City:

Here, the Mavericks run Flagg through a double screen on the left side of the floor, then he drives but is met at the rim by 7-foot-3 rookie center Aday Mara. So Flagg elevates and tosses up a soft floater.

Later on, Flagg brings the ball up himself and once again sees Mara inside so he tosses it up for another bucket.

The next play up the floor, Dallas runs a high pick-and-roll for Flagg with Daniel Gafford screening. Mara sees Flagg coming so he rotates over and contests hard. But that's not a floater — that's a lob to Gafford. The mere threat of a floater creates openings for his teammates, which puts the defensive big man in a dilemma: step up to stop the floater, and the same motion becomes a lob.

Flagg always had the floater in his bag. This year he's just making it more of a threat and he's improved his midrange pull-up. At Duke, Flagg made only 34% of his dribble jumpers from midrange. As a rookie in Dallas, he made 38%. So far this season, he's made 76%. That number is obviously unsustainable, but we'll see where it lands; Flagg has always had excellent touch as an 83% career free-throw shooter. The ability to make late-clock jumpers off the dribble at an efficient clip is the weapon that often separates All-NBA players from All-Stars, and right now Flagg has it.

Flagg still needs to grow in the 3-point department. He made 28% of his catch-and-shoot 3s and 30% of his dribble-jumper 3s as a rookie. Those numbers are pretty comparable so far in his second year. But given his month-to-month rate of improvement, it may only be a matter of time until the 3 is a knockdown shot for him. Maybe it'll even happen by the end of season.

Flagg is complementing that offensive growth with defense that is swinging games.

At 6-foot-10, Flagg is a Swiss Army knife who can defend across positions. You'll see him defend guards, and slide his feet to contain drives, he'll match up against big wings like Kevin Durant, and his coaching staff can feel comfortable with him battling against a true big.

Among the 100 players who have defended the most isolations since Flagg entered the NBA, Flagg ranks 13th in points allowed per play — one spot behind Stephon Castle and two spots behind Chet Holmgren, to put into perspective the company he's among.

Up 33 on the Pacers and defending Aaron Nesmith, Flagg attempts to steal the ball on the entry to Pascal Siakam before rotating right back over to steal it on the return. This starts a transition basket that ends with Nesmith clearly being a bit frustrated with a hard foul on Flagg that fortunately caused him to miss just a game.

And as good as Flagg is on the ball, he's an even better instinctive and disruptive help defender with quick reflexes to alter shots and swipe at the ball.

Flagg's best two-way sequence of the year came in the clutch against Miami:

With Dallas protecting a two-point lead, Miami pushes the ball up the floor and finds Andrew Wiggins seemingly open in the corner, but Flagg closes out hard, extends his right hand, and gets a fingertip on the shot to cause the airball. Flagg has always hustled like a madman on the floor, but he's also got great athleticism and instincts to actually make plays like this one.

Back up on the other end, Flagg crosses over to create space, drives hard, and gets his body by Wiggins before gathering the ball toward the rim as help arrives from 1-2-3 defenders. But look at his left-handed extension to evade them and preserve a finishing angle through the contest, turning a one-possession game into a four-point lead with 10 seconds left.

The ability to do this in traffic speaks to Flagg's dexterity, coordination, and improvements as a ball-handler. At lower levels, Flagg could always plow through defenders with hard straight-line drives. He has a freight-train finishing ability. But he didn't have great creativity off the bounce and he's progressed there to now use some wiggle and spin moves to get where he wants. On the year, he's making 69% of his layups, which is up from 52% as a rookie.

New Mavericks head coach Dusty May is designing actions to slingshot Flagg toward the basket. Sometimes to score on his own with gliding layups. Other times for kick-out passes to open shooters to take advantage of collapsing defenses. On some occasions, the player on the receiving end has been Kyrie Irving.

Flagg's chemistry is already blossoming with Kyrie. If Flagg is on the ball, Irving provides him an elite catch-and-shoot guy from 3 to target. Or Kyrie can set an inverted screen for him, something we saw Kyrie do for years to great effect alongside LeBron James.

But Irving can also run the show while Flagg moves around off-ball. Flagg has the ability to operate as both a play creator and a play finisher. As great as he's been on-ball this year, he can also set screens, cut with purpose, spot up, and crash the glass. So it's not a huge surprise that he's vibing with Kyrie, a similarly selfless player who can score or playmake. That willingness to give the ball up is part of the reason the Flagg leap is happening this year too.

I've scouted Flagg since he was in high school. The fact he is already this good at just 19 is kind of shocking, to be honest. He wasn't supposed to be doing these things this soon. But it follows a trend. He got way better after turning 18 years old at Duke. Then over the final 26 games of his rookie season, he averaged 25 points, 7 rebounds, and 5 assists. His surge was highlighted by a 51-point performance that helped him win Rookie of the Year. And so far this year, he's averaging 27/6/7 on elite scoring efficiency, an even better assist-to-turnover ratio and more impactful defense.

What's this production gonna look like by the end of the season when so far he's clearly been one of the league's five-to-10 best players? People will say I am being reactionary, but again: What we're seeing from Flagg right now follows a natural trajectory. It's not like this is me saying Ty Jerome is a top-10 guy because of some out-of-nowhere unstoppable performances. Flagg has been on this path for multiple years now.

So whether you want to say he's a top-five guy, a top-10 guy, or not even top 20 yet because you want to give him more time, what's happening is undeniable. Cooper Flagg has arrived.

→ Baca artikel asli

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

#2981. Lumentum CEO touts 'enormous' opportunity in massive AI connectivity deal with Marvell

Marvell Technology's deal for optical components maker Lumentum will create an "enormous" opportunity to address growing connectivity demands from the artificial intelligence infrastructure buildout, Lumentum CEO Michael Hurlston told CNBC on Monday.

"You need scale-up technology that needs semiconductors and optical working seamlessly together," Marvell CEO Matt Murphy told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." "We are going to create a new category of company here."

Since the tie-up is a fixed-ratio deal with no cash, the value will depend on the price of Marvell shares. The company said the transaction has an implied enterprise value of $115 billion for Lumentum, in a release.

Marvell shares slumped 13% in trading Monday, while Lumentum's stock sank nearly 19%. But both stocks have been huge winners in the artificial intelligence boom, with Marvell more than doubling in the past year, prior to Monday's sell-off, and Lumentum quadrupling over that stretch.

Through the acquisition, Marvell said it can combine electrical and optical technologies to meet accelerating and shifting AI connectivity demands across regions and systems. Companies are increasingly relying on optical connections to piece together chips used to run and train large language models.

"We're actually going to create solutions around those that are highly sticky, and the reason that they're sold out is because the demand is exploding, and we don't see that changing anytime soon," Murphy said.

Lumentum shareholders will receive 4.5 Marvell shares for each Lumentum share under the agreement. The deal is expected to close during the first half of 2028.

Marvell's takeover marks one of the most significant bets on AI in 2026 and comes despite recent concerns over the long-term sustainability of infrastructure spending.

This isn't the company's first bet on AI growth. This year, the company closed a multibillion-dollar deal for Celestial AI, a startup focused on optical interconnectivity technology.

Marvell's Murphy also shot down concerns about Nvidia's circular dealmaking, given the leading AI chipmaker's recent multibillion-dollar investments in both companies.

In a bid to further support the data center infrastructure that houses its AI chips, Nvidia has positioned itself as a central cog in a network of related companies, investing in several technology companies that purchase its chips or neoclouds that rent out computing capacity. That's sparked criticism that companies like Nvidia are virtually funding themselves by taking stakes in customers.

"This is a double down on supporting Nvidia, who is the ecosystem leader in this area," Murphy told CNBC. "We're very committed to that partnership, and I think having two of the key suppliers come together, that support that ecosystem, is going to be a big benefit for them."

Marvell has seen its market value more than triple over the last three years, buoyed by unrelenting AI enthusiasm. Lumentum's stock has more than doubled in 2026 alone.

CORRECTION: This story has been updated to reflect that Lumentum CEO Michael Hurlston described the deal as an 'enormous' opportunity. A previous version of this story incorrectly cited the Marvell CEO.

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

Resolution plans, also known as living wills, describe a banking organization's strategy for orderly resolution in the event of material financial distress or failure. #2974. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#2971. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

Anthropic is aiming to start trading the week of Nov. 16, according to a report in the Wall Street Journal. Traders on Kalshi place just a 14% chance that the company begins trading before then.

On the Polymarket platform, traders give a 69% chance that Anthropic will list on a public exchange and open for trading by Nov. 20.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2963. With Joel Embiid set to play, Sixers host Bulls in one-game homestand

Update, 7 p.m.: Joel Embiid has been upgraded from questionable to available. The Sixers will have their regular starting five on the floor tonight vs. the Bulls.

The 6-1 Philadelphia 76ers have a golden opportunity to continue their winning ways on Monday when they host a struggling Chicago Bulls squad.

Getting injuries out of the way, at the time of writing Monday morning Joel Embiid is currently listed as questionable to face the Bulls for plantar fasciitis injury management. This is also the designation that Embiid had when he missed the second leg of the weekend back-to-back on Saturday. Of course, any injury designation for Embiid makes us all nervous, but one could also speculate that the Sixers may just feel they shouldn't need the big fella on Monday and are giving him the extra rest. We will just have to wait and see how things develop.

Dominick Barlow remains out with the low back stress reaction that has delayed his participation to start the season. For the Bulls, Nic Claxton is doubtful with a hamstring strain while Jalen Smith (finger) and Tobe Awaka (ankle) are probable.

Not to be too harsh on Chicago, but the Bulls are struggling mightily to start off this season. At 1-5 and coming into Monday on a four-game losing streak, there's just a lot of things on the wrong trajectory for them. They're currently 29th of 30 in the league in field goal percentage sinking just 41.4% of shots from the floor; dead last in three-point percentage hitting just 28.1% from long range; 25th in offensive rating and 28th in defensive rating; 24th in turnovers per game; and 28th in personal fouls per game.

Yes, the season is still very new, and we all know how much can change over the marathon of an 82-game season… but right now, there are a lot of weaknesses the Sixers can look to exploit. If I'm Nick Nurse, I want to see my squad take advantage of those turnovers and get out in transition with the speed they have shown already, and I want to see them drawing as many fouls as possible. Take the easy points when you can get 'em, and hopefully you're never worrying about playing catch-up the way the Sixers have in a number of games to start their season. Maybe you won't even have to play Tyrese Maxey for 40 minutes just to snag the win.

One bright spot for Chicago thus far has been rookie Caleb Wilson. The 2026 4th-overall pick out of the University of North Carolina has been leading the Bulls to start the season putting up 21.2 points per game with 6.7 rebounds, 2.5 assists, 1.7 steals and 1.5 blocks per game. Thirty-three-year-old veteran Norman Powell has been right there with Wilson chipping in 20.7 points per game. Josh Giddey has been the main playmaker for Chicago averaging 7.5 assists per game, with the next highest mark in that category for the team being Tre Jones averaging just 3.0 assists off the bench.

All in all, with or without Embiid, this is a game the Sixers shouldn't struggle with. If anything, this is a game Philadelphia fans should really hope to see them not have to fight with all their might or stage a comeback in. This is a one-game homestand before the Sixers hit the road for a few games, so it would be in their best interest not to make things harder than they need to be.

Whether they do that, though, is always up in the air.

The Sixers and Bulls tip off in South Philadelphia at 7:30 p.m. ET.

When: Monday, November 2, 7:30 p.m. ETWhere: Xfinity Mobile Arena, Philadelphia, PAWatch: NBC Sports PhiladelphiaRadio: 97.5 The FanaticFollow: @LibertyBallers

#2961. Judge tosses DOJ case against ex-FBI officials accused of lying about Hunter Biden probe

A federal judge on Monday blasted the Department of Justice for "weaponization" as he dismissed its case accusing two former FBI officials of lying to Congress about investigations into Hunter Biden, multiple news outlets reported.

Magistrate Judge Zia Faruqui, in a hearing in U.S. District Court in Washington, reportedly tore into the DOJ over its "slapdash job" prosecuting ex-officials Nikki Floris and Bradley Benavides, saying he was "deeply troubled by what has happened here."

Faruqui said the government lacked probable cause and failed to present evidence that the officials' statements before the House Judiciary Committee in 2023 were knowingly false, MS NOW reported.

He also accused the DOJ of wrongly rushing the case ahead of the midterm election. The situation "cannot be described as anything other than weaponization," he said, according to MS NOW.

The judge even apologized to the government's lawyers, calling it "incredibly unfair" that they were assigned to defend the flawed case.

"This does not feel like an investigation," Faruqui reportedly added. "It feels like a broken one-wish willow obsession."

The DOJ, in response to an email sent to its press office, said it "disagrees" with the decision.

"We are evaluating all options but cannot comment further on an ongoing criminal investigation," the agency said in response to CNBC. "The Department's actions will continue to be guided by the facts, the evidence, and the law – not politics."

Attorneys for Floris and Benavides praised the judge's decision outside the courthouse later Monday morning.

"This case should never have been brought. The government had no case, as the judge just laid out," a lawyer for Floris said, slamming the prosecution as a "spaghetti-at-the-wall, Hunter-Biden-conspiracy fever dream."

A lawyer for Benavides said they hope the DOJ will not try to keep the case alive by seeking an indictment before a grand jury "where they get to be behind closed doors and get to shape the narrative, and we are unable to present our side."

The DOJ did not take that step when they unveiled the allegations against Floris and Benavides on Oct. 23.

Rather, despite the alleged misconduct taking place years earlier, prosecutors acquired a criminal complaint, which only requires a judge's signature. Criminal complaints are often used to obtain arrest warrant in situations where prosecutors need to move quickly.

Faruqui had flagged that circumstance as unusual, and last week refused to sign off on a superseding complaint that added an obstruction charge against the two ex-officials.

"I'm concerned with your view as to what the law is," the judge told the prosecutors in a hearing last Tuesday. "Why today, on this one case, have you changed the entire history of how you charge cases?"

#2957. Warriors redefining ‘Strength In Numbers' mantra to turn around terrible start

Warriors redefining 'Strength In Numbers' mantra to turn around terrible start originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO – Steve Kerr coined the phrase as a motivating tool. There was no way Kerr could have known the words "Strength In Numbers" would turn to a slogan forever attached to the Warriors, and still be used to describe his teams more than a decade later.

Like taking a page out of his former coach Phil Jackson's book, Kerr came up with "Strength In Numbers" before meeting his players for the first time as a way for them to play as a team. The phrase fit right into Kerr moving Andre Iguodala to the bench after starting all his previous 758 career regular-season games. Conveniently, Kerr's three words also fit right into the state of the roster for the 2014-15 Warriors.

Steph Curry was in his age-26 season and about to set a new pecking order in the league as MVP. Klay Thompson was 24, and so was Draymond Green. Harrison Barnes was just 22, and Andrew Bogut was the elder of the starting five at 30 years old. Iguodala was 31, and Leandro Barbosa was the oldest on the team at 32.

That's seven players right there, and a 29-year-old Shaun Livingston hasn't been mentioned yet. Neither has a 31-year-old David Lee, or a 27-year-old Marreese Speights. Festus Ezeli was 25 and back from missing the previous season to reconstructive knee surgery.

Numbers were literally a strength of those Warriors. They could go 11 deep and sometimes 12. The Warriors this season didn't even have 11 players from their 15-man roster available for the season opener in Los Angeles.

Injuries to Jimmy Butler and Moses Moody last season already set them back for this season. Then came Kristaps Porziņģis' mystery illness that was announced the morning of media day, leading to an ongoing absence. Numbers didn't look like a strength of the Warriors going into the season, but a weakness already for reasons out of their hands.

Somehow, though, the Warriors are redefining "Strength In Numbers" to turn around a terrible start.

"Well, I think it's obvious that we have a lot of depth," Kerr said Sunday after the Warriors' short-handed win over the LA Clippers. "To be able to play a game like that without Steph and Dray, obviously Jimmy, Moses – got a lot of injuries, and to be able to play a game like that shows that we got a lot of talented players."

For the second game this season, the Warriors were without Curry and Green, on top of already being down Butler, Moody and Porziņģis. And for the first time, they won with the Curry-Green duo watching from the bench.

Signs of depth being a sneaky strength started showing in training camp, the preseason and even when the Warriors lost their first three games. Rotations were able to be formed quicker than usual and the emergence of rookie Yaxel Lendeborg and the growth of others have made it easier for players to fit into proper roles.

De'Anthony Melton describes the Warriors' second unit as "fast and disruptive" with the bench taking "a lot of pride" in proving naysayers wrong and providing help to an already undermanned Warriors team.

"Obviously, everybody sees what we see too," Melton said. "But early on, I've talked about the depth we have, and I think we have a lot of guys that can contribute in various different ways. Some nights you're going to see some guys do more, some guys do whatever it is. We're going to do it as a committee, and we keep tracking along."

In the Warriors' road loss to the Phoenix Suns on Oct. 24, their third straight loss and the second night of a back-to-back without Curry, Green and others, Melton scored 25 points off the bench. His former high school teammate Brandon Williams dropped 20 in his first start of the season. The two have brought their chemistry to the Warriors and it shows in the stats.

Melton and Williams have played 37 minutes together and have a 17.5 net rating with a 117.5 offensive rating and a 100.0 defensive rating.

While Lendeborg's 37 points against the Clippers was the story Sunday night, so was Melton scoring an efficient 21 points in the starting lineup, Williams scoring 17 off the bench shooting at a highly efficient clip and Brandin Podziemski giving them 17 points, eight rebounds and eight assists as Melton's starting backcourt mate. That's strength in numbers on a night where numbers aren't supposed to be a strength.

Lendeborg in two games without Curry has scored 63 points for an average of 31.5 points on 57.9-percent shooting (22 of 38) and a 54.5 3-point percentage (12 of 22).

The Warriors need Melton and Williams to be reliable in games Curry plays. That becomes even more paramount when Curry is in street clothes. Melton and Williams in the two games Curry has missed to rest have scored a combined 83 points on 60.1-percent shooting (26 of 43).

"I tried to tell everybody, like, we don't have a player like him," Melton says of Williams. "He can just get downhill and his shiftiness and his flair and stuff like that, he needs that just to get the crowd involved and just to get the excitement of everybody. We all love it, and we're all fascinated with the way he can finish. We've seen it as opponents, too."

When told in the locker room of Melton's comments at the podium, Williams turns to his right and says "Who said that? Melt said that? My dog."

The oohs and aahs rang throughout the Chase Center crowd as Williams raced past his defender and flew to the rack for crafty layups to beat the Clippers. A season removed from giving the Warriors 26 points in San Francisco on Christmas, Williams already has become a crowd favorite of Dub Nation.

"Oh man, I love it," Williams told NBC Sports Bay Area. "I love it. I'm glad. I'm glad everybody's starting to recognize it."

If Williams could, he would tell the fans to get on their feet every time he has the ball, feeding off the vibes from thousands of fans.

"I'm a rhythm player, so like every time the crowd gets going, I feed off of it for sure. And then I bring it out to my team as well," he said. "So once the crowd gets into it, I feel like that's our sixth man."

The Warriors' win against the Clippers came one game after one in which Curry scored 31 points and made Luka Dončić touch earth. But it also was a game where the Warriors' bench outscored the Lakers' bench 50 to seven. Golden State through Sunday night now ranks sixth in bench points per game (43.0).

Georges Niang in the win vs. the Lakers was a perfect 3 of 3 from 3-point range and now is shooting 47 percent beyond the arc during the Warriors' four-game win streak.

Niang showed some fire against the Lakers, too, going nose to nose with Colin Sexton.

"I'm not out there to make anyone feel like it's a walk in the park," Niang told reporters in the locker room after.

Kerr had to use five different starting lineups in the Warriors' first five games. Consistency couldn't be part of the Warriors' equation, but connectivity still was.

The Warriors coach said Sunday night's victory was won in the summer, praising young guys like Lendeborg, Will Richard, Charles Bassey and Graham Ike for being around throughout the summer and buying into who the Warriors want to be, as well as Melton for rehabbing in the facility and digesting offensive tweaks and Frank Vogel's defense. The oldest team in the league is getting energy from their young role players and stability from their veterans to bring balance to the bench.

Williams is new to the Warriors' slogan but always believed this is who they would be, as seen in taking both games against the Southern California foes.

"I thought that would be our strength, for sure," Williams said. "Obviously with 30 [Curry] and Dray out, everybody's gonna have to step up, all of us all around. So BP did. Obviously, we saw what Yax did. Mel as well. G came in with a good spark, as well as Will. I think everybody kind of contributed in their own ways, and I think everybody's starting to see now that's how deep we are as a team."

With numbers not on their side, these Warriors are finding improbable ways to bring "Strength In Numbers" alive.

Download and follow the Dubs Talk Podcast

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2953. Breaking down Karl-Anthony Towns strong start to Knicks season — and what he can still improve

If there are any questions about the Knicks' commitment post-championship, or their distraction level amid contract negotiations, none of them can be pointed at Karl-Anthony Towns.

Coming off a legendary postseason run that helped secure his place in New York immortality, Towns has been his team's most locked-in player of the new season, averaging 21.8 points, 13.5 rebounds, and 3.2 assists per game on 65.5 percent shooting from two and 41.4 percent from three.

This, despite being the key hang-up in the Knicks' extension talks and coming off a busy summer of celebration. Let's dive into Towns' hellacious start and what it means to the team.

One difference from Towns this season is his frame. He came into the season considerably thinner than in years past, though he plans to bulk up as the year progresses.

This seems to only have benefited Towns' game. His mobility looks better and there haven't been major sacrifices on the defensive end or boards.

His inside finishing has been particularly efficient, while that was a weakness early last season.

Otherwise, it's been a lot of the Towns we know and love, specifically out of the hub offense that turned around last season's playoffs. The Knicks are leaning into it more now having the blueprint, and Towns looks as comfortable as ever with head coach Mike Brown's schemes.

It hasn't shown up on the stat sheet as many would have liked — Towns' turnovers have spiked while his assists are flat. But much of this is due to how teams have adjusted against this look.

They've been more aggressive in switching the flex screen and helping off the weak side, forcing the Knicks into the third and fourth option on these plays. There's also some sloppiness on Towns' part as he gets reacquainted and sees what passes he can force or not.

But these bumps are to be expected, the net is positive, and even some early hiccups are ultimately helpful toward the ultimate goal. The fact that the Knicks haven't made shots like normal is also skewing the numbers.

Inside or out of the hub offense, Towns has looked the part. His 41.4 percent three-point shooting mark could come on higher volume, but he's picked his spots well with deep catch-and-shoots off trails and even some self-creation in the clutch.

Towns has seen the most action in fourth quarters, scoring 7.3 of his points per game in that period. Unfortunately the Knicks haven't turned to him in the closing moments enough, as he's taken two shots in the eight minutes of crunch time in games New York's played thus far.

That the Knicks have lost most of these tight contests to fall to 2-4 record could be in part due to this. Until others pick up their slack, this may need to be more of a Towns-centric team.

Fans know first hand how these Towns reps will ultimately pay off in the playoffs, when the Knicks will need multiple answers and know they can depend on their big man. But they'll also play a role in the coming contract negotiations.

Towns can either hit free agency or re-sign long-term with the Knicks, with both parties seemingly favoring the latter -- just at different prices. He will have much more leverage walking in with continued dominance like this.

Towns can point to New York's falling point differential when he steps off the court, or the mixed results they're getting from their alternative second options on the wing. He can point to being second in the league in rebounds per game and first to the rim to defend it.

Hopefully this ends in an inked extension and repeat run. In the meantime, Towns is aiming to put together one of the best seasons of his career, and the early returns are promising.

#2951. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2948. Defense emerges as Sixers’ early calling card

First, a disclaimer: It's early. Ty Jerome, briefly a Sixer on draft night seven years ago, is among the NBA's leading scorers. Quentin Grimes and Matisse Thybulle are playing regular minutes for the Lakers. Another old friend, De'Anthony Melton, is shooting better from the arc than his Golden State teammate, Stephen Curry.

The point is, nobody knows nothin'. Not yet.

That said, it looks like the Sixers can really defend. Time and again they have shown a willingness to dig in on that end of the floor, often at the most critical moments. The want-to is there. The effort is there. The connection is there.

Through seven games, six of them victories, they are third in the league in defensive rating (105.4) and fifth in halfcourt rating (105.0).

As LeBron James said after Friday's victory over the Knicks, "We definitely hang our hats on the defensive end so far this season."

The caveats are many. Joel Embiid's health. LeBron's age. The lack of an adequate backup center (again). The many newcomers, besides James. And, as mentioned, the fact that we're not quite two weeks into the season; seven games in October don't carry nearly the same weight as seven games in May or June.

But it's clear the foundation is in place. The Sixers have four standout defenders in VJ Edgecombe and newcomers Jaylen Brown, Dean Wade and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope. Embiid, before his current foot problems, seemed to be approaching the rim-protector form he showed a few years back, and James, two months shy of his 42nd birthday, appears to have recommitted himself on the defensive end, after showing some slippage in recent seasons.

Even guys who are not known for their defense, like Tyrese Maxey and Anfernee Simons, are putting their best feet forward – Maxey because he's Maxey, Simons because he knows he will be out there in big moments, given what an offensive weapon he has shown himself to be.

"We are just willing to guard, and that's important," Edgecombe said. "We're connected. In training camp, we obviously worked on our standard, our principles. And I say we just kind of execute from there."

"We know," he added, "if we get some stops, we get out and have fun from there."

In James' mind, it is easier for a team featuring so many new pieces to coalesce on the defensive end.

"The offense usually comes after that," he said, "because guys are just kind of figuring it out and being on the floor with one another."

And indeed, the Sixers are just 23rd in offensive rating (109.3), despite Maxey's sizzling start; he's averaging just under 30 points a game, on .486/.352/.830 shooting splits.

He generated 38 in Saturday's 109-104 victory in Charlotte, a game that was in doubt until the Sixers reeled off 13 straight points to erase a seven-point deficit late in the fourth quarter. As had been the case in the 98-93 home victory over the Knicks the night before, Edgecombe provided the dagger three-pointer in the final minute. But when you zoom out you again see that ultimately D was the difference: The Sixers limited Charlotte to 34.8 percent shooting while outscoring them 32-21 in the fourth. And they did that, even though Embiid and James were held out of the game.

On Friday, they led the defending champs most of the night – at a couple points by as many as 14 – despite Brown's absence with a sore knee. And again there were moments of defensive brilliance, notably by Edgecombe and KCP, who tag-teamed Knicks star Jalen Brunson, and Wade, who jousted with OG Anunoby.

Wade started but did not squeeze off a shot while going scoreless in nearly 27 minutes of action. Still, he made a difference, and he was particularly stout during a second-quarter stretch that saw him register two of his three steals and contest an Anunoby miss. His efforts hardly went unnoticed.

"The stat sheet doesn't always determine winning plays and the things he was doing tonight defensively, and the things that he was doing offensively, giving us extra shots and things of that nature," James said, adding that the 6-foot-9 Wade is the "perfect player for every team."

"And," James said, "he's always going to make the winning plays."

Speaking of which, let us consider the work of Edgecombe, who at age 21 seems to make such plays nightly. He and Caldwell-Pope limited Brunson to 24 points on 8-for-22 shooting – no easy task, considering his herky-jerky old-man game. As Edgecombe put it, Brunson "uses the fundamentals of the game well" while repeatedly knocking down difficult shots.

"I just try my best to just be there, just make sure I'm contesting every shot," Edgecombe said.

The Knicks, as is their wont, kept coming, and finally took the lead at 90-89 on Brunson's right-corner three-pointer over Edgecombe with 1:32 remaining.

"There are really great players throughout this league," Edgecombe said. "They're going to make tough shots, but I said I can't hang my head down and get mad because he made a tough shot. He shot it over me. I kind of did my job, but like I say, it's going to happen."

James put the Sixers back in front with a foul-line fadeaway, and on the next Knicks possession Karl-Anthony Towns screened for Brunson as he advanced the ball over midcourt. Embiid hedged and Edgecombe recovered, tracking Brunson as he kept his dribble and moved to his right.

The moment of truth came when the Knicks star drove down the right side of the lane. There defenders are often easy pickings for his array of step-throughs, up-and-unders and fadeaways, but Edgecombe met the moment, forcing Brunson into a miss from eight feet.

Then, at the other end, Edgecombe nailed his enormous three-ball. That proved decisive, despite the fact that he was whistled for a foul on Brunson (apparently with his pinky toe) next time down – a call upheld after replay review.

Brunson made the first free throw, then had a ball-don't-lie miss, and the Sixers closed it out.

Again, miles to go. The Sixers are hardly fully formed. But their defense – "the physicality, the communication, the keeping the ball in front, the really not quitting on plays," as Nick Nurse said – is at present their calling card. If that proves to be sustainable, it's not hard to believe that they will be in this for the long haul – that they will be playing far more meaningful games next spring than they are right now.

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2946. Feeling isolated? Half of in-office workers describe their desk jobs as 'remote,' NY Fed survey finds

Many workers across the U.S. are now back in-office a majority of the time, and they are feeling more isolated than ever.

A recent survey from researchers at the New York Fed finds that roughly half of workers based in-office don't describe a daily experience on the job that matches the goal many corporate employers stated in upping in-person mandates.

Whether three-, four- or five-day mandates, companies have cited the importance of creative collaboration for business productivity and growth, as well as mentorship opportunities. Yet workers surveyed by the NY Fed describe endless Zoom calls and Slacks, and little actual face time.

"Days in the office often lack human contact," the researchers write. Specifically, 48.8% of survey respondents said they had no in-person meetings, either with teammates or non-teammates, on the days they were most recently in the office.

There are a variety of reasons for the disconnect between in-office work mandates and actual experience.

Roughly one in four workers are now fully remote, according to U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics data cited by the NY Fed, but they say that alone likely "understates the explosion in the experience of remoteness from colleagues."

With teams being spread across more offices — an effect they say has become more pronounced in the post-Covid organization structure — many workers "no-showing" on mandates, and employers not enforcing mandates strictly, the result for in-office workers, according to the researcher, is that "they commute in just to take Zoom calls and answer Slack messages from their cubicles."

The NY Fed paper, by NY Fed research economist Natalia Emanuel and University of Virginia economics professor Emma Harrington, concludes that "a staggering share of the workforce experiences this form of in-office remote work."

The survey was conducted in September among a final sample of 1,835 college-educated workers. The researchers chose September, they say, to generate a "conservative estimate," since many companies push in-office attendance after summer ends, and the period between Labor Day and Thanksgiving has less remote work than holiday periods.

The survey found that on in-office days, 70.6% of workers had at least one teammate who was not in the office; while half had no in-person meetings with all participants present, and over a third had neither in-person or hybrid meetings.

"Amazingly, 37% of respondents reported that they had at least one meeting that was virtual, even though all the participants were in the same office that day. In short, many workers' commutes are simply yielding a new background for their Zoom calls," they write.

They cite the example of Zoom CEO Eric Yuan, who said in 2023, "On Zoom, it's really hard. We cannot have a great conversation. We cannot debate each other well."

But the researchers conclude, "if the debates in the office are also happening over Zoom, the benefits of the office may not be so large."

The authors say that separate data on a Fortune 500 company employee base to which they had personnel data and badge swipe data shows that nearly half of corporate employees have at least one teammate who is out of the office on their in-office days. They say additional data from the BLS's American Time Use Survey similarly show that "Americans now spend more time physically alone on days they go into the office than they did before the pandemic."

Other recent polling data from Gallup found worker sentiment to be at a 15-year low.

The NY Fed authors caution that their research does not capture relationships with non-teammates, or worker's in-person interactions with clients, often outside the office. They also stress that workers who come to the office often cite seeing colleagues as a major draw. "But since the rise of remote work, the office increasingly fails to deliver this benefit," they write.

They contend that the research has practical implications for both employers and employees.

For one, the experience of remote work is much more widespread than understood, which could have many repercussions, including mental health issues.

Second, organizational structure post-pandemic may not be optimal for making in-office mandates work. It may, in fact, just lead to more pushback.

But the authors do not conclude employers should give up on the mandates. To the contrary, they write, "ensuring adherence to the policy helps deliver the face-to-face contact that workers crave."

#2944. Ravens Report Card, Week 8: Another injury-riddled offensive implosion

The Baltimore Ravens suffered a third consecutive loss in Week 8 and their second straight game losing by 20+ points, falling to the Buffalo Bills by a score of 36-10. Here are report card grades for the team's position groups from yesterday's defeat.

Lamar Jackson's injury on his first play of the game took the wind out of the team's sails immediately. Tyler Huntley's short stint in the opening quarter, prior to exiting with a foot injury, was eventful for the wrong reasons. Huntley's four pass attempts resulted in zero yards and he lost a fumble in the Ravens' own territory. Joe Fagnano made his NFL debut and played most of the game. After completing back-to-back throws immediately upon entering the game, Fagnano struggled. He completed 10-of-21 passes for only 93 yards with an interception and lost fumble. Fagnano also was banged up but finished the game. All three quarterbacks were under duress during their time on the field.

Derrick Henry broke his scoring drought and should have another touchdown, but it was called back due to a holding penalty. While he ran more efficiently than recent weeks (4.8 yards per carry), Henry only had 11 carries and the Ravens didn't make a concentrated effort to ride him after Jacksonw went down. He also continues to be inconsistent as a pass-blocker. Justice Hill had seven touches for 35 total yards and Rasheen Ali was given a pair of carries as well.

The Ravens' wideouts were victim to the team's quarterback and pass protection struggles. However, it was still disappointing that Zay Flowers failed to catch any passes on five targets against a leaky Bills' pass defense that was missing multiple players. Rashod Bateman and Ja'Kobi Lane each caught one pass for a first-down conversion, but that was the extent of production from the wide receiver room.

It was another quiet day for the Ravens' tight end group. Like the wide receivers, they too have to be graded on a curve given the in-flux state of the quarterback position and pass blocking issues. Nevertheless, Mark Andrews continues to be make a minimal impact (three catches, 14 yards) and Matt Hibner was blanked from the stat sheet for a second straight game. Josh Cuevas caught one reception for 15 yards.

The Ravens' offense line struggled mightily in pass protection against the Bills' front seven. The Ravens surrendered 12 quarterback hits and four sacks, with all three quarterbacks facing pressure consistently when on the field. Roger Rosengarten was beaten multiple times by Gregory Rosseau, Cam Jurgens appeared to miss linebacker blitz pickups, and both starting guards also allowed multiple hurries. They did better in run blocking this week, but it meant little in the grand scheme of the game.

The Ravens did an okay job bottling up the Bills' run defense for most of the game. While they wound up with 146 rushing yards, James Cook was limited to 3.8 yards per carry with a longest run of 16 yards and Josh Allen ran seven times for 30 yards. Broderick Washington made a huge defensive play by coming up with an interception off a dropped pass, which led to the Ravens' only touchdown of the game. Nnamdi Madubuike was the only defensive lineman to record a sack or quarterback hit.

Trey Hendrickson and Mike Green each had a quarterback hit, with the latter also recording a half-sack in tandem with Madubuike. Hendrickson and Zion Young each made some plays against the run as well. Overall, though, the Ravens didn't get a ton of disruption on Allen and failed to bring him down a few times.

Roquan Smith played much more inspired than he has in recent weeks, totaling 11 tackles with two pass breakups and a forced fumble. He had a huge first-half showing, but his impact tailed off as the game progressed late, along with the rest of the defense. Trenton Simpson had a quarterback hit with four tackles, and Teddye Buchanan had his most impactful performance so far this year with six tackles. Both Buchanan and Simpson were on the wrong end of open-field juke moves, but otherwise appeared to make no mistakes.

The Ravens' cornerbacks were much better than last week, which was anticipated given they weren't matching up with Ja'Marr Chase and Tee Higgins again. Marlon Humphrey was beaten by D.J. Moore for a long 49-yard reception, but he was in position to make a play. He made a pass breakup on the very next play in the red zone and also nearly had an interception. Chidobe Awuzie had a nice pass defended as well in coverage of Kincaid and backup T.J. Tampa made an impact and his return to action. Tampa recovered a fumble and broke up a two-point conversion attempt. For the Ravens to be most successful in the backend, they might need to play Humphrey in the slot with Awuzie and Tampa taking outside corner reps opposite Wiggins. Keyon Martin, and Chandler Rivers when he was playing, often appear too undersized and overmatched.

Kyle Hamilton rebounded from an uncharacteristic performance last week with a better showing against the Bills. He finished with seven tackles, including another big tackle for loss on a conversion run attempt in the red zone. Malaki Starks made a few tackles against the run including one tackle for loss of his own. Starks was beaten by Dalton Kincaid for a late touchdown pass, and Kincaid also had a first-down catch with Hamilton in coverage early in the game. K'Von Wallace had six tackles, playing an elevated role again with Jaylinn Hawkins absent.

Rasheen Ali had a great kick return for 59 yards, where he appeared bottled up but then broke free for a huge gain. LaJohntay Wester had another quiet outing as a punt returner with just one return for two yards. Ryan Eckley also made some nice punts again, including a long 62-yarder. The biggest negative on special teams was another missed field goal from Tyler Loop, which came from just 34 yards out. In the grand scheme of a 26-point loss, one missed field goal is not a huge deal, but at the time it could have cut the Ravens' deficit to three points.

#2943. Yaxel Lendeborg drops 37 points in win over Clippers, most for a Warriors rookie since Stephen Curry

We've come a long way since Golden State Warriors rookie Yaxel Lendeborg called out Los Angeles Lakers star Luka Dončić on opening night. Since then, the Warriors rookie has generally backed up his trash talk, and has already turned in games with 26, 31 and 26 points.

But he saved his best performance — so far — for Sunday's contest against the Los Angeles Clippers. With Stephen Curry and Draymond Green sitting out, Lendeborg went off for a career-high 37 points in a 124-114 win over the Clippers.

That's the most points scored by a Warriors rookie since … you guessed it … Curry back in the 2009-10 NBA season. During his rookie season, Curry exceeded 37 points just one time, when he dropped 42 points in the team's final game of the season. He did, however, cross the 30-point threshold eight total times.

After Sunday's win, Warriors coach Steve Kerr spoke extremely highly of Lendeborg, saying the team did not "expect" this, especially since Lendeborg is still learning the game, per ESPN.

"He's playing this way and he really doesn't know the NBA game yet," Kerr said. "The game is still really fast for him, but he's so skilled and so talented that he's making all kinds of plays. He's going to get a lot better."

Kerr later added that Lendeborg's emergence "changes everything" for the Warriors, who discovered the team is capable of winning games without its biggest names in the lineup. In addition to Curry and Green sitting out Sunday's game, the Warriors were also without Jimmy Butler, who is still making his way back from an ACL injury.

Lendeborg's performance should come as quite the surprise. Throughout his entire college career, Lendeborg scored 30 points just once. He's already exceeded that figure twice in seven NBA games. He's done it by hitting shots from beyond the arc, another thing Lendeborg didn't do much until his final year in college.

That long-range prowess was on display Sunday, as Lendeborg went 13 of 19 from the field, including six of 11 from 3-point range. It marked the second time in his NBA career Lendeborg has made six 3-pointers in a game.

While his output was impressive, it may not continue when Curry and Green return Monday. Lendeborg said he forced himself to step up his game with Curry out, per ESPN.

"Whenever Steph's not in the game, it's my opportunity to try to be the leader, be the main guy on the team," Lendeborg said.

But if the rookie can continue to put up big performances, it won't be long before he's a more regular staple of the team's offense, even in games where Curry is active.

#2938. Fantasy Basketball Waiver Wire Week 3: Aday Mara is ready to shine

Injuries plagued the first week of the NBA season, and the hits just kept coming in Week 2.

Anthony Edwards, Ja Morant, Tyrese Haliburton, Jaren Jackson Jr., Chet Holmgren, and Kawhi Leonard will all miss multiple weeks with injuries, opening the door for other players to take on expanded roles.

With injuries to star players piling up, I've put together my top waiver wire adds heading into Week 3.

Waiver wire adds are based on 8-cat scoring, and roster percentages come from Yahoo! leagues. Only players rostered in 50% or less will be included.

Others to consider: Derrick Jones Jr. (26%), Isaiah Jackson (24%), Cam Spencer (12%), Nolan Traore (9%), Ziaire Williams (9%), Keon Ellis (7%), Marcus Smart (3%), Kobe Sanders (2%)

Kawhi Leonard is injured … again. That means Walter - who has started each of the last four games with Leonard sidelined - should be a fixture in the first unit indefinitely.

Since joining the starting lineup, Walter has averaged 14 points, 4.0 rebounds, 1.8 assists, 1.3 steals, 0.5 swats and 3.5 triples while shooting 50% from the floor and 80% from the charity stripe.

Walter is a three-and-D specialist who's provided Top-75 value over his last four games. This is likely the last week his roster percentage will be 40% or less until Leonard returns.

Phoenix doesn't have a true PG in the starting five, but they have a Sixth Man who can run the show. Gillespie's 26.5 minutes per game are fifth most on the team and lead the Suns' bench.

In his last game out, Gillespie posted a 16/3/10 line with four steals, a block and four triples across 33 minutes. He's got big upside as a specialist in assists, steals and three-pointers. Even if he continues to come off the bench, Gillespie should see more than enough run to be a viable option in standard leagues.

The hits kept coming for OKC over the last week. After losing Jalen Williams to a gruesome leg injury, the Thunder got news that Chet Holmgren will be sidelined for 8-10 weeks after getting treatment on a patellar tendon tear.

Cason Wallace (shoulder) and Alex Caruso (ankle) are out indefinitely, and suddenly the Thunder's rotation is razor thin. Mara saw a season-high 28 minutes in his last game out, posting a 7/9/3 line with four blocked shots.

The rookie has the upside to average a double-double with multiple swats, and he's still available in 65% of Yahoo! leagues. Scoop him up while you still can.

In that span, the Arizona product has averaged 15.5 points, 4.0 rebounds, 4.8 assists, 1.3 steals and 1.3 triples while shooting 59.4% from the floor and 86% from the charity stripe. Miami plays four games in Week 3, which is just another reason to add Larsson.

"Airplane Mode" has taken off for LA this season, averaging career highs in points (12), rebounds (4.4), assists (1.6), triples (2.4), steals (1.4) and minutes (29). He's also swatting 0.6 shots per tilt.

Jones Jr. has started every game for the Clippers, and given the roster's lack of depth, he should see minutes in the high 20's to low 30's for the foreseeable future. He's ranked just outside the fifth round in per-game fantasy value.

DJJ injured his knee in Sunday's loss to the Warriors, and if he's forced to miss time, Kobe Sanders (2%) would be worth streaming until he returns.

Jusuf Nurkic is still out, and Jaren Jackson Jr. will be sidelined for 6-8 weeks. That leaves Utah with a gaping hole in its frontcourt and Hayes with a sizable opportunity.

Over his last two games (both starts), Hayes averaged 4.5 points, 9.0 rebounds, 3.0 assists, 1.5 steals and 1.5 blocked shots across 28.5 minutes. He won't score much, but Hayes is a solid source of rebounds and defensive stats.

Hunter started the season out slowly, averaging just 5.7 points, 5.0 rebounds and 1.3 triples on 37.5% shooting across 25 minutes over his first three games.

Over his last three, Hunter has averaged 22.3 points, 4.7 boards, 3.7 triples and a "stock" across 34 minutes per game. Hunter has shot 52.3% in that span, and he ranks 65th in per-game fantasy value.

Hunter's increased playing time and production is due in part to the absence of Keegan Murray (ankle), who will be out for at least the next week. Hunter is still a worthy option in category leagues, but he's better in points formats.

Don't look now, but Simons is a second-round option over the last week. Across his last three games, the sharpshooter has averaged 20 points, 1.3 steals and 5.3 triples across 27.7 minutes.

Simons is best deployed as a points-league guy, but fantasy managers can't ignore a player splashing triples and piling up points and swipes at this level.

Ja Morant is out for at least four weeks with a hamstring injury, and in Morant's first missed game, Henderson got the start over Jrue Holiday.

Henderson posted a 14/3/1 line with three steals and a triple. The production was solid, but Henderson's playing time was most notable, as he tied Toumani Camara for team lead in minutes at 31.

Henderson appears to have the inside track to holding down Morant's vacated backcourt spot in the starting five, making him a strong add off of the waiver wire.

After spending the last five and a half seasons with Orlando, Wagner is off to a great start in his first with the Nets.

The big man averaged 15.0 points, 6.0 rebounds, 1.3 assists, 1.3 triples and 1.3 "stocks" across 22 minutes in his last three games, ranking just inside the fifth round in fantasy value. Wagner won't post Top-60 value all season, but he can be a useful source of points and boards with 3-and-D upside.

Until Paolo Banchero and Jalen Suggs are back, da Silva should continue seeing meaningful run, making him a solid add in 12-team formats.

#2930. Uncharacteristic sloppiness costing Knicks in early-season losses

They say defense wins championships, and it definitely is true that you need to be able to defend, but defense does not only win championships.

The Knicks, through six games and the end of October, are a perfectly normal 10th in defensive rating. It's slightly down from last year, but it's more in line with everything they've done as a team for the last few years.

The reason they're 2-4 is not their defense; it's their offense. In all four of their losses, they've scored 103 or fewer points.

They're 22nd in offensive rating. They're shooting abhorrently bad at the rim. Jalen Brunson, a floater maestro, has been extremely bad at it. They've been uncharacteristically bad at the free-throw line. While they've actually been great at making wide-open threes, they go through puzzling cold stretches from some of their best shooters.

All of that stuff isn't sticky in small samples. A team this talented will not continue to shoot sub-60% at the rim. The craftiest point guard in basketball didn't lose his touch (although the wrist surgery may cause this slump to last longer than we want), the good shooters will eventually start making their shots.

There is one thing, though, that is a matter of execution, not hoping for luck to even out.

The Knicks, as of now, have not played structurally sound offensive basketball. They haven't deserved much better outcomes than what they're currently getting on a possession-by-possession basis.

In the 2025-26 season, the Knicks averaged 2.01 assists per turnover (27.4 assists, 13.6 turnovers), which ranked eighth in basketball

So far this season? Just 1.69 assists per turnover (26.2 assists, 15.5 turnovers), down to 15th.

While it's just one stat, the assist-to-turnover ratio is something that separates the wheat from the chaff. Of the top seven offenses last season, six of them had an AST/TO ratio above two, something only eight teams did. It's very rare for teams to have a good offense and a low AST/TO ratio, the Knicks are not one of those teams that can do that.

Looking player-by-player, there isn't any issue with the team's primary playmakers. Instead, it's their secondary playmakers.

Jalen Brunson, Tyler Kolek, and Jose Alvarado have combined for 61 assists to 19 turnovers.

Everyone else? 96 assists to 68 turnovers. While you might expect some drop-off, this is a bit bizarre.

To spell it out in AST/TO and compare it to last year:

2025-26:Brunson/Kolek/Alvarado: 2.99Everyone else: 1.85

2026-27:Brunson/Kolek/Alvarado: 3.21Everyone else: 1.41

That "everyone else" drop-off is concentrated in the rest of the starters, mainly Josh Hart, OG Anunoby, and Karl-Anthony Towns.

Hart was arguably the team's most efficient playmaker with a staggering 2.48 AST/TO ratio. So far this season, he's at 20 assists to 11 turnovers. This is a guy who averaged nearly five assists last year, second on the team, and it's down to 3.3 so far.

What's up with that? He seems to be touching the ball a lot less offensively. Obviously, Brunson gets the lion's share of on-ball reps, but the evolution to a KAT-centric offense and the desire to let Anunoby build on last year's on-ball success in the postseason have turned him into someone he isn't: a catch-and-shoot perimeter threat. That's for Deuce McBride, not for Hart.

For Anunoby, his numbers are similar in the playmaking aspect, but he's on ball significantly more than he was last year. He's averaging more touches; he's averaging more dribbles per touch, and there are more plays being run for him.

Visually, some of these touches and plays just look… bad. They're affecting not only the increased turnovers but also the lower shooting numbers. If things don't level off soon, you might see Mike Brown rein in these touches and go back to having Anunoby as an off-ball player to simplify the offense.

Stunningly, is the biggest issue Towns? The same guy whose playmaking evolved to such a degree in the postseason that it was the adjustment that led to a title?

Karl-Anthony Towns AST/TO:2025-26: 1.212026 postseason: 1.882026-27: 0.76

The Knicks haven't fully leaned into the KAT-centric offense as they did in the postseason, but they've spent a lot more time working through him than they did during the regular season last year. Brunson is spending significantly less time with the ball in his hands, and KAT is the biggest beneficiary.

Really, it seems he's just trying to do too much. There have been multiple plays:

The Knicks have gotten burned to a crisp on plays like these. Live-ball turnovers. Here, Towns is trying to hit an outlet and just throws it into no-man's land. It's high-risk, high-reward, as we saw last postseason, but the risk is rearing its ugly head.

The live ball turnovers aren't just a KAT issue; everyone is culpable.

These are just live-ball, bad-pass turnovers. There are a bunch more where the player just loses the ball, stumbles, or gets stripped in a bad position, which leads to a fast break.

A staple of Knicks basketball in the Brunson era has been being toward the bottom in turnovers. In three of the first four seasons, the Knicks were top-six in least turnovers per game. This season so far? 13th.

Mike Brown brought about more ball movement and less standing around doing nothing. At least so far, it's looking a lot more like standing around and doing nothing.

→ Baca artikel asli
